Codifferential Calculi on Quantum Homogeneous Spaces Thanks: The author acknowledges support by MSCA-DN CaLiForNIA - 101119552, and is grateful for the hospitality offered by the Korteweg de-Vries Institute of Mathematics of the University of Amsterdam.
Julius Benner

Abstract
. We develop the theory of first- and higher-order codifferential calculi over coalgebras C C over fields k k with characteristic char ⁡ ( k ) ≠ 2 \mathrm{char}(k)\neq 2 . For a given first-order codifferential calculus, we introduce its maximal prolongation by means of an explicit construction that associates to it a differential graded coalgebra, satisfying a universal property. For module coalgebras over a Hopf algebra U U , we introduce the notion of an equivariant codifferential calculus. If C C is of the form U ⊗ H k U\otimes_{H}k for a Hopf algebra U U and a right coideal subalgebra H H such that U U is faithfully flat as a left- and right H H -module, we show that equivariant first-order codifferential calculi correspond to certain right coideals T ⊆ ker ( ε : C → k ) T\subseteq\ker(\varepsilon\colon C\rightarrow k) called quantum tangent spaces. If H H is a sub bialgebra and the right C C -coaction on T T is trivial, then the maximal prolongation is described in terms of a quadratic coalgebra. We further relate codifferential calculi to differential calculi and Cartan pairs over the dual algebra C ∗ C^{\ast} , or more generally subalgebras thereof. We explicitly compute codifferential calculi on the coalgebra pre duals of the Podleś sphere and the quantized projective spaces. As an application, we give a new proof that the antiholomorphic Heckenberger–Kolb calculi on quantized projective spaces have classical dimension.



1.
Introduction

Noncommutative geometry studies noncommutative algebras
A
by thinking of them as algebras of functions or observables on noncommutative spaces
X
. A smooth structure on
X
corresponds to a choice of first-order differential calculus on
A
. First-order differential calculi and their higher-order counterparts are structures that have been intensively studied over the past decades. Notably, they are one of the central themes in the recent monograph
[
3
]
. Differential calculi appear in areas as far-reaching as deep learning
[
17
]
and stochastics through the Itô and Stratonovich calculi
[
3
, Section 1.9]
. Perhaps the field that has seen the most fruitful application of differential calculi, and which is at the same time the main motivation for this article, has been that of quantum groups and quantized flag manifolds. Besides the pioneering work of Woronowicz
[
42
]
, one of the main achievements in this area is the construction of direct
q
-deformations of the classical algebraic de Rham complexes on quantized irreducible flag manifolds. These deformations are named Heckenberger–Kolb differential calculi, after the authors of the celebrated article
[
21
]
, and they share many important features with their classical counterpart. To add to the significance of this result, outside the irreducible setting, deformations like this have only recently appeared in the literature for the
A
-series full flag manifolds via a construction using Lusztig’s root vectors
[
33
]
. Differential calculi on quantized flag manifolds have further been used in the construction of Dolbeault–Dirac spectral triples
[
14
,
12
,
41
]
, demonstrating that certain quantized flag manifolds fit into the framework of noncommutative geometry in the sense of Connes
[
11
]
.

If one takes the idea that a noncommutative manifold is a pair consisting of a noncommutative algebra
A
and a first-order differential calculus over
A
seriously, then there should also be the notion of noncommutative vector fields over
A
. Indeed, there is the notion of a Cartan pair introduced by Borowiec
[
7
,
8
,
9
]
which serves as a noncommutative algebraic substitute for vector fields. Unfortunately, Cartan pairs have seen very little attention elsewhere in the literature, and thus only few developments have been made. For instance, it is not known if combining a first-order differential calculus and a Cartan pair gives rise to a noncommutative version of a Cartan calculus (for an account of Cartan calculi for classical manifolds see
[
1
, Chapter 2.4]
).

The passage from a first-order differential calculus to a Cartan pair is a dualization at the level of modules, see for instance the main theorem in
[
7
]
. If one turns to quantum groups, then there is another way of dualizing: Many examples of quantum groups — for instance Drinfeld–Jimbo quantum groups — arise naturally as a pair of dually paired Hopf algebras
(A,U)
. If one takes a first-order differential on
A
, then the corresponding dual structure on
U
is neither a first-order differential calculus nor a Cartan pair, but a
first-order codifferential calculus
.

The definition of a first-order codifferential calculus essentially arises as a naive translation of the axioms of a first-order differential calculus into coalgebra language. More precisely, a first-order codifferential calculus over a coalgebra
C
over a field
k
of characteristic not equal to
2
consists of a
C
-bicomodule
\mathscr{W}_{1}
together with a linear map
\delta\colon\mathscr{W}_{1}\rightarrow C
, meaning that

\Delta\circ\delta=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\delta)\circ{}_{\mathscr{W}_{1}}\Delta+(\delta\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ\Delta_{\mathscr{W}_{1}}

where
{}_{\mathscr{W}_{1}}\Delta
and
\Delta_{\mathscr{W}_{1}}
denote the left- and right coactions of
\mathscr{W}_{1}
respectively, such that the map

(\mathrm{id}\otimes\delta)\circ{}_{\mathscr{W}_{1}}\Delta\colon\mathscr{W}_{1}\rightarrow C\otimes C

is injective (see Definition
3.2
).

Somewhat surprisingly, the first appearance of a first-order codifferential calculus in the literature predates the work of Woronowicz
[
42
]
, namely in
[
13
]
, Doi introduced what is now called the universal first-order codifferential calculus. Other than this work, not many results about codifferential calculi can be found in the literature. The first definition of a first-order codifferential calculus appeared in
[
6
]
, which however omits the injectivity axiom. The only account of a higher-order codifferential calculus appears in
[
27
]
, where — in our terminology — the maximal prolongation of the universal first-order codifferential calculus is constructed. The only more comprehensive study of first-order codifferential calculi, which uses a definition equivalent to the one in this article, was carried out by Borowiec and Mieszkalski in the recent preprint
[
5
]
.

The goal of this article is to expand the theory of codifferential calculi, by proving analogs of several results that are known for differential calculi, and in addition relate codifferential calculi to differential calculi and Cartan pairs. Firstly, we introduce higher-order codifferential calculi, and the maximal prolongation of a first-order codifferential calculus. The maximal prolongation is best understood by comparing it to the process of taking the 1-forms of a manifold, and extending them to the de Rham complex. The corresponding construction for differential calculi goes back to Schauenburg
[
38
]
. The precise result we obtain is Theorem
3.19
which states that the maximal prolongation determines a functor, which is part of an adjunction between the category of first-order codifferential calculi and the category of dg coalgebras.

Secondly, we investigate into cocalculi in the (left-) equivariant setting. More precisely, we consider first-order codifferential calculi
\mathscr{W}_{1}
over coalgebras of the form
C=U/UH^{+}
, where
U
is a Hopf algebra,
H
is a right coideal subalgebra and
H^{+}
denotes the kernel of the counit of
H
. Here, equivariance means that
\mathscr{W}_{1}
is equipped with a compatible left
U
-action (in a sense made precise in Section
5
), and that the coderivation
\delta
is
U
-linear. We show that if
U
is faithfully flat over
H
, then
U
-equivariant first-order codifferential calculi on
C
correspond to quantum tangent spaces, that is, subspaces
T\subseteq C^{+}:=\ker(\varepsilon\colon C\rightarrow k)
such that

\Delta(T)\subseteq(T\oplus k[1])\otimes C,\quad HT\subseteq T.

The precise statement is found in Theorem
5.6
. A coalgebra as above can be thought of as a coalgebraic counterpart of a quantum homogeneous space, which for us are certain coideal subalgebras of Hopf algebras. We elucidate this interpretation in Section
4.3
. An analog of this result for a quantum homogeneous space
B\subseteq A
goes back to Hermission
[
24
, Theorem 2]
. Notably, left covariant first-order differential calculi over
B
also correspond to quantum tangent space, as shown by Heckenberger and Kolb
[
19
]
. However, in this case one considers quantum tangent spaces inside the finitary dual
B^{\circ}
. If we let
H=k
, we obtain a first-order codifferential calculus-analog of a theorem by Woronowicz
[
42
, Theorem 1.6]
. In the case of codifferential calculi over Hopf algebras, even more is known. See for instance the classification of bicovariant first-order codifferential calculi over Hopf algebras due to Borowiec and Mieszkalski
[
5
, Proposition 16]
.

For an equivariant first-order codifferential calculus over a coalgebra
C
as above, we further obtain a description of its maximal prolongation. Namely, if
H
is a sub bialgebra, and the induced right
C
-coaction on the corresponding quantum tangent (see (
20
)) space is trivial, then its maximal prolongation can be written as
U\otimes_{H}\mathfrak{C}(T,\check{R})
. Here,
\mathfrak{C}(T,\check{R})
is a quadratic coalgebra (see Appendix
A
) where
\check{R}\subseteq T\otimes T
is given in Proposition
5.9
.

It was pointed out in
[
5
]
that a first-order codifferential calculus over
C
induces a first-order differential calculus on the dual of
C
. We extend this observation to higher orders by showing that the maximal prolongation of the first-order codifferential calculus determines a prolongation of the dual calculus, which we call codifferential prolongation
6.6
. For a class of quantum homogeneous spaces, which quantized irreducible flag manifolds belong to, we show that the codifferential prolongation is isomorphic to the maximal prolongation (Theorem
6.9
), and from the proof of this statement, it follows that the maximal prolongation is determined by the quadratic dual algebra of
\mathfrak{C}(T,\check{R})
(Corollary
6.10
). To put this observation into context, this means that to compute the relations for the maximal prolongation of the dual first-order differential calculus, one might as well compute
\check{R}
. We demonstrate this for the antiholomorphic Heckenberger–Kolb calculus on
\mathcal{O}_{q}(\mathbb{C}P^{n})
, which leads to a new proof that the latter has classical dimension.

For the Podleś sphere
\mathcal{O}_{q}(S^{2})
, we explicitly compute the maximal prolongation of a two-dimensional first-order codifferential calculus, and obtain a sequence of Verma-like modules for
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
. An identical sequence appeared in the introduction of
[
22
]
, where it was noted that by taking finitary duals one recovers the unique covariant differential calculus of classical dimension on
\mathcal{O}_{q}(S^{2})
discovered by Podleś
[
36
]
. In
[
22
]
it is more generally shown that the Heckenberger–Kolb calculi on arbitrary irreducible quantized flag manifolds can be recovered as finitary duals of sequences derived from quantum generalized parabolic Bernstein–Gelfand–Gelfand (BGG) resolutions introduced in their earlier paper
[
23
]
. This marks the first indication, that codifferential calculi and BGG resolutions are related. In fact, the initial motivation for this article was to understand the construction in
[
22
]
in a more conceptual way, and adapt it to more general quantum flag manifolds. The precise relation between codifferential calculi and BGG resolutions — including the classical case — is unknown up to this point and will be addressed in future work. We note that BGG resolutions have been used in the construction of spectral triples
[
41
]
, and the noncommutative geometric counterparts of BGG resolutions — BGG sequences — have been used in the proof of the Baum–Connes conjecture for
SU_{q}(3)

[
40
]
. Moreover, BGG sequences are one of the cornerstones of classical parabolic geometry
[
10
]
.

a

Summary of the Paper.
The paper is organized as follows: In Section
2
we mainly fix notation and recall elementary facts about coalgebras and comodules over coalgebras. In addition, we recall the notion of a cotensor coalgebra of a bicomodule
M
over a coalgebra
C
. The latter serves as a foundation for the construction of the maximal prolongation of a first-order codifferential calculus.

In Section
3
we first recall the notion of a first-order codifferential calculus. Our definition slightly differs from the one given in
[
5
]
, but we show that there are indeed equivalent (Proposition
3.5
). After a brief account on codifferentiable maps, we introduce the notion of a higher-order codifferential calculus, and detail the construction of the maximal prolongation of a first-order codifferential calculus.

Section
4
specializes to quantum homogeneous spaces. We recall two equivalences between categories of relative Hopf modules — Takeuchi’s equivalence and its dual counterpart named Schneider’s equivalence — as well as monoidal versions thereof. We show how a quotient coalgebra of the form
C=U/UH^{+}
dualizes to a quantum homogeneous space, and end the section with an account of admissible pairings between relative Hopf modules.

Section
5
contains the main results on codifferential calculi on quantum homogeneous spaces, which heavily rely on the result of Section
4.2
.

In Section
6
we construct differential calculi and Cartan pairs from first- and higher-order codifferential calculi, where we first consider the general case and then the case of quantum homogeneous spaces.

In Section
7
we demonstrate our results by explicitly constructing codifferential calculi on the Podleś sphere and the quantized projective spaces.

a

[
27
]
and Ulrich Krähmer for suggesting me the paper of Masuoka
[
30
]
. In addition, I thank Thomas Weber for his course on Takeuchi’s categorical equivalence, which has been a big source of inspiration, as well as helpful discussions. Many thanks to Réamonn Ó Buachalla for a number of discussions and guidance in writing this paper, including comments on earlier versions.

2.
Preliminaries on coalgebras

For the entirety of this paper,
k
denotes a field of characteristic
\mathrm{char}(k)\neq 2
, and every vector space will be considered over
k
. We start by recalling some general facts about coalgebras and comodules over coalgebras, while also laying out our notation. For the purpose of this section, we fix a coalgebra
C
.

2.1.
Bicomodules over coalgebras

If
M
is a left, respectively right comodule over
C
, we will write
{}_{M}\Delta
respectively
\Delta_{M}
for the left- and right
C
-coactions of
M
. We will make heavy use of Sweedler notation, that is we write
\Delta(c)=c_{(1)}\otimes c_{(2)}
for the coproduct of an element
c\in C
, and
{}_{M}\Delta(m)=:m_{(-1)}\otimes m_{(0)}
, respectively
\Delta_{M}(m)=:m_{(0)}\otimes m_{(1)}
for an element
m\in M
. For a detailed explanation of Sweedler notation, we refer the reader to Chapters 2 and 3 in
[
37
]
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
C
and
D
be coalgebras. A
C
-
D
-bicomodule
is a
k
-vector space
M
together with a left
C
-coaction and a right
D
-coaction such that

m_{(-1)}\otimes m_{(0)(0)}\otimes m_{(0)(1)}=m_{(0)(-1)}\otimes m_{(0)(0)}\otimes m_{(1)}

for all
m\in M
.

Thus, in a
C
-
D
-bicomodule
M
, we can unambiguously write
m_{(-1)}\otimes m_{(0)}\otimes m_{(1)}
. We denote by
\mathcal{M}^{C},{}^{C}\mathcal{M}
and
{}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{D}
the categories of left
C
-comodules, right
C
-comodules and
C
-
D
-bicomodules, with morphisms given by left or right
C
-colinear, respectively
C
-
D
-bicolinear maps. If
C=D
, we write
C
-bicomodule
and
C
-bicolinear
in place of
C
-
C
-bicomodule and
C
-
C
-bicolinear. We denote by
{}^{C}\mathrm{Hom}^{D}(M,N)
the space of
C
-
D
-bicolinear maps between two
C
-
D
-bicomodules
M
and
N
. Let
C^{op}
be the opposite coalgebra of
C
. A
C
-
D
-bicomodule can be regarded as a right
D\otimes C^{op}
-comodule and vice versa. In one direction, this works as follows. For a
C
-
D
-bicomodule
M
we define a right
D\otimes C^{op}
-coaction via
m\mapsto m_{(0)}\otimes m_{(1)}\otimes m_{(-1)}
.

Next we recall the definition of a cotensor product
[
13
,
15
]
. For a right
C
-comodule
M
and a left
C
-comodule
N
the cotensor product
M\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptN
is defined as the kernel of the map

M\otimes N\rightarrow M\otimes C\otimes N,\quad m\otimes n\mapsto m_{(0)}\otimes m_{(1)}\otimes n-m\otimes n_{(-1)}\otimes n_{(0)}.

We denote elements of
M\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptN
symbolically by
m\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptn
, where
m\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptn
is in general not an elementary tensor. If
D,E
are other coalgebras, and if in addition
M
is a left
D
-comodule and
N
is a right
E
-comodule, then
M\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptN
is naturally a
D
-
E
-bicomodule with left
D
-coaction and right
E
-coactions given by
m\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptn\mapsto m_{(-1)}\otimes m_{(0)}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptn
and
m\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptn\mapsto m\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptn_{(0)}\otimes n_{(1)}
. Moreover, we obtain a functor

-\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt-\colon{}^{D}\mathcal{M}^{C}\times{}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{E}\rightarrow{}^{D}\mathcal{M}^{E}.

Thus, for morphisms
f\in{}^{D}\mathcal{M}^{C},g\in{}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{E}
, we can sense of the morphisms
f\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptg
, which are defined as
m\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptn\mapsto f(m)\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptg(n)
.
If
D=E=C
, this gives rise to a monoidal structure on
{}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
, which is remarked in
[
2
, Section 2]
in a more general setting (for more background on monoidal categories consult standard texts in category theory
[
16
,
29
]
). Here, the components of the left- and right unitors in
{}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
at a
C
-bicomodule
M
are given as follows:

C\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptM\rightarrow M,\quad c\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptm\mapsto\varepsilon(c)m,\quad M\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptC\rightarrow M,\quad m\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptc\mapsto\varepsilon(c)m.

The inverses of these maps are given by the left- and right coactions. If we are given a morphism of coalgebras
\theta\colon D\rightarrow C
, then we get a
corestriction functor

\mathrm{coRes}_{C}^{D}\colon{}^{D}\mathcal{M}^{D}\rightarrow{}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
which sends a
D
-bicomodule
M
to the same underlying vector space
M
with left- and right
C
actions given by
m\mapsto\theta(m_{(-1)})\otimes m_{(0)}
and
m\mapsto m_{(0)}\otimes\theta(m_{(1)})
.

Since we work over a field, we have the following well-known result at our disposal.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
M
be a right
C
-comodule, let
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a vector space basis of
C
and let
m\in M
. Then there are unique elements
m_{i}\in M,i\in I
(where
m_{i}=0
for all but finitely many
i\in I
) such that

\Delta_{M}(m)=\sum_{i\in I}m_{i}\otimes c_{i}.

Moreover, the subspace
\mathrm{span}_{k}\{m_{i}\mid i\in I\}
is a
C
-subcomodule of
M
containing
m
. In particular if
\Delta(m)\subseteq V\otimes C
for some subspace
V\subseteq M
, then
V
contains a
C
-subcomodule of
M
containing
m
.

2.2.
Reduced coproducts

Recall that for any coalgebra
D
that admits a coalgebra section
\iota\colon k\rightarrow D
of its counit, we can define a reduced coproduct on
D^{+}=\ker(\varepsilon)
via

\overline{\Delta}(d)=d_{(1)}\otimes d_{(2)}-\iota(1)\otimes d-d\otimes\iota(1)

which remains coassociative.
The following lemma shows that this observation generalizes to the case where we replace
\varepsilon
by a morphism of coalgebras
\theta\colon D\rightarrow C
and
\iota
by a section of
\theta
. We will use the reduced coproduct mainly in the proof of Theorem
3.19
to simplify notation.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\theta\colon D\rightarrow C
be a morphism of coalgebras, let
\iota\colon C\rightarrow D
be a morphism of coalgebras such that
\iota\circ\theta=\mathrm{id}_{C}
and write
D^{+}=\ker(\theta)
. Then the following assertions hold:

(1)

For an element
d\in D
write
d^{+}:=d-\iota(\theta(d))
. Then the subspace
D^{+}
is a
D
-bicomodule via

\Delta_{D^{+}}(d):=d_{(1)}^{+}\otimes d_{(2)},\quad{}_{D^{+}}\Delta(d):=d_{(1)}\otimes d_{(2)}^{+}.

In particular
D^{+}
is also a
C
-bicomodule, via
\mathrm{coRes}_{D}^{C}
. Moreover, the maps
D\rightarrow D^{+},d\mapsto d^{+}
and
D^{+}\rightarrow D,d\mapsto d
are
C
-bicolinear.

(2)

The map

\overline{\Delta}\colon D^{+}\rightarrow D^{+}\otimes D^{+},\quad d\mapsto d_{(1)}\otimes d_{(2)}-\iota(\theta(d_{(1)}))\otimes d_{(2)}-d_{(1)}\otimes\iota(\theta(d_{(2)}))

turns
D^{+}
into a non-counital coalgebra in
({}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C},\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt,C)
. In addition, for all
d\in D^{+}
it holds that

(1)

\Delta(d)=d_{(-1)}\otimes d_{(0)}+d_{(0)}\otimes d_{(1)}+\overline{\Delta}(d)

where the Sweedler notation refers to the left- and right
C
-actions on
D^{+}
.

Definition 2.4
.

In the situation of Lemma
2.3
, we call
\overline{\Delta}
the
reduced coproduct
and write
d_{[1]}\otimes d_{[2]}:=\overline{\Delta}(d)
for
d\in D^{+}
.

2.3.
Cotensor coalgebras

Cotensor coalgebras were introduced by Nichols
[
32
]
and later generalized by Ardizzoni, Menini and Ştefan to arbitrary coalgebras in Abelian monoidal categories
[
2
]
. Let us recall their definition.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
M
be a
C
-bicomodule. The
cotensor coalgebra
over
C
of
M
is the graded vector space

T_{C}^{c}(M):=C\oplus M\oplus\bigoplus_{n\geq 2}M^{\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptn},\quad\text{where }M^{\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptn}=\underbrace{M\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptM}_{n\text{ times}}

together with the following coproduct
\Delta
: For
c\in C
the coproduct is given by the coproduct of
C
, for
m\in M
, we set
\Delta(m)=m_{(-1)}\otimes m_{(0)}+m_{(0)}\otimes m_{(1)}
and for
\underline{m}=m^{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptm^{n}\in M^{\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptn}
where
n\geq 2
, we set

\Delta(\underline{m})=\underline{m}_{(-1)}\otimes\underline{m}_{(0)}+\underline{m}_{(0)}\otimes\underline{m}_{(1)}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(m^{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptm^{i})\otimes(m^{i+1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptm^{n}).

Recall that the coradical
\mathrm{coRad}(D)
of a coalgebra
D
is given by the sum of all simple subcoalgebras of
D

[
37
, Definition 3.4.1]
. The cotensor coalgebra has the following mapping property
[
32
, Proposition 1.4.2]
.

Proposition 2.6

(Nichols ’78)
.

Let
M
be a
C
-bicomodule. If
D
and
C^{\prime}
are coalgebras,
q\colon D\rightarrow C^{\prime},r\colon C^{\prime}\rightarrow C
are morphisms of coalgebras and
h\colon D\rightarrow M
is a
C
-bicolinear map such that
h(\mathrm{coRad}(D))=0
then there exists a unique morphism of coalgebras
h^{\flat}\colon D\rightarrow T_{C}^{c}(M)
such that the diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{C}^{c}(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{C}^{c}(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

commute (here
T_{C}^{c}(M)\rightarrow C
and
T_{C}^{c}(M)\rightarrow M
denote the projections onto the degree 0 and 1 part). Explicitly, the map
h^{\flat}
is given by

h^{\flat}(d)=r(q(d))+\sum_{n\geq 1}h(d_{(1)})\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pth(d_{(n)}).

We are mostly interested in the case where
D
is a graded coalgebra.

Example 2.7
.

Recall that a (non-negatively) graded coalgebra consists of a coalgebra
D_{\bullet}
together with a decomposition
D_{\bullet}=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}D_{n}
such that

(2)

\Delta(D_{n})\subseteq\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}D_{n-i}\otimes D_{i}

for all
n\geq 0
. This condition implies that
D_{0}
is a subcoalgebra of
D_{\bullet}
. Moreover, the projection
\pi_{0}\colon D_{\bullet}\rightarrow D_{0}
is a morphism of coalgebras, such that
\pi_{0}\circ\iota_{0}=\mathrm{id}_{D_{0}}
, where
\iota_{0}\colon D_{0}\rightarrow D_{\bullet}
is the inclusion. In the notation of Lemma
2.3
we get
D_{\bullet}^{+}=\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}D_{n}
. Here, the left- and right
D_{0}
-coactions on
D_{\bullet}^{+}
are given by
d\mapsto\pi_{0}(d_{(1)})\otimes d_{(2)}^{+}
and
d\mapsto d_{(1)}^{+}\otimes\pi_{0}(d_{(2)})
, and it follows that every homogeneous component
D_{n}
with
n\geq 0
is a sub
D_{0}
-bicomodule of
D_{\bullet}
. Now consider the projection
\pi_{1}\colon D_{\bullet}\rightarrow D_{1}
. This map is
D_{0}
-bicolinear. We claim that
\pi_{1}(\mathrm{coRad}(D_{\bullet}))=0
. Indeed, consider the quotient map
p\colon D_{\bullet}\rightarrow D_{\bullet}/D_{0}
. It follows from (
2
) that

(3)

D_{\bullet}=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\ker(p^{\otimes n}\circ\Delta^{(n-1)})

(here
\Delta^{(n-1)}
denotes the iterated coproduct). If
S
is a simple subcoalgebra of
C
, then either
S\subseteq D_{0}
or
S\cap D_{0}=0
. Suppose we have
S\cap D_{0}=0
, then
(p^{\otimes n}\circ\Delta^{(n-1)})|_{S}
is injective for all
n\geq 1
. However, by (
3
) and since simple coalgebras are finite dimensional we also have
S\subseteq\ker(p^{\otimes n}\circ\Delta^{(n-1)})
for some
n\geq 1
which is a contradiction. Thus, we have
S\subseteq D_{0}
and the claim follows. This observation allows us to construct maps from
D_{\bullet}
into cotensor coalgebras. Let
M
be a
C
-bicomodule and let
r\colon D_{0}\rightarrow C
be a morphism of coalgebras. If
h\colon D_{\bullet}\rightarrow M
is a
C
-bicolinear map such that
h=h^{\prime}\circ\pi_{1}
for some
C
-bicolinear map
h^{\prime}\colon D_{1}\rightarrow M
, then
h
induces a unique morphism of coalgebras
h^{\flat}\colon D_{\bullet}\rightarrow T_{C}^{c}(M)
by Proposition
2.6
.

3.
Codifferential calculi

We now introduce the main objects of this article, namely codifferential calculi. We begin by discussing the first order.

3.1.
First-order codifferential calculi

Recall that a
first-order differential calculus

[
42
, Definition 1.1]
over a unital associative
k
-algebra
A
consists of an
A
-bimodule
\Omega^{1}
together with a derivation
d\colon A\rightarrow\Omega^{1}
such that
\Omega^{1}
is generated as a left
A
-module by
\{d(a)\mid a\in A\}
. The latter condition is equivalent to the assertion that the map

A\otimes A\rightarrow\Omega^{1},\quad a\otimes b\mapsto ad(b)

is surjective.

We arrive at the definition of a first-order codifferential calculus by naively dualizing the above to coalgebras. For instance, instead of a derivation, we have a coderivation
[
13
, p. 44]
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
C
be a coalgebra and let
W
be a
C
-bicomodule. A
coderivation
is a linear map
\delta\colon W\rightarrow C
such that

\Delta(\delta(w))=\delta(w_{(0)})\otimes w_{(1)}+w_{(-1)}\otimes\delta(w_{(0)})

for all
w\in W
. We denote by
\mathrm{Coder}(W,C)
the
k
-vector space of all coderivations on
W
with values in
C
.

Since
\mathrm{char}(k)\neq 2
we have the following simple-to-prove, but essential property for any coderivation
\delta
:

(4)

\varepsilon\circ\delta=0.

For first-order codifferential calculi we demand that an appropriate dualization of the surjectivity condition of differential calculi holds.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
C
be a coalgebra. A
first-order codifferential calculus (FOCC)
over
C
is a
C
-bicomodule
\mathscr{W}_{1}
together with a coderivation
\delta\colon\mathscr{W}_{1}\rightarrow C
, such that the map

\mathscr{W}_{1}\rightarrow C\otimes C,\quad w\mapsto w_{(-1)}\otimes\delta(w_{(0)})

is injective.

As a first example in this exposition, we will consider the universal first-order codifferential calculus. To motivate it from a categorical point of view, note that
\mathrm{Coder}(-,C)
gives rise to a contravariant functor from
{}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
to the category of vector spaces. On morphisms
\varphi\colon V\rightarrow W
, this functor acts as follows:

\varphi^{\ast}\colon\mathrm{Coder}(W,C)\rightarrow\mathrm{Coder}(V,C),\quad\delta\mapsto\delta\circ\varphi.

In fact, as shown by Doi
[
13
]
, the functor
\mathrm{Coder}(-,C)
is representable, and the representing object is called the universal first-order codifferential calculus.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
C
be a coalgebra and let
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}:=C\otimes C/\mathrm{im}(\Delta)
, with
C
-bicomodule structure given by

[c\otimes d]\mapsto c_{(1)}\otimes[c_{(2)}\otimes d],\quad[c\otimes d]\mapsto[c\otimes d_{(1)}]\otimes d_{(2)}

and let
\delta^{u}
be the map

\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}\rightarrow C,\quad[c\otimes d]\mapsto\varepsilon(c)d-\varepsilon(d)c.

Then the pair
(\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u},\delta^{u})
is a first-order codifferential calculus, and the following universal property holds: For any
C
-bicomodule
W
and any coderivation
\delta\colon W\rightarrow C
, there exists a unique
C
-bicolinear map
\varphi\colon W\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}
, such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{u}}

commutes. In particular, the natural bijection

{}^{C}\mathrm{Hom}^{C}(W,\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u})\rightarrow\mathrm{Coder}(W,C),\quad\varphi\mapsto\delta^{u}\circ\varphi

exhibits
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}
as a representing object of
\mathrm{Coder}(-,C)
.

Proof.

Universality of the pair
(\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u},\delta^{u})
follows from
[
13
, Proposition 13]
. Thus, we only need to show that
[c\otimes d]\mapsto c_{(1)}\otimes\delta^{u}([c_{(2)}\otimes d])
is injective. Observe that for all
c,d\in C
we have

\displaystyle[c_{(1)}\otimes\delta^{u}([c_{(2)}\otimes d])]

\displaystyle=[\varepsilon(c_{(2)})c_{(1)}\otimes d-\varepsilon(d)c_{(1)}\otimes c_{(2)}]

\displaystyle=[c\otimes d].

Thus, the map
[c\otimes d]\mapsto c_{(1)}\otimes\delta^{u}([c_{(2)}\otimes d])
is a section of the projection
C\otimes C\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}
. In particular, it is injective.
∎

If
\delta\colon W\rightarrow C
, is a coderivation, then the unique map
\varphi\colon W\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}
such that
\delta^{u}\circ\varphi=\delta
is given by

(5)

\varphi(w)=[w_{(-1)}\otimes\delta(w_{(0)})]

for all
w\in W
(see for instance the proof of
[
13
, Proposition 13]
).

Remark 3.4
.

The universal first-order codifferential calculus has an alternative description due to Khalkhali
[
27
]
: As the underlying
C
-bicomodule consider
C\otimes C^{+}
, where
C^{+}=\ker(\varepsilon)
with left- and right
C
-coactions

\displaystyle{}_{C\otimes C^{+}}\Delta\colon C\otimes C^{+}\rightarrow C\otimes C\otimes C^{+},\quad c\otimes d\mapsto c_{(1)}\otimes c_{(2)}\otimes d

\displaystyle\Delta_{C\otimes C^{+}}\colon C\otimes C^{+}\rightarrow C\otimes C^{+}\otimes C,\quad c\otimes d\mapsto c_{(1)}\otimes c_{(2)}\otimes d-c\otimes d_{(1)}\otimes d_{(2)}.

For the coderivation, we take

\delta\colon C\otimes C^{+}\rightarrow C,\quad c\otimes d\mapsto\varepsilon(c)d.

The universal first-order codifferential calculus gives us an alternative way to phrase the injectivity condition, which is the one used in
[
5
]
.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
\delta\colon W\rightarrow C
be a coderivation, and let
\varphi\colon W\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}
be the unique
C
-bicolinear map as in Proposition
3.3
. Then
W/\ker(\varphi)
together with the induced coderivation
\overline{\delta}\colon W/\ker(\varphi)\rightarrow C,[w]\mapsto\delta(w)
is a first-order codifferential calculus. Moreover, the pair
(W,\delta)
is a first-order codifferential calculus if and only if
\varphi
is injective.

Proof.

We have
\overline{\delta}=\delta^{u}\circ\overline{\varphi}
, where
\overline{\varphi}\colon W/\ker(\varphi)\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u},[w]\mapsto\varphi(w)
is the
C
-bicolinear map induced by
\varphi
. Thus, this map is a well-defined coderivation. Next observe that for any pair
(W,\delta)
consisting of a
C
-bicomodule and a coderivation, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\otimes C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

commutes, where
\varphi
is again the unique
C
-bicolinear map as in Proposition
3.3
and the vertical maps are as in Definition
3.2
. This shows at once that
W/\ker(\varphi)
is a first-order codifferential calculus and that
(W,\delta)
is a first-order codifferential calculus if and only
\varphi
is injective.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

As remarked in the introduction, codifferential calculi have not been thoroughly studied until recently. The work on this article started some time before the preprint
[
5
]
appeared, after noticing that the degree 1 differential in the sequence of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
-modules in the introduction of
[
22
]
is a coderivation. This is why our approach and notation differ from the one used in
[
5
]
.

3.2.
Codifferentiable maps

In this section, we want to investigate how first-order codifferential calculi interact with morphisms of coalgebras. More generally, one can consider morphisms of coderivations
[
5
, Definition 1]
.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
C
and
D
be coalgebras, and let
(\mathscr{W}_{1}^{C},\delta^{C})
and
(\mathscr{W}_{1}^{D},\delta^{D})
be first-order codifferential calculi over
C
and
D
respectively. A morphism of coalgebras
\varphi\colon C\rightarrow D
is
codifferentiable
if there exists a
D
-bicolinear map
\varphi^{\delta}\colon\mathscr{W}_{1}^{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{1}^{D}
such that the diagram

(6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{W}_{1}^{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{W}_{1}^{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

commutes. We refer to
\varphi^{\delta}
as a
codifferential
of
\varphi

If a codifferential exists, then it is necessarily unique.

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
\varphi\colon C\rightarrow D
be a morphism of coalgebras, and let
(\mathscr{W}_{1}^{C},\delta^{C})
and
(\mathscr{W}_{1}^{D},\delta^{D})
be first-order codifferential calculi over
C
and
D
respectively. If
\varphi
is codifferentiable, then there exists at most one codifferential of
\varphi
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi^{\delta}
be a codifferential of
\varphi
. Using (
6
) and the fact that
\varphi^{\delta}
is
D
-bicolinear, we get

\varphi^{\delta}(w)_{(-1)}\otimes\delta^{D}(\varphi^{\delta}(w)_{(0)})=\varphi(w_{(-1)})\otimes\varphi(\delta^{C}(w_{(0)}))

for all
w\in\mathscr{W}_{1}^{C}
. The expression on the left-hand side is simply the canonical embedding
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{D}\hookrightarrow D\otimes D
(see Definition
3.2
) applied to
\varphi^{\delta}(w)
. Since the right-hand side does not involve
\varphi^{\delta}
, uniqueness follows.
∎

Note that for this argument to apply, the injectivity axiom for first-order codifferential calculi is essential. We also observe that any coalgebra map
\varphi\colon C\rightarrow D
is codifferentiable if we equip
C
and
D
with their corresponding universal first-order codifferential calculus. The codifferential of
\varphi
is in this case given by
[c\otimes d]\mapsto[\varphi(c)\otimes\varphi(d)]
.

Definition 3.9
.

We denote by
\mathbf{FOCC}
the category consisting of objects given by coalgebras
C
together with a first-order codifferential calculus and morphisms given by codifferential maps. Let
U\colon\mathbf{FOCC}\rightarrow\mathbf{Coalg}_{k}
be the functor that takes a first-order codifferential calculus
(\mathscr{W}_{1},C)
to the coalgebra
C
, and acts as the identity on morphisms. For a fixed coalgebra
C
we write
\mathbf{FOCC}_{C}
for the fiber category
U^{-1}(\{C\})
, that is, the category of first-order codifferential calculi over
C
, where a morphism
\mathscr{W}_{1}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathscr{W}}_{1}
is given by the unique codifferential of the identity
\mathrm{id}_{C}
.

Remark 3.10
.

It follows by Proposition
3.3
, that for any coalgebra
C
, the category
\mathbf{FOCC}_{C}
is equivalent poset category of sub-bicomodules of the universal first-order codifferential calculus
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}
over
C
. This classification of first-order codifferential calculi is stricter than the one in
[
5
]
. The reason is that the category
\mathbf{FOCC}_{C}
excludes isomorphisms in
\mathbf{FOCC}
between two first-order codifferential calculi over
C
, that are given by coalgebra automorphisms
C\rightarrow C
.

3.3.
Higher-order codifferential calculi

In this section we extend the notion of a first-order codifferential calculi to higher orders. First, recall the definition of a differential graded coalgebra.

Definition 3.11
.

A
differential graded coalgebra (dg coalgebra)
is a (non-negatively) graded coalgebra (see Example
2.7
)
D_{\bullet}
together with a
differential

\delta
, that is a linear map of degree -1 such that
\delta^{2}=0
and

(7)

\Delta(\delta(c))=\delta(c_{(1)})\otimes c_{(2)}+(-1)^{|c_{(1)}|}c_{(1)}\otimes\delta(c_{(2)})

for all homogeneous elements
c\in D_{\bullet}
.

One can equivalently describe a dg coalgebra as a comonoid in the category of (non-negatively graded) chain complexes. With this description (
7
) corresponds to the condition
\Delta(\delta(d))=\delta^{D_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet}}(\Delta(d))
where
\delta^{D_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet}}
is the differential of the tensor product of chain complexes
D_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet}
.

Let us now discuss how to verify (
7
) in practice, using this equivalent comonoidal description. Fix
d\in D_{n}
for some
n\geq 1
, then both
\Delta(\delta(d))
and
\delta^{D_{\bullet}\otimes D_{\bullet}}(\Delta(d))
lie in
\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n-1}D_{i}\otimes D_{n-1-i}
. Thus, to show that they are equal, one has to compare their projections onto each
D_{i}\otimes D_{n-1-i}
for
0\leq i\leq n-1
. Write

\Delta(\delta(d))=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\delta(d)_{(1)}^{i}\otimes\delta(d)_{(2)}^{n-1-i},\quad\Delta(d)=\sum_{j=0}^{n}d_{(1)}^{j}\otimes d_{(2)}^{n-j}

such that
\delta(d)_{(1)}^{i}\otimes\delta(d)_{(2)}^{n-1-i}\in D_{i}\otimes D_{n-1-i}
for all
0\leq i\leq n-1
and
d_{(1)}^{j}\otimes d_{(2)}^{n-j}\in D_{j}\otimes D_{n-j}
for all
0\leq j\leq n
. In order to show that (
7
) holds, it is thus sufficient and necessary to show that

(8)

\delta(d)_{(1)}^{i}\otimes\delta(d)_{(2)}^{n-1-i}=\delta(d_{(1)}^{i+1})\otimes d_{(2)}^{n-(i+1)}+(-1)^{i}d_{(1)}^{i}\otimes\delta(d_{(2)}^{n-i})

for all
d\in D_{n}
with
n\geq 1
and
0\leq i\leq n-1
.

Write
C:=D_{0}
as noted in Example
2.7
, the latter is a subcoalgebra of
D_{\bullet}
and every homogeneous component
D_{i}
for
i\geq 0
is a
C
-bicomodule. If we explicitly write down the compatibility condition (
8
) for an element
d\in D_{1}
, we get

\Delta(\delta_{1}(d))=\delta_{1}(d_{(0)})\otimes d_{(1)}+d_{(-1)}\otimes\delta_{1}(d_{(0)}).

That is,
\delta_{1}
is a coderivation, and thus by Proposition
3.5
, the quotient
D_{1}/\ker(\varphi)
, where
\varphi\colon D_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}
is the unique
C
-bicolinear map from Proposition
3.3
, is a first-order codifferential calculus over
D_{0}
, which we denote by
\underline{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(D_{\bullet})
. This construction behaves well with morphisms of dg coalgebras.

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
f\colon D_{\bullet}\rightarrow\tilde{D}_{\bullet}
be a morphism of dg coalgebras. Then the restriction
f_{0}\colon D_{0}\rightarrow\tilde{D}_{0}
is codifferentiable with respect to the codifferential calculi
\underline{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(D_{\bullet})
and
\underline{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(\tilde{D}_{\bullet})
. In particular, we get a functor

\underline{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(-)\colon\mathbf{dgCoalg}\rightarrow\mathbf{FOCC}.

Proof.

Since morphisms of dg coalgebras commute with the respective differentials, the assertion follows directly by checking that
f_{1}\colon D_{1}\rightarrow\tilde{D}_{1}
is
\tilde{D}_{0}
-bicolinear, and that it descends to a map
\underline{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(D_{\bullet})\rightarrow\underline{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(\tilde{D}_{\bullet})
.
∎

A (higher-order) codifferential calculus is a dg coalgebra
D_{\bullet}
, which embeds into the cotensor coalgebra
T_{D_{0}}^{c}(\underline{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(D_{\bullet}))
. In more detail:

Definition 3.13
.

A dg coalgebra
D_{\bullet}
is called
codifferential calculus
if the map

D_{\bullet}\rightarrow T_{D_{0}}^{c}(\underline{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(D_{\bullet}))

induced by the projection
D_{\bullet}\overset{\pi_{1}}{\rightarrow}D_{1}\rightarrow\underline{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(D_{\bullet})
(see Example
2.7
) is injective.

3.4.
The maximal prolongation

For this section we fix a coalgebra
C
and a first-order codifferential calculus
\mathscr{W}_{1}
over
C
. In addition, we write
\varphi
for the canonical map
\mathscr{W}_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}
from Proposition
3.3
. Note that since
\mathscr{W}_{1}
is a first-order codifferential calculus, the map
\varphi
is injective by Proposition
3.5
. The goal is to universally construct a codifferential calculus
\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}
such that
\underline{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}})=\mathscr{W}_{1}
. The key step for this construction is the following definition.

Definition 3.14
.

Consider the following map

\check{\delta}_{2}\colon\mathscr{W}_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\mathscr{W}_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u},\quad w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}\mapsto[\delta(w_{1})\otimes\delta(w_{2})].

We let
\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}
be the largest sub
C
-bicomodule of
\mathscr{W}_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\mathscr{W}_{1}
such that
\check{\delta}_{2}(\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}})\subseteq\varphi(\mathscr{W}_{1})
. Moreover, we let
\delta_{2}\colon\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{1}
be the unique map such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{W}_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\mathscr{W}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{W}_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\delta}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

commutes.

From the definition above it is not obvious which elements of
\mathscr{W}_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\mathscr{W}_{1}
belong to
\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}
. However, we have the following sufficient condition.

Lemma 3.15
.

If
w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}\in\mathscr{W}_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\mathscr{W}_{1}
such that
\check{\delta}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2})=0
, then
w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}\in\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}
.

Proof.

We claim that
(\mathrm{id}\otimes\check{\delta}\otimes\mathrm{id})(w_{1(-1)}\otimes w_{1(0)}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2(0)}\otimes w_{2(1)})\in C\otimes\varphi(\mathscr{W}_{1})\otimes C
. Indeed, using the coderivation property of
\delta
gives

\displaystyle\phantom{==}w_{1(-1)}\otimes[\delta(w_{1(0)})\otimes\delta(w_{2(0)})]\otimes w_{2(1)}

\displaystyle=\delta(w_{1})_{(1)}\otimes[\delta(w_{1})_{(2)}\otimes\delta(w_{2(0)})]\otimes w_{2(1)}-\delta(w_{1(0)})\otimes[w_{1(1)}\otimes\delta(w_{2(0)})]\otimes w_{2(1)}

\displaystyle=\delta(w_{1})_{(1)}\otimes[\delta(w_{1})_{(2)}\otimes\delta(w_{2})_{(1)}]\otimes\delta(w_{2})_{(2)}-\delta(w_{1})_{(1)}\otimes[\delta(w_{1})_{(2)}\otimes w_{2(-1)}]\otimes\delta(w_{2(0)})

\displaystyle\phantom{==}-\delta(w_{1(0)})\otimes[w_{1(1)}\otimes\delta(w_{2})_{(1)}]\otimes\delta(w_{2})_{(2)}+\delta(w_{1(0)})\otimes[w_{1(1)}\otimes w_{2(-1)}]\otimes\delta(w_{2(0)}).

Here, the first summand is zero, since
[\delta(w_{1})\otimes\delta(w_{2})]=0
by assumption, the second summand is equal to

\displaystyle-({}_{\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}}\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})([\delta(w_{1})\otimes w_{2(-1)}]\otimes\delta(w_{2(0)}))

\displaystyle=-({}_{\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}}\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})([\delta(w_{1(0)})\otimes w_{1(1)}]\otimes\delta(w_{2}))

\displaystyle=({}_{\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}}\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})([w_{1(-1)}\otimes\delta(w_{1(0)})]\otimes\delta(w_{2}))

\displaystyle=({}_{\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}}\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})(\varphi(w_{1})\otimes\delta(w_{2}))

where we used that
w_{1}\otimes w_{2}\in\mathscr{W}_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\mathscr{W}_{1}
, and the last step uses (
5
). Similarly, the third summand equals to

\displaystyle-(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta_{\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}})(\delta(w_{1(0)})\otimes[w_{1(1)}\otimes\delta(w_{2})])=-(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta_{\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}})(\delta(w_{1})\otimes\varphi(w_{2}))

and finally the last summand vanishes since

\displaystyle w_{1(0)}\otimes w_{1(1)}\otimes w_{2(-1)}\otimes w_{2(0)}

\displaystyle=w_{1(0)}\otimes w_{1(1)}\otimes{}_{\mathscr{W}_{1}}\Delta(w_{2})

\displaystyle=w_{1}\otimes w_{2(-1)}\otimes{}_{\mathscr{W}_{1}}\Delta(w_{2(0)})

\displaystyle=w_{1}\otimes w_{2(-1)(1)}\otimes w_{2(-1)(2)}\otimes w_{2(0)}.

Thus, we conclude that
w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}\in\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}
by Lemma
2.2
.
∎

The following assertion shows that
\delta_{2}
has the properties of a differential of a dg coalgebra in degree 2.

Lemma 3.16
.

The map
\delta_{2}
from Definition
3.14
satisfies
\delta\circ\delta_{2}=0
. In addition, the identities

(9)

\displaystyle{}_{\mathscr{W}_{1}}\Delta(\delta_{2}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}))

\displaystyle=w_{1(-1)}\otimes\delta_{2}(w_{1(0)}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2})+\delta(w_{1})\otimes w_{2}

(10)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(\delta_{2}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}))

\displaystyle=\delta_{2}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2(0)})\otimes w_{2(1)}-w_{1}\otimes\delta(w_{2})

hold for every
w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}\in\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}
.

Proof.

Let
w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}\in\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}
, then

\delta(\delta_{2}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}))=\delta^{u}(\varphi(\delta_{2}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2})))=\delta^{u}([\delta(w_{1})\otimes\delta(w_{2})])=0

where the last equality follows from the definition of
\delta^{u}
and (
4
). This shows the first assertion. For the second assertion we compute

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}\otimes\varphi)({}_{\mathscr{W}_{1}}\Delta(\delta_{2}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2})))

\displaystyle={}_{\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}}\Delta(\varphi(\delta_{2}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2})))=\delta(w_{1})_{(1)}\otimes[\delta(w_{1})_{(2)}\otimes\delta(w_{2})]

\displaystyle=w_{1(-1)}\otimes[\delta(w_{1(0)})\otimes\delta(w_{2})]+\delta(w_{1(0)})\otimes[w_{1(1)}\otimes\delta(w_{2})]

\displaystyle=w_{1(-1)}\otimes[\delta(w_{1(0)})\otimes\delta(w_{2})]+\delta(w_{1})\otimes[w_{2(-1)}\otimes\delta(w_{2(0)})]

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\varphi)(w_{1(-1)}\otimes\delta_{2}(w_{1(0)}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2})+\delta(w_{1})\otimes w_{2})

where we used (
5
) in the last step. Thus, the first identity holds by injectivity of
(\mathrm{id}\otimes\varphi)
. Similarly, we compute

\displaystyle(\varphi\otimes\mathrm{id})(\Delta_{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(\delta_{2}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2})))

\displaystyle=[\delta(w_{1})\otimes\delta(w_{2(0)})]\otimes w_{2(1)}+[\delta(w_{1(0)})\otimes w_{1(1)}]\otimes\delta(w_{2})

\displaystyle=[\delta(w_{1})\otimes\delta(w_{2(0)})]\otimes w_{2(1)}

\displaystyle\phantom{==}+[\delta(w_{1})_{(1)}\otimes\delta(w_{1})_{(2)}-w_{1(-1)}\otimes\delta(w_{1(0)})]\otimes\delta(w_{2})

\displaystyle=(\varphi\otimes\mathrm{id})(\delta_{2}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2(0)})\otimes w_{2(1)}-w_{1}\otimes\delta(w_{2})).

Hence, also the second identity holds.
∎

We now extend the above to a full dg coalgebra. For
n\geq 2
we define the degree
n
component as

(11)

\mathscr{W}_{n}^{\mathrm{max}}:=\bigcap_{i+j+2=n}\mathscr{W}_{1}^{\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pti}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\mathscr{W}_{1}^{\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptj}.

By setting
\mathscr{W}_{0}^{\mathrm{max}}:=C
it follows that
\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}
is a subcoalgebra of
T_{C}^{c}(\mathscr{W}_{1})
. We define a differential on
\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}
by setting
\delta_{1}:=\delta
and letting

(12)

\delta_{n}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{n}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{i-1}w_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\delta_{2}(w_{i}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{i+1})\otimes\dots\otimes w_{n}

where
w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{n}\in\mathscr{W}_{n}^{\mathrm{max}}
for
n\geq 2
.

Proposition 3.17
.

The pair
(\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}},\delta_{\bullet})
is codifferential calculus with
\underline{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}})=\mathscr{W}_{1}
.

Proof.

If we can show that
(\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}},\delta_{\bullet})
is a dg coalgebra, then it is clear by construction that
\underline{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}})=\mathscr{W}_{1}
and that
\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}\rightarrow T_{C}^{c}(\mathscr{W}_{1})
is an embedding. Thus, it is sufficient to prove the first assertion. Firstly, we show that
\delta_{\bullet}
is well-defined. For
n=2
this follows by definition. If
n\geq 3
we can write

\displaystyle\delta_{n}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{n})

\displaystyle=\delta_{i}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{i})\otimes(w_{i+1}\otimes w_{i+2})\otimes\dots\otimes w_{n}

\displaystyle\phantom{==}+(-1)^{i-1}w_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\delta_{3}(w_{i}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{i+1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{i+2})\otimes\dots\otimes w_{n}

\displaystyle\phantom{==}+(-1)^{i+1}w_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes(w_{i}\otimes w_{i+1})\otimes\delta_{n-i-1}(w_{i+2}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{n})

for all
1\leq i\leq n-2
and
w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{n}\in\mathscr{W}_{n}^{\mathrm{max}}
, where we emphasized the
i,i+1
component. Thus, well-defined ness reduces to the case
n=3
.
Firstly, for any element
w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{3}\in\mathscr{W}_{3}^{\mathrm{max}}
we have
\delta_{3}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{3})\in\mathscr{W}_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\mathscr{W}_{1}
by applying Lemma
3.16
:

\displaystyle\phantom{==}(\Delta_{\mathscr{W}_{1}}\otimes\mathrm{id})(\delta_{2}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2})\otimes w_{3}-w_{1}\otimes\delta_{2}(w_{2}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{3}))

\displaystyle=\delta_{2}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2(0)})\otimes w_{2(1)}\otimes w_{3}-w_{1}\otimes\delta(w_{2})\otimes w_{3}-w_{1(0)}\otimes w_{1(1)}\otimes\delta_{2}(w_{2}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{3})

\displaystyle=\delta_{2}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2})\otimes w_{3(-1)}\otimes w_{3(0)}-w_{1}\otimes\delta(w_{2})\otimes w_{3}-w_{1}\otimes w_{2(-1)}\otimes\delta_{2}(w_{2(0)}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{3})

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{id}\otimes{}_{\mathscr{W}_{1}}\Delta)(\delta_{2}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2})\otimes w_{3}-w_{1}\otimes\delta_{2}(w_{2}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{3})).

Next, we also have
\delta_{3}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{3})\in\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}
as a consequence of Lemma
3.15
, since

(13)

\check{\delta}(\delta_{3}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{3}))=[\delta(\delta_{2}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2})\otimes\delta(w_{3}))]-[\delta(w_{1})\otimes\delta(\delta_{2}(w_{2}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{3}))]=0

using the first assertion of Lemma
3.16
. We proceed by showing that
\delta_{n-1}\circ\delta_{n}=0
for all
n\geq 2
. For
n=2
this follows from Lemma
3.16
and for
n=3
this follows from (
13
). For
n\geq 4
we get

\displaystyle\phantom{==}\delta_{n-1}(\delta_{n}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{n}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq j<i-1\leq n}(-1)^{i+j}w_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\delta_{2}(w_{j}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{j+1})\otimes\dots\otimes\delta_{2}(w_{i}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{i+1})\otimes\dots\otimes w_{n}

\displaystyle\phantom{==}+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n-2}(-1)^{i+j}w_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\delta_{2}(w_{i}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{i+1})\otimes\dots\otimes\delta_{2}(w_{j+1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{j+2})\otimes\dots\otimes w_{n}

\displaystyle\phantom{==}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-2}w_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\delta_{2}(\delta_{3}(w_{i}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{i+1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{i+2}))\otimes\dots\otimes w_{n}.

The first two summands cancel after applying an index shift and the last summand is zero by the case
n=3
.

We are left to show that
\delta_{\bullet}
satisfies (
7
). For this fix
\underline{w}:=w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{n}\in\mathscr{W}_{n}^{\mathrm{max}}
. As we noted previously, it is sufficient to show (
8
) for all
0\leq i\leq n-1
. Consider first the case
i\neq 0,n-1
. In this case left-hand side of (
8
) evaluates to

\displaystyle\delta_{i+1}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{i+1})\otimes(w_{i+2}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{n})

\displaystyle+(-1)^{k}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{i})\otimes\delta_{n-i}(w_{i+1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{n}).

It is also immediate that the right-hand side gives the same, since the coproduct is given by deconcatenation. Now let
i=0
the by using Lemma
3.16
the left-hand side of (
8
) becomes

\displaystyle\delta_{2}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2})_{(-1)}\otimes(\delta_{2}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2})_{(0)}\otimes w_{3}\otimes\dots\otimes w_{n})

\displaystyle\phantom{==}-w_{1(-1)}\otimes(w_{1(0)}\otimes\delta_{n-1}(w_{2}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{n}))

\displaystyle=w_{1(-1)}\otimes(\delta_{2}(w_{1(0)}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2})\otimes w_{3}\otimes\dots\otimes w_{n})+\delta(w_{1})\otimes(w_{2}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{n})

\displaystyle\phantom{==}-w_{1(-1)}\otimes(w_{1(0)}\otimes\delta_{n-1}(w_{2}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{n}))

\displaystyle=\delta(w_{1})\otimes(w_{2}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{n})+w_{1(-1)}\otimes\delta_{n}(w_{1(0)}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{n}).

Again, by definition of the coproduct on
T_{C}^{c}(\mathscr{W}_{1})
it is immediate that the right-hand side of (
8
) is the same in this situation. The case
i=n-1
is analogous.
∎

Definition 3.18
.

Let
\mathscr{W}_{1}
be a first-order codifferential calculus. We call
(\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}},\delta_{\bullet})
the
maximal prolongation
of
\mathscr{W}_{1}
.

This name is justified by the result below.

Theorem 3.19
.

Let
D_{\bullet}
be a dg coalgebra, and let
\mathscr{W}_{1}
be a first-order codifferential calculus over
C
. Write
D:=D_{0}
, such that
\underline{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(D_{\bullet})
is a first-order codifferential calculus over
D
. Then any codifferentiable map
f\colon D\rightarrow C
(with respect to
\underline{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(D_{\bullet})
and
\mathscr{W}_{1}
) extends uniquely to a morphism of dg coalgebras
f^{\flat}\colon D_{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}
. In particular, the maximal prolongation determines a functor
\mathbf{FOCC}\rightarrow\mathbf{dgCoalg}
which is right-adjoint to
\underline{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(-)
.

Proof.

Let
f\colon D\rightarrow C
be codifferentiable.
We start by showing uniqueness. Let
f^{\flat}\colon D_{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}
be a morphism of dg coalgebras which extends
f
. By the same logic as in the proof of Proposition
3.12
, the degree 1 component
f_{1}\colon D_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{1}
descends to a
C
-bicolinear map
\underline{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(D_{\bullet})\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{1}
, and this map is precisely the codifferential
f^{\delta}
of
f
. Next view
f^{\flat}
as a morphism of coalgebras
D_{\bullet}\rightarrow T_{C}^{c}(\mathscr{W}_{1})
— which we can do since
\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}
is a subcoalgebra of
T_{C}^{c}(\mathscr{W}_{1})
— and observe that the diagrams

(14)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{C}^{c}(\mathscr{W}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{\bullet}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{C}^{c}(\mathscr{W}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{W}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{\bullet}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

commute, where
h
is given by the projection
D_{\bullet}\rightarrow\underline{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(D_{\bullet})
followed by
f^{\delta}
. As in Example
2.7
, the coradical of
D_{\bullet}
gets mapped to zero by
h
, thus it follows that
f^{\flat}
is unique by
2.6
. For existence, note that the same result also gives existence of a morphism of coalgebras
f^{\flat}\colon D_{\bullet}\rightarrow T_{C}^{c}(\mathscr{W}_{1})
such that the diagrams in (
14
) commute. Thus, we only need to show that
f^{\flat}
restricts to a morphism of dg coalgebras to
\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}
. Recall that
f^{\flat}
is given by the formula

(15)
