f^{\flat}(d)=f(\pi_{0}(d))+\sum_{n\geq 0}h(d_{(1)})\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pth(d_{(n)}),\quad d\in D_{\bullet}.

It follows that
f^{\flat}(d)\in\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}
for every
d\in D_{n}
if
n=0,1
. Also note that by definition of
h
we have
\delta(h(d))=f(\delta_{1}(d))
for all
d\in D_{1}
, and that we can replace the coproduct in (
15
) by the reduced coproduct since
h(d)=0
for all
d\in D
. We first show that
f^{\flat}(D_{2})\subseteq\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}
. Since
f^{\flat}
is
C
-bicolinear it suffices to show that for all
d\in D_{2}
we have
\check{\delta}(f^{\flat}(d))=\check{\delta}(h(d_{(1)})\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pth(d_{(2)}))=[\delta(h(d_{(1)}))\otimes\delta(h(d_{(2)}))]=[w_{(-1)}\otimes\delta(w_{(0)})]
for some
w\in\mathscr{W}_{1}
. We claim that the latter equality holds for
w=h(\delta_{2}(d))
. Indeed, by combining (
1
) and (
8
) we have

\delta_{2}(d)_{(-1)}\otimes\delta_{2}(d)_{(0)}=d_{(-1)}\otimes\delta_{2}(d_{(0)})+\delta_{1}(d_{[1]})\otimes d_{[2]}.

Because
h
is left
C
-colinear we obtain

\displaystyle w_{(-1)}\otimes w_{(0)}

\displaystyle=h(\delta_{2}(d))_{(-1)}\otimes h(\delta_{2}(d)_{(0)})=f(\delta_{2}(d)_{(-1)})\otimes h(\delta_{2}(d)_{(0)})

\displaystyle=f(d_{(-1)})\otimes h(\delta_{2}(d_{(0)}))+f(\delta_{1}(d_{[1]}))\otimes h(d_{[2]})

\displaystyle=f(d_{(-1)})\otimes h(\delta_{2}(d_{(0)}))+\delta(h(d_{[1]}))\otimes h(d_{[2]})

and consequently

\displaystyle w_{(-1)}\otimes\delta(w_{(0)})

\displaystyle=f(d_{(-1)})\otimes\delta(h(\delta_{2}(d_{(0)})))+\delta(h(d_{[1]}))\otimes\delta(h(d_{[2]}))

\displaystyle=f(d_{(-1)})\otimes f(\delta_{1}(\delta_{2}(d_{(0)})))+\delta(h(d_{[1]}))\otimes\delta(h(d_{[2]}))

\displaystyle=\delta(h(d_{[1]}))\otimes\delta(h(d_{[2]}))=\delta(h(d_{(1)}))\otimes\delta(h(d_{(2)})).

It follows that
f^{\flat}(d)\in\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}
. Moreover, for the reason that
h(d)=0
if
d\in D_{n}
with
n\neq 1
, it also holds that
h(d_{(1)})\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pth(d_{(2)})\in\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}
for any
d\in D_{\bullet}
, and thus
f^{\flat}(d)\in\mathscr{W}_{n}^{\mathrm{max}}
for any
d\in D_{\bullet}
, since we can write

h(d_{(1)})\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pth(d_{(n)})=h(d_{(1)})\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pth(d_{(i)(1)})\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pth(d_{(i)(2)})\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pth(d_{(i-1)})

for any
n\geq 2
and
1\leq i\leq n-1
. We are left with showing that
f^{\flat}(\delta_{n}(d))=\delta_{n}(f^{\flat}(d))
for all
n\geq 1
and
d\in D_{n}
. For
n=1
this is equivalent to
f(\delta_{1}(d))=\delta(h(d))
, which holds as already remarked. For
n=2
we have
\delta_{2}(h(d_{(1)})\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pth(d_{(2)}))=w=h(\delta_{2}(d))
in view of
3.14
. For
n\geq 3
, note that the differential of
D_{\bullet}
is also compatible with the reduced coproduct in the sense that

\overline{\Delta}(\delta(d))=\delta(d_{[1]})\otimes d_{[2]}+(-1)^{|d_{[1]}|}d_{[1]}\otimes\delta(d_{[2]}).

By induction on
n
it follows that

\overline{\Delta}^{(n-2)}(\delta_{n}(d))=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{i-1}d_{[1]}\otimes\dots\otimes\delta(d_{[i]})\otimes\dots\otimes d_{[n-1]}.

Therefore, we can write

\displaystyle f^{\flat}(\delta_{n}(d))

\displaystyle=h(\delta_{n}(d)_{(1)})\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pth(\delta_{n}(d)_{(n-1)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{i-1}h(d_{(1)})\otimes\dots\otimes h(\delta(d_{(i)}))\otimes\dots\otimes h(d_{(n-1)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{i-1}h(d_{(1)})\otimes\dots\otimes\delta_{2}(h(d_{(i)})\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pth(d_{(i+1)}))\otimes\dots\otimes h(d_{(n)})

\displaystyle=\delta_{n}(f^{\flat}(d)).

∎

The case where
D_{\bullet}
is itself a codifferential calculus with
\underline{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(D_{\bullet})=\mathscr{W}_{1}
is of most interest to us.

Corollary 3.20
.

Let
\mathscr{W}_{1}
be a first-order codifferential calculus over
C
. If
D_{\bullet}
is a codifferential calculus such that
\underline{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(D_{\bullet})=\mathscr{W}_{1}
(here this also means that
D_{0}=C
) then the unique morphism of dg coalgebras
D_{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}
extending
\mathrm{id}_{C}\colon D_{0}=C\rightarrow C
is an embedding.

Proof.

Since
D_{\bullet}
is a codifferential calculus we know that
D_{\bullet}\rightarrow T_{C}^{c}(\mathscr{W}_{1})
is an embedding. However, we know from the proof of Theorem
3.19
that this map restricts to
\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}
, and that it is the unique extension of
\mathrm{id}_{C}
to a morphism of dg coalgebras
D_{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}
.
∎

4.
Quantum homogeneous spaces

In the present section, we will make use of notation for relative Hopf modules. Let
A
be a bialgebra, and recall that a left
A
-comodule algebra
B
is a monoid in the category of left
A
-comodules, which amounts to the assertion that multiplication of
B
is
A
-colinear. We denote by
{}_{B}^{A}\mathcal{M}
the category of left
B
-modules and left
A
-comodules
\mathcal{F}
such that

{}_{A}\Delta(b\triangleright f)=b_{(-1)}f_{(-1)}\otimes b_{(0)}\triangleright f_{(0)},\quad f\in\mathcal{F},b\in B

with morphisms given by left
B
-linear and left
A
-colinear maps. We write
{}^{A}\mathcal{M}_{B}
and
{}_{B}^{A}\mathcal{M}_{B}
for analogously defined categories, where in the last category, the objects are also assumed to be
B
-bimodules. Dually, for a left
A
-module coalgebra
C
, we can define categories
{}_{A}^{C}\mathcal{M},{}_{A}\mathcal{M}^{C},{}_{A}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
, where the compatibility condition for an object
M
in the first category reads as

{}_{M}\Delta(a\triangleright m)=a_{(1)}\triangleright m_{(-1)}\otimes a_{(2)}\triangleright m_{(0)},\quad a\in A,m\in M.

Lastly, for a left
A
-comodule algebra
B
and a
right

A
-module coalgebra
C
, we write
{}^{C}\mathcal{M}_{B}
for the category consisting of left
C
-comodules and right
B
-modules
V
such that

{}_{V}\Delta(vb)=v_{(-1)}\triangleleft b_{(-1)}\otimes v_{(0)}\triangleleft b_{(0)},\quad v\in V,b\in B

and for a
right

A
-comodule algebra
B
and a left
A
-module coalgebra
C
, we write
{}_{B}\mathcal{M}^{C}
for the analogous category.

4.1.
Takeuchi’s categorical equivalence

For this section, fix a Hopf algebra
A
and a left coideal subalgebra
B\subseteq A
, that is,
B
is a subalgebra of
A
such that
\Delta(B)\subseteq A\otimes B
. Note that
B
is naturally a left
A
-comodule algebra with coaction given by the coproduct. We consider the quotient coalgebra
A/B^{+}A
together with the canonical projection
\pi_{B}\colon A\rightarrow A/B^{+}A
, and denote this quotient from now on by
\pi_{B}(A)
. Observe that the latter is a right
A
-module coalgebra with right
A
-action induced by the multiplication of
A
.

We now recall the Takeuchi functors between
{}_{B}^{A}\mathcal{M}_{B}
and
{}^{\pi_{B}}\mathcal{M}_{B}:={}^{\pi_{B}(A)}\mathcal{M}_{B}
. The first functor
\Phi\colon{}_{B}^{A}\mathcal{M}_{B}\rightarrow{}^{\pi_{B}}\mathcal{M}_{B}
sends an object
\mathcal{F}\in{}_{B}^{A}\mathcal{M}_{B}
to
\mathcal{F}/B^{+}\mathcal{F}
, where the right
B
-action is the one induced by the right
B
-action on
\mathcal{F}
, and the left
\pi_{B}(A)
-coaction is given by
[f]\mapsto\pi_{B}(f_{(-1)})\otimes[f_{(0)}]
. On morphisms the functor acts by taking induced maps on quotients. The second functor
\Psi\colon{}^{\pi_{B}}\mathcal{M}_{B}\rightarrow{}_{B}^{A}\mathcal{M}_{B}
sends an object
V\in{}^{\pi_{B}}\mathcal{M}_{B}
to
A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptV
(here we write
\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0pt:=\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}(A)}\hskip 1.0pt
), where the left
B
-action is given by left multiplication on the first factor, the left
A
-coaction is given by applying the coproduct to the first factor, and the right
B
-action is given by
(a\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptv)\triangleleft b=ab_{(1)}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptvb_{(2)}
. The functor
\Psi
sends a morphism
\varphi\in{}^{\pi_{B}}\mathcal{M}_{B}
to
\mathrm{id}_{A}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0pt\varphi
.

Theorem 4.1
.

The functor
\Phi
is left adjoint to
\Psi
. In more detail, the unit of the adjunction is given by

\eta_{\mathcal{F}}\colon\mathcal{F}\rightarrow A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{F}/B^{+}\mathcal{F},\quad f\mapsto f_{(-1)}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0pt[f_{(0)}]

and the counit by

\varepsilon_{V}\colon(A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptV)/B^{+}(A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptV)\rightarrow V,\quad[a\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptv]\mapsto\varepsilon(a)v.

If
A
is faithfully flat as a right
B
-module, then this adjunction is an equivalence.

As a consequence of the above Theorem, we can write
B
as the coinvariants under the right
\pi_{B}(A)
-coaction on
A
.

Corollary 4.2
.

Suppose that
A
is faithfully flat as a right
B
-module. Then

B=A{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0pt\pi_{B}(A)}=\{a\in A\mid a_{(1)}\otimes\pi_{B}(a_{(2)})=a\otimes\pi_{B}(1)\}.

The latter justifies the following terminology.

Definition 4.3
.

A left coideal subalgebra
B\subseteq A
such that
A
is faithfully flat as a right
B
-module is called
quantum homogeneous space
.

Next let us recall that the category
{}_{B}^{A}\mathcal{M}_{B}
is monoidal with monoidal product given by
-\otimes_{B}-
and monoidal unit
B
(consult
[
34
, Section 4.1]
for more details on this monoidal structure). Thus, the category
{}^{\pi_{B}}\mathcal{M}_{B}
inherits a monoidal structure through the equivalence in Theorem
4.1
. In general however, there is no known description of the monoidal structure without reference to the category
{}_{B}^{A}\mathcal{M}_{B}
.

The situation changes if we specialize our setup. Assume that the antipode of
A
is invertible and that
AB^{+}\subseteq B^{+}A
. Then, by Lemma
D.1
,
\pi_{B}(A)
is a quotient Hopf algebra of
A
. In this case consider the full subcategory
{}^{\pi_{B}}\mathcal{M}_{0}
of
{}^{\pi_{B}}\mathcal{M}_{B}
consisting of objects
V
, such that
v\triangleleft b=\varepsilon(b)v
. This category is equivalent to
{}^{\pi_{B}}\mathcal{M}
and thus monoidal with monoidal product given by the tensor product over
k
. Further consider the full subcategory
{}_{B}^{A}\mathcal{M}_{0}
of
{}_{B}^{A}\mathcal{M}_{B}
consisting of objects
\mathcal{F}
such that
\mathcal{F}B^{+}\subseteq B^{+}\mathcal{F}
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Assume that the antipode of
A
is bijective and that
AB^{+}\subseteq B^{+}A
. If
A
is faithfully flat as a right
B
-module, then the functor
\Phi
from Theorem
4.1
restricts to a monoidal equivalence
{}_{B}^{A}\mathcal{M}_{0}\rightarrow{}^{\pi_{B}}\mathcal{M}_{0}
. The monoidal structure on this restriction is given by

\Phi(\mathcal{F}\otimes_{B}\mathcal{G})\rightarrow\Phi(\mathcal{F})\otimes\Phi(\mathcal{G}),\quad[f\otimes_{B}g]\mapsto[f]\otimes[g].

Proof.

See
[
34
, Proposition 4.1]
.
∎

4.2.
Schneider’s dual equivalence

In this section, we demonstrate that the results of the previous section carry over to a dualized setup. Let
U
be a Hopf algebra and let
H
be a right coideal subalgebra, that is
H
is a subalgebra such that
\Delta(H)\subseteq H\otimes U
. As in the previous section, we obtain a quotient coalgebra
C:=U/UH^{+}\cong U\otimes_{H}k
, which is naturally a left
U
-module coalgebra.

The following Hopf–Galois-type isomorphism will be useful later.

Lemma 4.5
.

The canonical map

\chi\colon U\otimes_{H}U\rightarrow C\otimes U,\quad x\otimes_{H}y\mapsto x_{(-1)}\otimes x_{(0)}y

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The inverse is given by

C\otimes U\rightarrow U\otimes_{H}U,\quad[x]\otimes y\mapsto x_{(1)}\otimes_{H}S(x_{(2)})y.

One checks that both maps are well-defined.
∎

We would now like to describe an equivalence of categories analogous to
4.1
. To that end, we consider the coinvariant functor

{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}(-)\colon{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}\rightarrow{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{C},\quad M\mapsto{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}M=\{m\in M\mid m_{(-1)}\otimes m_{(0)}=1\otimes m\}.

As well as the induction functor

U\otimes_{H}-\colon{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{C}\rightarrow{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C},\quad V\mapsto U\otimes_{H}V.

Here, the left
C
-coaction on
U\otimes_{H}V
is given by applying the left
C
-coaction on
U
to the first factor, and the right
C
-coaction is given by the formula

x\otimes_{H}v\mapsto(x_{(1)}\otimes_{H}v_{(0)})\otimes x_{(2)}v_{(1)}.

By a routine argument one shows that the above functors are mutually adjoint.

Proposition 4.6
.

The induction functor
U\otimes_{H}-\colon{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}\rightarrow{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{C}
is left adjoint to the coinvariant functor
{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}(-)\colon{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}\rightarrow{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{C}
. This adjunction is exhibited the unit, and counit given by

\eta_{V}\colon V\rightarrow{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}(U\otimes_{H}V),\quad v\mapsto 1\otimes_{H}v

for
V\in{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{C}
and

\varepsilon_{M}\colon U\otimes_{H}{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}M\rightarrow M,\quad x\otimes_{H}m\mapsto xm

for
M\in{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
.

Remark 4.7
.

The functors
U\otimes_{H}-
and
{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}(-)
can also be viewed as functors between
{}_{H}\mathcal{M}
and
{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}
, and they still form a pair of adjoint functors with respect to those domains.

Note that if
U
is faithfully flat as a left
H
-module, we have an equivalence of categories

{}_{H}\mathcal{M}_{H}^{U}\simeq{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{C}.

This equivalence is given by the same functors
\Phi
and
\Psi
as in the previous section, by changing their respective handedness, that is, in one direction we have
M\mapsto M/MH^{+}
and in the other direction
V\mapsto V\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptU
. Using this equivalence it follows that
H={}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}U
(compare Corollary
4.2
). This observation allows us to show that the functors
{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}(-)
and
U\otimes_{H}-
are equivalences.

Theorem 4.8
.

Assume that
U
is faithfully flat as a left- and right
H
-module then
U\otimes_{H}-
and
{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}(-)
are mutually inverse equivalences of categories. This assertion holds for both pairs of categories
({}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{C},{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C})
and
({}_{H}\mathcal{M},{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M})
.

Proof.

Since
H={}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}U
, the assumptions of
[
39
, Theorem 3.7]
are satisfied. Moreover, by Lemma
4.5
also condition of (1) in the same theorem holds. Thus, the induction functor is an equivalence between
{}_{H}\mathcal{M}
and
{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}
. The assertion that this equivalence also holds between
{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
and
{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{C}
follows by Theorem
C.2
using the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
4.1
.
∎

To end our discussion of categorical equivalences, we would like to prove a monoidal version of the above result, similar to Proposition
4.4
. To this end observe that the category
{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
is monoidal with monoidal product given by
-\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt-
. Indeed, if
M,N\in{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
, then for any elements
m\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptn\in M\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptN
and
x\in U
, we have

\displaystyle\Delta_{M}(x_{(1)}m)\otimes x_{(2)}n

\displaystyle=x_{(1)}m_{(0)}\otimes x_{(2)}m_{(1)}\otimes x_{(3)}n=x_{(1)}m\otimes x_{(2)}n_{(-1)}\otimes x_{(3)}n_{(0)}

\displaystyle=x_{(1)}m\otimes{}_{N}\Delta(x_{(2)}n).

That is, the left
U
-action on
M\otimes N
restricts to the cotensor product. Moreover, one can show that this left action is compatible with the left- and right
C
-coactions, in other words, we have
M\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptN\in{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
. Since
C
is a left
U
-module coalgebra it holds that
C\in{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
, and lastly, by checking that the associators and unitors of
({}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C},\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt,C)
are
U
-linear one shows that the monoidal structure of
{}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
extends to
{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
.

Again, it is not obvious how to describe the monoidal structure on
{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{C}
, which is induced by the one on
{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
via the equivalence in
4.8
. We therefore restrict to the full subcategory
{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{0}
consisting of objects
V\in{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{C}
, where the right
C
-coaction is given by
v\mapsto v\otimes[1]
. This subcategory is equivalent to
{}_{H}\mathcal{M}
. Thus, if
H
is a sub bialgebra, then it is monoidal with monoidal product given by the tensor product over
k
. As the next result shows, this monoidal structure is the same as the one induced by the equivalence. In the following, we denote the full subcategory of
{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
consisting of objects
M
such that
{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}M\in{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{0}
by
{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{0}
.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
M\in{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{0}
and
N\in{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
, then

{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}(M\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptN)={}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}M\otimes{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}N.

In particular,
{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{0}
is a monoidal subcategory of
{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
, and moreover, if
H
is a sub bialgebra of
U
and
U
is faithfully flat as a left and right
H
-module, then
{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}(-)
restricts to a monoidal equivalence between
({}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{0},\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt,C)
and
({}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{0},\otimes,k)
.

Proof.

Recall the left
C
-coaction on
M\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptN
is given by
m\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptn\mapsto m_{(-1)}\otimes m_{(0)}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptn
. Thus, the inclusion “
\supseteq
” is immediate. For the converse inclusion let
u\in{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}(M\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptN)
. Fix a basis
\{w_{i}\}_{i}
of
N
. This lets us write

u=\sum_{i}m_{i}\otimes w_{i}

for uniquely determined
m_{i}\in M
, and we have

\sum_{i}{}_{M}\Delta(m_{i})\otimes w_{i}={}_{M\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptN}\Delta(u)=[1]\otimes u=\sum_{i}[1]\otimes m_{i}\otimes w_{i}.

It follows that
\Delta(m_{i})=[1]\otimes m_{i}
for all
i
, and we have
u\in{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}M\otimes N
. Next, fix a basis
\{v_{j}\}_{j}
of
{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}M
. We can similarly write

u=\sum_{j}v_{j}\otimes n_{j}

for uniquely determined
n_{j}\in N
, and we have

\sum_{j}v_{j}\otimes{}_{N}\Delta(n_{j})=\sum_{j}\Delta_{M}(v_{j})\otimes n_{j}=\sum_{j}v_{j}\otimes[1]\otimes n_{j}.

Therefore, we have
n_{j}\in{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}N
for all
j
and
u\in{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}M\otimes{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}N
.
∎

4.3.
Quantum homogeneous spaces as finitary duals of coalgebras

Let
U
be a Hopf algebra and
H\subseteq U
a right coideal subalgebra and
C:=U\otimes_{H}k
as in the previous section. In this section we will explain how to obtain a principal quantum homogeneous space as the finitary dual of
C
. To begin, we fix a tensor subcategory
\mathcal{C}\subseteq{}_{U}\mathcal{M}
of finite dimensional
U
-modules, that is a full subcategory of finite dimensional
U
-modules such that
k\in\mathcal{C}
, and which is closed under taking direct sums, tensor products and duals
[
31
, p. 165]
. We consider the finitary dual of
U
with respect to
\mathcal{C}
, the subspace of
U^{\ast}
which consists of all matrix coefficients of modules in
\mathcal{C}
(see Appendix
B
)

A:=U_{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}=\{c_{f,v}^{M}\mid M\in\mathcal{C}\}.

There is a natural left
U
-action on
A
given by
x\triangleright a=a_{(2)}(x)a_{(1)}
, for
x\in U
and
a\in A
. Let
B:={}^{H}A:=\{a\in A\mid h\triangleright a=\varepsilon(h)a\text{ for all }h\in H\}
denote the
infinitesimal invariants
of
A
with respect to the restriction of this action to
H
. We argue that we can identify
B
with the finitary dual
C_{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
of
C
with respect to
\mathcal{C}
. The latter is defined as the subspace of
C^{\ast}
consisting of all functionals
f\colon C\rightarrow k
, such that there is a left
U
-submodule
N\subseteq C
such that
C/N\in\mathcal{C}
and
f|_{N}\equiv 0
.

Lemma 4.10
.

The space infinitesimal invariants
B
is a left coideal subalgebra of
A
. In addition, we have an isomorphism of
k
-algebras

\iota\colon C_{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}\rightarrow B,\quad\iota(f)(x)=f(x\otimes_{H}1).

Proof.

We only show the second assertion. The first one is well known (see for instance
[
31
, Theorem 2.2]
).
To see that the map
\iota
is well-defined, let
f\in C_{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
and let
N\subseteq C
be a
U
-submodule of finite codimension such that
f|_{N}=0
and
C/N\in\mathcal{C}
. Denote by
\overline{f}
the induced linear map
C/N\rightarrow k
, then
\iota(f)
can be written as the matrix coefficient
c_{\overline{f},1\otimes_{H}1+N}^{C/N}
, which shows that the image of
\iota
lies in
A
. Next let
h\in H
and
x\in U
be arbitrary, then

\displaystyle(h\triangleright\iota(f))(x)

\displaystyle=(\iota(f)_{(2)}(h)\iota(f)_{(1)})(x)=\iota(f)(xh)=f(xh\otimes_{H}1)=\varepsilon(h)\iota(f)(x)

and thus even
\iota(f)\subseteq B
for all
f\in C_{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
. The inverse is given by the map
B\rightarrow C_{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
which sends an element
b\in B
to the map
x\otimes_{H}1\mapsto b(x)
. This is well-defined, since

b(xh)=(h\triangleright b)(x)=\varepsilon(h)b(x)

for all
b\in B,x\in U
and
h\in H
.
∎

As in Section
4.1
we consider the quotient map
\pi_{B}\colon A\rightarrow A/B^{+}A
.

Theorem 4.11
.

Assume that the antipode of
A
is bijective. Then the following hold

(1)

If
H
is a sub bialgebra, then
AB^{+}\subseteq B^{+}A
. In particular,
\pi_{B}(A)
is a quotient Hopf algebra.

(2)

Suppose that
H
is
\mathcal{C}
-semisimple, in the sense that the restriction of every
V\in\mathcal{C}
is completely reducible as an
H
-module. Then
A
is faithfully flat as a left- and right
B
-module.

Proof.

(1)
: For any
a\in A
and
b\in B
we have

\displaystyle h\triangleright(a_{(1)}bS(a_{(2)}))

\displaystyle=a_{(1)(2)}(h_{(1)})b_{(2)}(h_{(2)})S(a_{(2)})_{(2)}(h_{(3)})a_{(1)(1)}b_{(1)}S(a_{(2)})_{(1)}

\displaystyle=a_{(2)}(h_{(1)})b_{(2)}(h_{(2)})S(a_{(3)})(h_{(3)})a_{(1)}b_{(1)}S(a_{(4)})

\displaystyle=(a_{(2)}S(a_{(3)}))(h)a_{(1)}bS(a_{(4)})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(h)a_{(1)}bS(a_{(2)}).

We see that
B
is closed under the left adjoint action. In particular, if
b\in B^{+}
, then
ab=a_{(1)}bS(a_{(2)})a_{(3)}\in B^{+}A
, thus
AB^{+}\subseteq B^{+}A
. The assertion that
\pi_{B}(A)
is a quotient Hopf algebras now follows from
D.1
.

For
(2)
see
[
31
, Theorem 2.2]
.
∎

We can summarize the previous result by saying that if the antipode of
A
is bijective and
H
is a sub bialgebra which is
\mathcal{C}
-semisimple, then the finitary dual
C_{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
is a principal quantum homogeneous space.

Remark 4.12
.

The condition that the antipode of
A
is bijective is for instance implied if the antipode of
U
is bijective, and the tensor category
\mathcal{C}
is closed under duals with respect to
S^{-1}
(see Section
B
).

4.4.
Admissible pairings

We let
U,H,C,A,B
and
\pi_{B}
be as in the previous section. In this section, we discuss pairings between objects
\mathcal{F}\in{}_{B}^{A}\mathcal{M}_{B}
and
M\in{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
, as well as between objects
V\in{}^{\pi_{B}}\mathcal{M}_{B}
and
T\in{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{C}
, that are compatible with the various (co)module structures as described in the following.

Definition 4.13
.

(1)

Let
V\in{}^{\pi_{B}}\mathcal{M}_{B}
and
T\in{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{C}
. A pairing
\langle-,-\rangle\colon V\times T\rightarrow k
is called
admissible
if

(16)

\langle v,ht\rangle=v_{(-1)}(h)\langle v_{(0)},t\rangle,\quad\langle vb,t\rangle=\langle v,t_{(0)}\rangle b(t_{(1)})

for all
v\in V,t\in T,h\in H
and
b\in B
.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{F}\in{}_{B}^{A}\mathcal{M}_{B}
and
M\in{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
. Then a pairing
\langle-,-\rangle\colon\mathcal{F}\times M\rightarrow k
is called
admissible
if

(17)

\begin{gathered}\langle bf,m\rangle=b(m_{(-1)})\langle f,m_{(0)}\rangle,\quad\langle fb,m\rangle=\langle f,m_{(0)}\rangle b(m_{(1)})\\
\langle f,Xm\rangle=f_{(-1)}(X)\langle f_{(0)},m\rangle\end{gathered}

for all
f\in\mathcal{F},b\in B,m\in M
and
X\in U
.

In the definition above, we make sense of and expression like
b(c)
for
b\in B
and
c\in C
, using the identification
B\cong C_{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
of
4.10
, and an expression
\pi_{B}(a)(h)
is understood as
a(h)
for
a\in A
and
h\in H
. The value of
a(h)
only depends on
\pi_{B}(a)
, since

b(h)=b(1h)=(h\triangleright b)(1)=\varepsilon(h)b(1)=0

for all
b\in B^{+}
.

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\in{}_{B}^{A}\mathcal{M}_{B},M\in{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
, and write
V:=\mathcal{F}/B^{+}\mathcal{F},T:={}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}M
. Then for every admissible pairing
\langle-,-\rangle\colon\mathcal{F}\times M\rightarrow k
, the induced pairing
\langle-,-\rangle^{\flat}\colon V\times T\rightarrow k
given by
\langle[f],t\rangle^{\flat}=\langle f,t\rangle
is admissible. Conversely, any admissible pairing
\langle-,-\rangle\colon V\times T\rightarrow k
between arbitrary objects
V\in{}^{\pi_{B}}\mathcal{M}_{B}
and
T\in{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{C}
extends to an admissible pairing

\langle-,-\rangle^{\sharp}\colon A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptV\times U\otimes_{H}T\rightarrow k,\quad\langle a\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptv,x\otimes_{H}t\rangle^{\sharp}=a(x)\langle v,t\rangle.

If the antipode of
A
is bijective,
A
is faithfully flat as a right
B
-module and
U
is faithfully flat as a left- and right
H
-module, then
\langle-,-\rangle\mapsto\langle-,-\rangle^{\flat}
is a bijection from the set of admissible pairings between
\mathcal{F}
and
M
and the set of admissible pairings between
V
and
T
.

Proof.

Let
\langle-,-\rangle\colon\mathcal{F}\times M\rightarrow k
be an admissible pairing. Let
f\in\mathcal{F},t\in T
and
b\in B
, then

\displaystyle\langle b^{+}f,t\rangle=b^{+}(t_{(-1)})\langle f,t_{(0)}\rangle=b^{+}([1])\langle f,t\rangle=0.

Thus, the pairing
\langle-,-\rangle^{\flat}
is well-defined. Admissibility follows directly from the admissibility of
\langle-,-\rangle
.

For the other direction, we start with a pairing
\langle-,-\rangle\colon V\times T\rightarrow k
, then its extension is well-defined since

\displaystyle\langle a\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptv,Xh\otimes t\rangle

\displaystyle=a(Xh)\langle v,t\rangle=a_{(1)}(X)a_{(2)}(h)\langle t,v\rangle=a_{(1)}(X)[a_{(2)}](h)\langle v,t\rangle

\displaystyle=a(X)v_{(-1)}(h)\langle v_{(0)},t\rangle=a(X)\langle v,ht\rangle=\langle a\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptv,X\otimes ht\rangle.

For all
a\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptv\in A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptV,X\in U,h\in H
and
t\in T
. One then checks that
\langle-,-\rangle^{\sharp}
is admissible.

For the last assertion, we can use that under our assumptions
\mathcal{F}\cong A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptV
and
M\cong U\otimes_{H}T
(see Theorem
4.8
and Theorem
4.1
). Thus, the extension
\langle-,-\rangle^{\sharp}
of an admissible pairing
\langle-,-\rangle
induces an admissible pairing between
\mathcal{F}
and
M
. The resulting mapping is inverse to
\langle-,-\rangle\mapsto\langle-,-\rangle^{\flat}
, arguing as follows: If we start with a pairing
\langle-,-\rangle
between
\mathcal{F}
and
M
, then we have for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
and
t\in T

\displaystyle\langle f,t\rangle^{\flat,\sharp}

\displaystyle=\langle f_{(-1)}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0pt[f_{(0)}],1\otimes_{H}t\rangle^{\flat,\sharp}=f_{(-1)}(1)\langle[f_{(0)}],t\rangle^{\flat}=\langle[f],t\rangle^{\flat}=\langle f,t\rangle

which implies that
\langle-,-\rangle^{\flat,\sharp}=\langle-,-\rangle
, since admissible pairings are determined by their restriction
\mathcal{F}\times T\rightarrow k
. If we start with a pairing
\langle-,-\rangle^{\flat}\colon V\times T\rightarrow k
, then using the same computation, its extension satisfies

\displaystyle\langle f,t\rangle^{\sharp}=\langle[f],t\rangle

for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
and
t\in T
. Thus, the correspondence is bijective.
∎

For a pairing between vector spaces
\langle-,-\rangle\colon V\times T\rightarrow k
we write

W^{\perp}:=\{t\in T\mid\langle w,t\rangle=0\text{ for all }w\in W\}

for a subset
W\subseteq V
and similarly

S^{\perp}=\{v\in V\mid\langle v,s\rangle=0\text{ for all }s\in S\}

for a subset
S\subseteq T
.

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\in{}_{B}^{A}\mathcal{M}_{B}
and
M\in{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
, and let
\langle-,-\rangle\colon\mathcal{F}\times M\rightarrow k
be an admissible pairing. Then for any subobject
N\subseteq M
in
{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
, the orthogonal complement
N^{\perp}
is a subobject of
\mathcal{F}
in
{}_{B}^{A}\mathcal{M}_{B}
. Moreover, if
B
separates the elements of
C
, then also for every subobject
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
, the orthogonal complement
\mathcal{E}^{\perp}
is a subobject of
M
.

Proof.

For the first assertion let
f\in N^{\perp},b\in B
and
n\in N
, then

\displaystyle\langle fb,n\rangle=\langle f,n_{(0)}\rangle b(n_{(1)})=0

since
n_{(0)}\otimes n_{(1)}\in N\otimes C
, thus
fb\in N^{\perp}
. Similarly,
bf\in N^{\perp}
. Also for any
f\in N^{\perp},x\in U
and
n\in N

(18)

\displaystyle\langle f_{(-1)}(x)f_{(0)},n\rangle=\langle f,xn\rangle=0

since
xn\in N
, thus
f_{(-1)}\otimes f_{(0)}\in A\otimes N^{\perp}
. For the second assertion let
m\in\mathcal{E}^{\perp},b\in B
and
e\in\mathcal{E}
, then

\langle e,b(m_{(1)})m_{(0)}\rangle=\langle eb,m\rangle=0

since
eb\in\mathcal{E}
. It follows that
b(m_{(1)})m_{(0)}\in\mathcal{E}^{\perp}
for all
b\in B
, since
B
separates the elements of
C
, we have
m_{(0)}\otimes m_{(1)}\in\mathcal{E}^{\perp}\otimes C
. By the same argument, we also have
m_{(-1)}\otimes m_{(0)}\in C\otimes\mathcal{E}^{\perp}
. Lastly, if
x\in U
, then for all
e\in\mathcal{E}

\langle e,xm\rangle=e_{(-1)}(x)\langle e_{(0)},m\rangle=0

since
e_{(-1)}\otimes e_{(0)}\in A\otimes\mathcal{E}
, and thus also
xm\in\mathcal{E}^{\perp}
.
∎

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
V\in{}^{\pi_{B}}\mathcal{M}_{B},T\in{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{C}
and let
\langle-,-\rangle^{\flat}\colon W\times T\rightarrow k
be an admissible pairing. Then the following assertions for the extended pairing

\langle-,-\rangle\colon(A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptV)\times(U\otimes_{H}T)\rightarrow k

(see Proposition
4.14
) hold.

(1)

For any subobject
S\subseteq T
in
{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{C}
, one has
(U\otimes_{H}S)^{\perp}=A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptS^{\perp}
.

(2)

Assume that the antipode of
A
is bijective, that
A
is faithfully flat as a left
B
-module, and that
U
is faithfully flat as a left- and right
H
-module. Further assume that
B
separates the elements of
C
. Let
W\subseteq V
be a subobject in
{}^{\pi_{B}}\mathcal{M}_{B}
. Using Lemma
4.15
and Theorem
4.8
we can write
(A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptW)^{\perp}=U\otimes_{H}S
for some subobject
S\subseteq T
in
{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{C}
. If
W^{\perp\perp}=W
and
S^{\perp\perp}=S
(note that this is automatic if
V
and
T
are finite dimensional and
\langle-,-\rangle^{\flat}
is non-degenerate), then
(A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptW)^{\perp}=U\otimes_{H}W^{\perp}
.

Proof.

(1)
: The inclusion
A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptS^{\perp}\subseteq(U\otimes_{H}S)^{\perp}
follows by direct computation. For the other inclusion let
a\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptv\in(U\otimes_{H}S)^{\perp}
and write

a\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptv=\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}\otimes v_{i}

such that
a_{1},\dots,a_{N}
is linear independent. This lets us choose
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\in U
such that
a_{i}(X_{j})=\delta_{ij}
for all
1\leq i,j\leq N
. Now for all
1\leq i\leq N
and
s\in S

\displaystyle\langle v_{i},s\rangle^{\flat}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}a_{j}(X_{i})\langle v_{j},s\rangle=\langle a\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptv,X_{i}\otimes_{H}s\rangle=0.

That is
v_{i}\in S^{\perp}
for all
1\leq i\leq N
, and therefore
a\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptv\in A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptS^{\perp}
.

(2)
: On the one hand we have

A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptW\subseteq(A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptW)^{\perp\perp}=(U\otimes_{H}S)^{\perp}\overset{(1)}{=}A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptS^{\perp}.

Therefore, we have
W\subseteq S^{\perp}
. On the other hand one has
U\otimes_{H}W^{\perp}\subseteq(A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptW)^{\perp}=U\otimes_{H}S
by direct computation. Again using
(1)
this gives

A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptS^{\perp}=(U\otimes_{H}S)^{\perp}\subseteq(U\otimes_{H}W^{\perp})^{\perp}=A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptW^{\perp\perp}=A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptW.

Thus, also
S^{\perp}\subseteq W
, and it follows that
W^{\perp}=S^{\perp\perp}=S
.
∎

5.
Equivariant codifferential calculi

In this section, we consider codifferential calculi, that are equipped with a compatible left action of a Hopf algebra. Thus, we fix a Hopf algebra
U
and a left
U
-module coalgebra
C
over
U
.

5.1.
First- and higher-order equivariant codifferential calculi

We start with basic definitions.

Definition 5.1
.

(1)

A first-order codifferential calculus
(\mathscr{W}_{1},\delta)
over
C
is defined to be
U
-equivariant
if
\mathscr{W}_{1}\in{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
and
\delta
is
U
-linear.

(2)

A (higher-order) codifferential calculus
(\mathscr{W}_{\bullet},\delta)
over
C
is called
U
-equivariant
if for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
the component
\mathscr{W}_{n}
is an object in
{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
, and the coproduct as well as the differential
\delta
are
U
-linear.

The easiest example of an equivariant codifferential calculus is given by the universal one.

Example 5.2
.

Let
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}
be the universal first-order codifferential calculus. Since
C
is a
U
-module coalgebra, the image of the comultiplication is a
U
-submodule of
C\otimes C
, thus
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}
is a quotient
U
-module of
C\otimes C
. Moreover, one can check that the
U
-action is compatible with the
C
-coactions and that
\delta^{u}
is
U
-linear. Thus, the universal first-order codifferential calculus is
U
-equivariant. This equivariance extends to the higher-orders. Since
{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
is a monoidal category with monoidal structure as in the discussion above
4.9
it follows that
(\mathscr{W}_{n}^{u})^{\mathrm{max}}=(\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u})^{\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptn}\in{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
for all
n\geq 0
. Moreover, recall that in the case of the universal codifferential the degree two differential is given by

\delta_{2}^{u}\colon\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u},\quad w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}\mapsto[\delta^{u}(w_{1})\otimes\delta^{u}(w_{2})]

which is a
U
-linear map. It follows that also for every
n\geq 2
the differential
\delta_{n}^{u}
is
U
-linear since it is given by the formula (
12
). Since the coproduct is given by deconcatenation, it is
U
-linear as well, therefore the maximal prolongation of the universal first-order codifferential calculus is
U
-equivariant.

It follows directly from the definition, that for any
U
-equivariant codifferential calculus
(\mathscr{W}_{\bullet},\delta)
over
C
the associated first-order codifferential calculus
\underline{\mathscr{W}_{1}}(\mathscr{W}_{\bullet})
is
U
-equivariant. The converse also holds.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
(\mathscr{W}_{1},\delta)
be a
U
-equivariant first-order codifferential calculus. Then its maximal prolongation is also
U
-equivariant.

Proof.

Consider the embedding
\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}\hookrightarrow T_{C}^{c}(\mathscr{W}_{1})
. As in Example
5.2
, we see that each
(\mathscr{W}_{1})^{\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptn}
is a
U
-submodule and that the coproduct of
T_{C}^{c}(\mathscr{W}_{1})
is
U
-linear. Thus, it suffices to show that every
\mathscr{W}_{n}^{\mathrm{max}}
is a
U
-submodule of
(\mathscr{W}_{1})^{\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptn}
and that the differential is
U
-linear. In fact, we only need to check this for
\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}
and
\delta_{2}
by (
11
) and (
12
). Recall that
\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}
is defined as the maximal sub
C
-bicomodule such that the map

\check{\delta}\colon\mathscr{W}_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\mathscr{W}_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u},\quad w\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptv\mapsto[\delta(w)\otimes\delta(v)]

factors through the canonical embedding
\mathscr{W}_{1}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}
. As in the previous example this map is
U
-linear. Note also that the embedding
\varphi\colon\mathscr{W}_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u},w\mapsto[w_{(-1)}\otimes\delta(w_{(0)})]
is
U
-linear. Thus, if
M
is any sub
C
-bicomodule such that
\check{\delta}(M)\subseteq\varphi(\mathscr{W}_{1})
, then also
\check{\delta}(UM)\subseteq\varphi(\mathscr{W}_{1})
. Moreover,
UM
is again a sub
C
-bicomodule. For instance, the assertion that
UM
is closed under the left
C
-coaction holds, since

(xm)_{(-1)}\otimes(xm)_{(0)}=x_{(1)}m_{(-1)}\otimes x_{(2)}m_{(0)}\in C\otimes UM

for all
x\in U
and
m\in M
. In particular, it follows that
\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}
is a
U
-submodule, and the induced map
\delta_{2}\colon\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{1}
is
U
-linear.
∎

5.2.
Classification of equivariant first-order codifferential calculi

In this section, we specialize the setup to the case where
C=U\otimes_{H}k
, for a right coideal subalgebra
H\subseteq U
. Our goal is to show that
U
-equivariant first-order codifferential calculi are all classified by quantum tangent spaces if
U
is faithfully flat as a left and right
H
-module.

Definition 5.4
.

A
quantum tangent space
is a subspace
T\subseteq C^{+}=\ker(\varepsilon)
such that

(19)

\Delta(T)\subseteq(T\oplus k[1])\otimes C,\quad HT\subseteq T.

Note that the condition on the image of
T
under the coproduct can be equivalently phrased as demanding that
T
is a right
C
-subcomodule of
C^{+}
, where the latter is endowed with the coaction

(20)

C^{+}\rightarrow C^{+}\otimes C,\quad c\mapsto c_{(1)}^{+}\otimes c_{(2)}.

Moreover, since
T
is closed under the left
H
-action one can deduce that
T
is an object in
{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{C}
. Indeed, we have compatibility of the
H
-action and the right
C
action

\displaystyle(hc)_{(1)}^{+}\otimes(hc)_{(2)}

\displaystyle=h_{(1)}c_{(1)}\otimes h_{(2)}c_{(2)}-\varepsilon(h_{(1)})\varepsilon(c_{(1)})[1]\otimes h_{(2)}c_{(2)}

\displaystyle=h(c_{(1)}\otimes c_{(2)})-\varepsilon(c_{(1)})h_{(1)}[1]\otimes h_{(2)}c_{(2)}

\displaystyle=h(c_{(1)}^{+}\otimes c_{(2)})

for all
h\in H
and
c\in C
, where we used the fact that
h[1]=[h]=\varepsilon(h)[1]
.

If we apply the induction functor
U\otimes_{H}-
to
T
, we get the following.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
T\subseteq C
be a quantum tangent space, and write
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{T}:=U\otimes_{H}T
. Then

\delta^{T}\colon\mathscr{W}_{1}^{T}\rightarrow C,\quad x\otimes_{H}t\mapsto xt

is a coderivation. Moreover, if
U
is flat as a left
H
-module then
(\mathscr{W}_{1}^{T},\delta^{T})
is a
U
-equivariant first-order codifferential calculus.

Proof.

Let
x\in U
and let
t\in T
. Then

\displaystyle\Delta(\delta^{T}(x\otimes_{H}t))

\displaystyle=\Delta(xt)=x_{(1)}t_{(1)}\otimes x_{(2)}t_{(2)}

\displaystyle=x_{(1)}t_{(1)}^{+}\otimes x_{(2)}t_{(2)}+x_{(1)}\varepsilon(t_{(1)})[1]\otimes x_{(2)}t_{(2)}

\displaystyle=\delta^{T}(x_{(1)}\otimes_{H}t_{(1)}^{+})\otimes x_{(2)}t_{(2)}+[x_{(1)}]\otimes\delta^{T}(x_{(2)}\otimes_{H}t)

\displaystyle=(\delta^{T}\otimes\mathrm{id})\Delta_{\mathscr{W}_{1}^{T}}(x\otimes_{H}t)+(\mathrm{id}\otimes\delta^{T}){}_{\mathscr{W}_{1}^{T}}\Delta(x\otimes_{H}t).

Now assume that
U
is flat as a left
H
-module. Consider the map

\overline{\chi}\colon U\otimes_{H}C\rightarrow C\otimes C,\quad x\otimes_{H}c,\quad x\otimes_{H}c\mapsto[x_{(1)}]\otimes x_{(2)}c

and observe that this is simply the map
\chi\otimes_{H}\mathrm{id}_{k}
where
\chi
is the canonical map
U\otimes_{H}U\rightarrow C\otimes U
from Lemma
4.5
. Hence, it is an isomorphism. By flatness, we have an embedding
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{T}\hookrightarrow U\otimes_{H}C
. Therefore, also the map

\mathscr{W}_{1}^{T}\rightarrow C\otimes C,\quad x\otimes_{H}t\mapsto x_{(1)}\otimes x_{(2)}t

is injective. However, this map is precisely the map which is required to be injective in Definition
3.2
, since
x_{(1)}\otimes x_{(2)}t=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\delta^{T}){}_{\mathscr{W}_{1}^{T}}\Delta(x\otimes_{H}t)
for all
x\in U
and
t\in T
. Lastly, equivariance follows directly by the definition of
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{T}
and
\delta^{T}
.
∎

We can now state our classification result.

Theorem 5.6
.

Assume that
U
is faithfully flat as a left- and right
H
module. Then the assignment
T\mapsto(\mathscr{W}_{1}^{T},\delta^{T})
from Proposition
5.5
induces a bijection

\displaystyle\{\text{quantum tangent spaces }T\subseteq C\}

\displaystyle\overset{1:1}{\longleftrightarrow}\{\text{equivariant first-order codifferential calculi }(\mathscr{W}_{1},\delta)\}/\cong.

Proof.

Given an equivariant first-order codifferential calculus
(\mathscr{W}_{1},\delta)
set
T:=\delta({}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}\mathscr{W}_{1})
. By the universal property of the universal first-order codifferential calculus
3.3
,
T
only depends on the isomorphism class of
\mathscr{W}_{1}
. Moreover,
T
is a quantum tangent space. Indeed, we have
HT\subseteq T
, since the
C
-coinvariants are closed under the
H
-action and
\delta
is
U
-linear. Since the image of any coderivation is contained in
C^{+}
, we in particular have
T\subseteq C^{+}
. Lastly, by

\displaystyle\delta(w)_{(1)}^{+}\otimes\delta(w)_{(2)}

\displaystyle=w_{(-1)}^{+}\otimes\delta(w_{(0)})+\delta(w_{(0)})^{+}\otimes w_{(1)}=[1]^{+}\otimes\delta(w)+\delta(w_{(0)})\otimes w_{(1)}

\displaystyle=\delta(w_{(0)})\otimes w_{(1)}

for all
w\in{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}\mathscr{W}_{1}
, the restriction of
\delta
to
{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}\mathscr{W}_{1}
is right
C
-colinear, and
T
is a right
C
-subcomodule of
C^{+}
. To show the assignment is a bijection, note that on the one hand, by the categorical equivalence of Corollary
4.8
, we get an isomorphism in
{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}

U\otimes_{H}T=U\otimes_{H}\delta({}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}\mathscr{W}_{1})\cong\mathscr{W}_{1}

which is concretely given by
x\otimes_{H}\delta(w)\mapsto xw
. Since
\delta
is
U
-linear, this map is also compatible with the differentials, and hence an isomorphism of
U
-equivariant first-order codifferential calculi. On the other hand, given a quantum tangent space
T\subseteq C
, then
{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}(U\otimes_{H}T)=1\otimes_{H}T
, which again follows from the categorical equivalence, and thus
\delta^{T}({}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}\mathscr{W}_{1}^{T})=T
.
∎

Example 5.7
.

Consider the universal first-order codifferential calculus
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}
over
C
. As an object in
{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
we have

\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}\cong C\otimes C^{+}.

Here, the
C
-bicomodule structure on
C\otimes C^{+}
is given as in
3.4
, and the left
U
-action is simply the diagonal action.
Since the left coaction is given by applying the coproduct to the first factor, the space of left
C
-coinvariants of
C\otimes C^{+}
is
C^{+}
. Thus, the quantum tangent space of
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}
is
C^{+}
, and we have an isomorphism of first-order codifferential calculi
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}\cong U\otimes_{H}C^{+}
. Explicitly, we have an isomorphism

U\otimes_{H}C^{+}\rightarrow C\otimes C^{+},\quad x\otimes d\mapsto[x_{(1)}]\otimes x_{(2)}d

which is given by restricting the isomorphism
\overline{\chi}
in the proof of Proposition
5.5
. In particular, its inverse is the map given by
[x]\otimes d\mapsto x_{(1)}\otimes_{H}S(x_{(2)})d
. Combining this isomorphism with the isomorphism
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}\cong C\otimes C^{+}
, the isomorphism between
U\otimes_{H}C^{+}
and
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}
is given by

U\otimes_{H}C^{+}\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u},\quad x\otimes_{H}d\mapsto[[x_{(1)}]\otimes x_{(2)}d]

with inverse

\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}\rightarrow U\otimes_{H}C^{+},\quad[[x]\otimes d]\mapsto x_{(1)}\otimes_{H}S(x_{(2)})d-\varepsilon(d)x\otimes_{H}[1].

To finish the section, we look at the special case where
H
is the ground field. The result we obtain dualizes a result of Woronowicz
[
42
]
about covariant first-order differential calculi on Hopf algebras.

Corollary 5.8
.

For any Hopf algebra
U
, equivariant first-order codifferential calculi on
U
are, up to isomorphism, in bijective correspondence with subspaces
T\subseteq U^{+}
such that

\Delta(T)\subseteq(T\oplus k1)\otimes U.

More precisely, given a subspace
T
as above, then
U\otimes T
is a first-order codifferential calculus with codifferential

\delta^{T}\colon U\otimes T\rightarrow U,\quad x\otimes t\mapsto xt.

5.3.
Computation of higher-order equivariant codifferential calculi

We let
U,H
and
C
be the same as in the previous section. We additionally assume that
H
is a subbialgebra and that
U
is faithfully flat as a left and right
H
-module.

As we know from Theorem
5.6
an equivariant first-order codifferential calculus
\mathscr{W}_{1}
over
C
is uniquely determined by its quantum tangent space
T
. The goal of this section is to give a description of the maximal prolongation of
\mathscr{W}_{1}
, in the case where the right
C
-coaction on
T
is trivial, that is, when
T\in{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{0}
(see the discussion around Proposition
4.9
). The key step in computing the maximal prolongation is to compute
\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}
. Recall from section
3.4
that this space is determined by the map

\mathscr{W}_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\mathscr{W}_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u},\quad w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}\mapsto[\delta(w_{1})\otimes\delta(w_{2})].

Let us assume that
\mathscr{W}_{1}=U\otimes_{H}T
with
T\in{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{0}
. By Example
5.7
, we can identify
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}
with
U\otimes_{H}C^{+}
, and using the monoidal equivalence from Proposition
4.9
we can identify
\mathscr{W}_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\mathscr{W}_{1}
with
U\otimes_{H}(T\otimes T)
. Under these identifications, the map above becomes

\hat{\delta}\colon U\otimes_{H}(T\otimes T)\rightarrow U\otimes_{H}C^{+},\quad 1\otimes_{H}([x]\otimes t)\mapsto x_{(1)}\otimes_{H}S(x_{(2)})t.

Here it suffices to specify the values of elements of the form
1\otimes_{H}(t_{1}\otimes t_{2})
by
U
-linearity. In the following we write
\check{\delta}_{0}([x]\otimes t):=\check{\delta}(1\otimes_{H}([x]\otimes t))
.

Proposition 5.9
.

Write
\check{R}:=\check{\delta}_{0}^{-1}(U\otimes_{H}T)
, then under the identification
\mathscr{W}_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\mathscr{W}_{1}\cong U\otimes_{H}(T\otimes T)
, the subspace
\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}
corresponds to
U\otimes_{H}\check{R}
.

Proof.

We have to show that
U\otimes_{H}\check{R}
is the largest
C
-sub-bicomodule of
U\otimes_{H}(T\otimes T)
which gets mapped to
U\otimes_{H}T
under
\check{\delta}
. Indeed, it follows by construction that
U\otimes_{H}\check{R}
gets mapped into
U\otimes_{H}T
under
\check{\delta}
. To show that it is maximal, let
L\subseteq U\otimes_{H}(T\otimes T)
be a
C
-sub-bicomodule which gets mapped to
U\otimes_{H}T
. Arguing as in the end of the proof of Proposition
5.3
we can assume without loss of generality that
L
is a
U
-submodule, and thus a subobject of
U\otimes_{H}(T\otimes T)
in
{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
. Write
W:={}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}L
. By Proposition
4.6
and Theorem
4.8
, the map

U\otimes_{H}W\rightarrow L,\quad u\otimes_{H}w\mapsto uw

is an isomorphism in
{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
. Since
W\subseteq{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}(U\otimes_{H}(T\otimes T))=1\otimes_{H}(T\otimes T)
, we can identify
W
as a subspace of
T\otimes T
. Under this identification, the above map is just the identity, and we have
L=U\otimes_{H}W
, and by assumption
\check{\delta}(W)\subseteq\check{\delta}(L)\subseteq U\otimes_{H}T
. Thus, we have
W\subseteq\check{R}
and therefore
L\subseteq U\otimes_{H}\check{R}
.
∎

For the next result, recall the notion of a quadratic coalgebra
\mathfrak{C}(T,\check{R})
, corresponding to a finite dimensional vector space
T
and a subspace
\check{R}\subseteq T\otimes T
(see Section
A
). Note that if
T\in{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{0}
and
\check{R}
is an
H
-submodule of
T\otimes T
, then
\mathfrak{C}(T,\check{R})
is a coalgebra in the category
{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{0}
.

Theorem 5.10
.

Let
\mathscr{W}_{1}
be an equivariant first-order codifferential calculus such that its quantum tangent space
T
is an object in
{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{0}
, and let
\check{R}
be as in Proposition
5.9
. Then

\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}\cong U\otimes_{H}\mathfrak{C}(T,\check{R})

as coalgebras in
{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
.

Proof.

For simplicity, assume that
\mathscr{W}_{1}=U\otimes_{H}T
. First we show that
{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}\cong\mathfrak{C}(T,\check{R})
as objects in
{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{C}
. In degree zero and one, it is immediate that
{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}\mathscr{W}_{0}^{\mathrm{max}}={}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}C\cong k
, and
{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}\mathscr{W}_{1}^{\mathrm{max}}={}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}(U\otimes_{H}T)\cong T
. Hence, these coinvariant subspaces agree with
\mathfrak{C}^{(0)}(T,\check{R})
, respectively
\mathfrak{C}^{(1)}(T,\check{R})
. For
n\geq 2
, combining Proposition
5.9
with the definition of
\mathscr{W}_{n}^{\mathrm{max}}
, given by (
11
), and Lemma
4.9
we get

\displaystyle{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}(\mathscr{W}_{n}^{\mathrm{max}})

\displaystyle={}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}\left(\bigcap_{i+j+2=n}(U\otimes_{H}T)^{\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pti}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt(U\otimes_{H}\check{R})\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt(U\otimes_{H}T)^{\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptj}\right)

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{i+j+2=n}{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}\left((U\otimes_{H}T)^{\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pti}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt(U\otimes_{H}\check{R})\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt(U\otimes_{H}T)^{\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptj}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\bigcap_{i+j+2=n}T^{\otimes i}\otimes\check{R}\otimes T^{\otimes j}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{C}^{(n)}(T,\check{R}).

This shows that
{}^{\text{co C}}\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}\cong\mathfrak{C}(T,\check{R})
as objects in
{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{C}
, and thus by Theorem
4.8

\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}\cong U\otimes_{H}\mathfrak{C}(T,\check{R})
as objects in
{}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
. Lastly, this isomorphism is also an isomorphism of coalgebras, since the comultiplication on
{}^{\mathrm{co}\hskip 2.0ptC}\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}
induced by
\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}
is simply given by deconcatenation of tensors, which agrees with the comultiplication on
\mathfrak{C}(T,\check{R})
.
∎

6.
Relation to differential calculi and Cartan pairs

In this section we discuss how first-order codifferential calculi relate to other types of algebraically defined differential structures.

6.1.
Correspondence between first-order differential- and codifferential calculi

Let
C
be a coalgebra and fix a subalgebra
A\subseteq C^{\ast}
of the dual algebra of
C
. Recall that every first-order differential calculus on
A
is a quotient of the universal one
\Omega_{u}^{1}
, which is given as the kernel of the multiplication map
\mu\colon A\otimes A\rightarrow A
with differential given by
d_{u}(a)=1\otimes a-a\otimes 1

[
42
]
. We use this observation to define a first-order differential calculus on
A
in terms of a subbimodule of
\Omega_{u}^{1}
determined by a first-order codifferential calculus on
C
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\mathscr{W}_{1}
be a first-order codifferential calculus on
C
, then the subspace

N^{(1)}:=\mathrm{span}_{k}\{ad_{u}(b)\mid a(w_{(-1)})b(\delta(w_{(0)}))=0\text{ for all }w\in\mathscr{W}_{1}\}

is sub bimodule. In particular, we obtain a first-order differential calculus
\Omega^{1}:=\Omega_{u}^{1}/N^{(1)}
, determined by
\mathscr{W}_{1}
.

Proof.

Let
ad_{u}(b)\in N^{(1)},c\in A
and
w\in\mathscr{W}_{1}
then

\displaystyle(ca)(w_{(-1)})b(\delta(w_{(0)}))

\displaystyle=c(w_{(-2)})a(w_{(-1)})b(\delta(w_{(0)}))=0

and thus,
cad_{u}(b)\in N^{(1)}
. Observe that, since
d_{u}
is a derivation

ad_{u}(b)c=ad_{u}(bc)-abd_{u}(c)

and since
\delta
is a coderivation

\displaystyle\phantom{=}a(w_{(-1)})(bc)(\delta(w_{(0)}))-(ab)(w_{(-1)})c(\delta(w_{(0)}))

\displaystyle=a(w_{(-1)})b(\delta(w_{(0)})_{(1)})c(\delta(w_{0})_{(2)})-a(w_{(-2)})b(w_{(-1)})c(\delta(w_{(0)}))

\displaystyle=a(w_{(-2)})b(w_{(-1)})c(\delta(w_{(0)}))+a(w_{(-1)})b(\delta(w_{(0)}))c(w_{(1)})-a(w_{(-2)})b(w_{(-1)})c(\delta(w_{(0)}))

\displaystyle=0.

Thus, also
ad_{u}(b)c\in N^{(1)}
.
∎

The resulting first-order differential calculus
\Omega^{1}=\Omega_{u}^{1}/N^{(1)}
pairs in a canonical way with
\mathscr{W}_{1}
:

\langle-,-\rangle\colon\Omega^{1}\times\mathscr{W}_{1}\rightarrow k,\quad\langle ad(b),w\rangle=a(w_{(-1)})b(\delta(w_{(0)}))

where
d
denotes the differential of
\Omega^{1}
.
Moreover, this pairing has the following properties

(21)

\langle a\omega,w\rangle=a(w_{(-1)})\langle\omega,w_{(0)}\rangle,\quad\langle\omega a,w\rangle=\langle\omega,w_{(0)}\rangle a(w_{(1)}),\quad\langle d(a),w\rangle=a(\delta(w)).

Definition 6.2
.

Let
M\in{}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
and
\mathscr{F}\in{}_{A}\mathcal{M}_{A}
. We call pairing
\langle-,-,\rangle\colon\mathcal{F}\times M\rightarrow k
a
bimodule pairing
if it satisfies the first two identities in (
21
).

By considering the pairing between the corresponding universal first-order calculi, we can also pass from first-order differential calculi to first-order codifferential calculi.

Proposition 6.3
.

Assume that
A
separates the elements of
C
(for instance, if
A=C^{\ast}
). Let
N^{(1)}\subseteq\Omega_{u}^{1}
be a sub bicomodule. Then
\mathscr{W}_{1}:=(N^{(1)})^{\perp}
is a sub-bicomodule of
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}
. In particular, it is a first-order codifferential calculus with codifferential given by the restriction of
\delta^{u}
.

Proof.

Let
w\in\mathscr{W}_{1}
. We have to show that
w_{(-1)}\otimes w_{(0)}\in C\otimes\mathscr{W}_{1}
and
w_{(0)}\otimes w_{(1)}\in\mathscr{W}_{1}\otimes C
. Since
A
separates the elements of
C
by assumption, this is equivalent to
c(w_{(-1)})w_{(0)},c(w_{(1)})w_{(0)}\in\mathscr{W}_{1}
for all
c\in A
. Indeed, for any
c\in A
and
\sum_{i}a_{i}d_{u}(b_{i})\in N^{(1)}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\langle c(w_{(-1)})a_{i}d_{u}(b_{i}),w_{(0)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}c(w_{(-2)})a_{i}(w_{(-1)})b_{i}(\delta(w_{(0)}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}(ca_{i})(w_{(-1)})b_{i}(\delta(w_{(0)}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\langle ca_{i}d_{u}(b_{i}),w\rangle

\displaystyle=0

using that
N^{(1)}
is a sub bimodule. Moreover, using that
\delta
is a coderivation

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\langle c(w_{(1)})a_{i}d_{u}(b_{i}),w_{(0)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}a_{i}(w_{(-1)})b_{i}(\delta(w_{(0)}))c(w_{(1)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}(a_{i}(w_{(-1)})b_{i}(\delta(w_{(0)})_{(1)})c(\delta(w_{(0)})_{(2)})

\displaystyle\phantom{==\sum_{i}}-a_{i}(w_{(-2)})b_{i}(w_{(-1)})c(\delta(w_{(0)})))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\langle a_{i}d_{u}(b_{i}c)-a_{i}b_{i}d_{u}(c),w\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\langle a_{i}d_{u}(b_{i})c,w\rangle

\displaystyle=0

∎

As a consequence of Proposition
6.3
, we can make the following observation: First note the categories of first-order (co)differential calculi are equivalent to the poset categories of sub
A
-bimodules, respectively sub
C
-bicomodules. Next, if we assume that
A
separates the elements of
C
, then taking orthogonal complements gives functors between those poset categories, that form a Galois connection (see
[
29
, IV.5 Theorem 1]
). In particular, the constructions in Lemma
6.1
and Proposition
6.3
give rise to a pair of adjoint functors.

6.2.
The codifferential prolongation

We fix a coalgebra
C
, a subalgebra
A\subseteq C^{\ast}
, and a first-order codifferential calculus
\mathscr{W}_{1}
. We let
\Omega^{1}:=\Omega_{u}^{1}/N^{(1)}
denote the first-order differential calculus corresponding to
\mathscr{W}_{1}
as in Lemma
6.1
.

In this section, we present a construction which extends the first-order differential calculus
\Omega^{1}
to a full differential calculus, incorporating the maximal prolongation of
\mathscr{W}_{1}
. Firstly, note that we have a canonical bimodule pairing between
(\Omega^{1})^{\otimes_{A}n}
and
(\mathscr{W}_{1})^{\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptn}
for all
n\geq 0
. For
n=0
, we use the pairing
A\times C\rightarrow k,\langle a,c\rangle=a(c)
, and for
n\geq 2
, the pairing is constructed using the following:

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\in{}_{A}\mathcal{M}_{A},M,N\in{}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{C}
, and let

\langle-,-\rangle_{1}\colon\mathcal{F}\times M\rightarrow k,\quad\langle-,-\rangle_{2}\colon\mathcal{G}\times N\rightarrow k

be bimodule pairings. Then

\langle-,-\rangle\colon(\mathcal{F}\otimes_{A}\mathcal{G})\times(M\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptN)\rightarrow k,\quad(f\otimes_{A}g,m\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptn)\mapsto\langle f,m\rangle_{1}\langle g,n\rangle_{2}.

Is a bimodule pairing.

Proof.

For any
f\in\mathcal{F},g\in\mathcal{G}
,
m\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptn\in M\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptN
and
a\in A
, we have by the properties of cotensor products, and (
21
)

\displaystyle\langle fa,m\rangle_{1}\langle g,n\rangle_{2}

\displaystyle=\langle f,m_{(0)}\rangle_{1}a(m_{(1)})\langle g,n\rangle_{2}=\langle f,m\rangle_{1}a(n_{(-1)})\langle g,n_{(0)}\rangle_{2}

\displaystyle=\langle f,m\rangle_{1}\langle ag,n\rangle_{2}.

This shows that the pairing is well-defined. The fact that it is a bimodule pairing follows directly from the definition of the
A
-actions on
\mathcal{F}\otimes_{A}\mathcal{G}
, the
C
-coactions on
M\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptN
.
∎

We use the pairing defined in Lemma
6.4
to define an ideal of
T_{A}(\Omega^{1})
. For each
n\geq 2
consider the following subbimodule of
(\Omega^{1})^{\otimes_{A}n}
(the fact that the space defined below is indeed a subbimodule follows since the given pairing is a bimodule pairing)

N_{\text{codiff}}^{(n)}:=(\mathscr{W}_{n}^{\mathrm{max}})^{\perp}.

If we let
\Omega_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{(n)}:=(\Omega^{1})^{\otimes_{A}n}/N_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{(n)}
, we get an induced pairing

(22)

\Omega_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{(n)}\times\mathscr{W}_{n}^{\mathrm{max}}\rightarrow k.

Note that with this choice,
\mathscr{W}_{n}^{\mathrm{max}}
separates the elements of
\Omega_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{(n)}
.

Theorem 6.5
.

Let
N_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{\bullet}:=\bigoplus_{n\geq 2}N_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{(n)}
. Then
N_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{\bullet}
is a two-sided ideal of
T_{A}(\Omega^{1})
and the resulting quotient algebra
\Omega_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{\bullet}
is a differential calculus over
A
with differential determined by the equation

(23)

\langle d(\omega),w\rangle=\langle\omega,\delta(w)\rangle

Where
\omega\in(\Omega^{1})^{\otimes_{A}n}/N_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{(n)}
and
w\in\mathscr{W}_{n}^{\mathrm{max}}
with
n\geq 0
.
Moreover, the extension of the pairing (
22
)
\Omega_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{\bullet}\times\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}\rightarrow k
is compatible with the algebra and coalgebra structures, in the sense that

(24)

\langle\omega\eta,w\rangle=\langle\omega,w_{(1)}\rangle\langle\eta,w_{(2)}\rangle

for all
\omega,\eta\in\Omega_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{\bullet}
and
w\in\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}
.

Proof.

We first argue that (
24
) holds for the pairing between
T_{A}(\Omega^{1})
and
T_{C}^{c}(\mathscr{W}_{1})
induced by Lemma
6.4
. Let
\omega\in(\Omega^{1})^{\otimes_{A}m},\eta\in(\Omega^{1})^{\otimes_{A}n}
, and
w\in\mathscr{W}_{1}^{\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptl}
for
m,n,l\geq 0
. Note that (
24
) holds if
l\neq m+n
. Indeed, in this case both sides of the equation are zero. For the right-hand side this follows directly, since the pairing is only non-zero for elements of the same degree, and for the left-hand side recall that the coproduct of
w
can be written as

w_{(1)}\otimes w_{(2)}=\sum_{i=0}^{l}w_{(1)}^{l-i}\otimes w_{(2)}^{i}

where
w_{(1)}^{l-i}\otimes w_{(2)}^{i}\in\mathscr{W}_{1}^{\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptl-i}\otimes\mathscr{W}_{1}^{\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pti}
for
0\leq i\leq l
. Thus, let
l=m+n
if
m=0
, then
\omega=a
for some
a\in A
and since
\langle-,-\rangle
is a bimodule pairing

\langle a\eta,w\rangle=\langle a,w_{(-1)}\rangle\langle\eta,w_{(0)}\rangle=\langle a,w_{(1)}^{0}\rangle\langle\eta,w_{(2)}^{n}\rangle=\langle a,w_{(1)}\rangle\langle\eta,w_{(2)}\rangle.

By symmetry, we also get (
24
) if
n=0
. Thus let
m,n>0
. We can assume without loss of generality, that
\omega=\omega_{1}\otimes_{A}\dots\otimes_{A}\omega_{m}
and
\eta=\omega_{m+1}\otimes_{A}\dots\otimes_{A}\omega_{m+n}
, and if we write
w=w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{m+n}
, we get

\displaystyle\langle\omega\eta,w\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\omega_{1},w_{1}\rangle\dots\langle\omega_{m+n},w_{m+n}\rangle=\langle\omega,w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{m}\rangle\langle\eta,w_{m+1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{m+n}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\omega,w_{(1)}^{m}\rangle\langle\eta,w_{(2)}^{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\omega,w_{(1)}\rangle\langle\eta,w_{(2)}\rangle.

Next, we show that
N_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{\bullet}
is a two-sided ideal. Indeed, by construction of
\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}
(see (
11
)), we have that
\mathscr{W}_{n+1}\subseteq\mathscr{W}_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\mathscr{W}_{n}^{\mathrm{max}},\mathscr{W}_{n}^{\mathrm{max}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\mathscr{W}_{1}
for all
n\geq 2
. Thus,
N_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{\bullet}
is closed under left- and right multiplication with generators of
T_{A}(\Omega^{1})
, and therefore it is a two-sided ideal. Note also, that (
24
) still holds for the induced pairing between
\Omega_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{\bullet}
and
\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}
.

We are left with showing that
\Omega_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{\bullet}
admits a differential. We will argue as follows: First we show that
N_{\mathrm{max}}^{(2)}\subseteq N_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{(2)}
, where

N_{\mathrm{max}}^{(2)}=\mathrm{span}_{A\otimes A^{op}}\{d(a)\otimes_{A}d(b)\mid ad_{u}(b)\in N^{(1)}\}

This will imply that
\Omega_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{\bullet}
is a quotient algebra of the maximal prolongation of
\Omega^{1}
which is given by
\Omega_{\mathrm{max}}^{\bullet}=T_{A}(\Omega^{1})/\langle N_{\mathrm{max}}^{(2)}\rangle
(see for instance
[
3
, Lemma 1.32]
). We will then show that the image of
N_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{\bullet}
in
\Omega_{\mathrm{max}}^{\bullet}
is a differential ideal, and that the induced differential on
\Omega_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{\bullet}
satisfies (
23
). Let
ad_{u}(b)\in N^{(1)}
and
w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}\in\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}
. Then by definition of
\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}
, we have
[\delta(w_{1})\otimes\delta(w_{1})]=[w_{(-1)}\otimes\delta(w_{(0)})]
for some
