w\in\mathscr{W}_{1}
, and thus

\displaystyle\langle d(a)\otimes_{A}d(b),w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle d(a),w_{1}\rangle\langle d(b),w_{2}\rangle=\langle ad_{u}(b),[\delta(w_{1})\otimes\delta(w_{2})]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle ad_{u}(b),[w_{(-1)}\otimes\delta(w_{(0)})]\rangle

\displaystyle=a(w_{(-1)})b(\delta(w_{(0)}))

\displaystyle=0

Where the last equality holds by definition of
N^{(1)}
(see Lemma
6.1
). It follows that
\Omega_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{\bullet}
is a quotient of
\Omega_{\mathrm{max}}^{\bullet}
. In particular, we get a well-defined pairing
\Omega_{\mathrm{max}}^{\bullet}\times\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}
which moreover satisfies (
24
). We show that this pairing satisfies (
23
) by induction on the degree
n
of a homogeneous element
\omega\in\Omega_{\mathrm{max}}^{n}
. For
n=0
, this follows from (
21
). For
n=1
, we have for all
ad(b)\in\Omega^{1},w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}\in\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}
and with
w:=\delta_{2}(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2})

\displaystyle\langle d(ad(b)),w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle d(a)\wedge d(b),w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}\rangle=\langle d(a),w_{1}\rangle\langle d(b),w_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=a(\delta(w_{1}))b(\delta(w_{2}))

\displaystyle=\langle ad_{u}(b),[\delta(w_{1})\otimes\delta(w_{2})]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle ad_{u}(b),[w_{(-1)}\otimes\delta(w_{(0)})]\rangle

\displaystyle=a(w_{(-1)})b(\delta(w_{(0)}))

\displaystyle=\langle ad(b),\delta(w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2})\rangle

Where we denoted by
-\wedge-
the product of
\Omega_{\mathrm{max}}^{\bullet}
. Now let
n\geq 2
and let
\omega\in\Omega^{1},\eta\in\Omega_{\mathrm{max}}^{n-1}
and
w\in\mathscr{W}_{n+1}
. Then using induction and (
8
) we get

\displaystyle\langle d(\omega\wedge\eta),w\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle d(\omega)\wedge\eta,w\rangle-\langle\omega\wedge d(\eta),w\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle d(\omega),w_{(1)}\rangle\langle\eta,w_{(2)}\rangle-\langle\omega,w_{(1)}\rangle\langle d(\eta),w_{(2)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle d(\omega),w_{(1)}^{2}\rangle\langle\eta,w_{(2)}^{n-1}\rangle-\langle\omega,w_{(1)}^{1}\rangle\langle d(\eta),w_{(2)}^{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\omega,\delta(w_{(1)}^{2})\rangle\langle\eta,w_{(2)}^{n-1}\rangle-\langle\omega,w_{(1)}^{1}\rangle\langle\eta,\delta(w_{(2)}^{n})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\omega,\delta(w)_{(1)}^{1}\rangle\langle\eta,\delta(w)_{(2)}^{n-1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\omega\wedge\eta,\delta(w)\rangle.

From the assertion that (
23
) holds for
\Omega_{\mathrm{max}}^{\bullet}
and
\mathscr{W}_{\bullet}^{\mathrm{max}}
, it follows immediately that the differential on
\Omega_{\mathrm{max}}^{\bullet}
descends to
\Omega_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{\bullet}
and that (
23
) also holds for
\Omega_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{\bullet}
.
∎

Definition 6.6
.

We call the differential calculus
\Omega_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{\bullet}
constructed in Theorem
6.5
the
codifferential prolongation
of
\Omega^{1}
.

6.3.
Differential calculi over quantum homogeneous spaces arising as finitary duals of coalgebras

It is natural to ask whether the codifferential prolongation of a first-order differential calculus coming from a first-order codifferential calculus agrees with the maximal prolongation. In this section we will give a sufficient condition in the framework of quantum homogeneous spaces.

We let
U
be a Hopf algebra,
H\subseteq U
a right coideal subalgebra,
C:=U\otimes_{H}k
, as well as
A:=U_{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
for a tensor category
\mathcal{C}
of finite dimensional
U
-modules which is closed under taking duals.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
\mathscr{W}_{1}
be a
U
-equivariant first-order codifferential calculus over
C
, then the pairing

\Omega_{u}^{1}\times\mathscr{W}_{1}\rightarrow k,\quad\langle ad_{u}(b),w\rangle=a(w_{(-1)})b(\delta(w_{(0)}))

where
\Omega_{u}^{1}
is the universal first-order differential calculus over
B
, is admissible (see Definition
4.13
). Moreover, the first-order differential calculus
\Omega^{1}=\Omega_{u}^{1}/\mathscr{W}_{1}^{\perp}
is
A
-covariant.

Proof.

First note that the universal first-order differential calculus is
\Omega_{u}^{1}
is
A
-covariant. In particular, we have
\Omega_{u}^{1}\in{}_{B}^{A}\mathcal{M}_{B}
. We have already seen that the above is a bimodule pairing (
21
). Let
X\in U,a,b\in B
and
w\in\mathscr{W}_{1}
, then

\displaystyle\langle ad_{u}(b),Xw\rangle

\displaystyle=a((Xw)_{(-1)})b(\delta((Xw)_{(0)}))=a(X_{(1)}w_{(-1)})b(X_{(2)}\delta(w_{(0)}))

\displaystyle=a_{(1)}(X_{(1)})b_{(1)}(X_{(2)})a_{(2)}(w_{(-1)})b_{(2)}(\delta(w_{(0)}))

\displaystyle=a_{(1)}b_{(1)}(X)\langle a_{(2)}d_{u}(b_{(2)})w\rangle.

This shows admissibility, recalling that the left
A
-coaction on
\Omega_{u}^{1}
can be written as
ad_{u}(b)\mapsto a_{(1)}b_{(1)}\otimes a_{(2)}d_{u}(b_{(2)})
. By Lemma
4.15
the orthogonal complement of
\mathscr{W}_{1}
is a subobject of
\Omega_{u}^{1}
and thus
\Omega^{1}\in{}_{B}^{A}\mathcal{M}_{B}
as a quotient object.
∎

In order to proceed, we use the classification results for first-order (co)differential calculi. On this note we also assume for the rest of this section that
U
is faithfully flat as a left and right
H
-module, and that
A
is faithfully flat as a right
B
-module (a criterion for the latter is given in Theorem
4.11
). Recall that by
[
24
, Theorem 2]
any
A
-covariant first-order differential calculus
\Omega^{1}
is isomorphic to one of the form
A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptB^{+}/I
for some subobject
I\subseteq B^{+}
, where
B^{+}
is an object in
{}^{\pi_{B}}\mathcal{M}_{B}
via right multiplication and
\pi_{B}(A)
-coaction given by

b\mapsto\pi_{B}(b_{(1)})\otimes b_{(2)}^{+}.

The differential of
A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptB^{+}/I
is given by

B\rightarrow A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptB^{+}/I,\quad b\mapsto b_{(1)}\otimes p(b_{(2)}^{+})

where
p\colon B^{+}\rightarrow B^{+}/I
denotes the canonical projection. For more details on the construction consult
[
24
]
.
For the universal first-order differential calculus
\Omega_{u}^{1}
, we have an explicit isomorphism

A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptB^{+}\rightarrow\Omega_{u}^{1},\quad a\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptb\mapsto aS(b_{(1)})d_{u}(b_{(2)}).

Indeed, the above map is an isomorphism of first-order differential calculus, which follows by checking that it is inverse to the canonical map

\Omega_{u}^{1}\rightarrow A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptB^{+},\quad ad_{u}(b)\mapsto ad(b)=ab_{(1)}\otimes b_{(2)}^{+}.

Under this identification, the pairing between
\Omega_{u}^{1}
and a
U
-equivariant first-order codifferential calculus
\mathscr{W}_{1}
, such that
\mathscr{W}_{1}\cong U\otimes_{H}T
for some quantum tangent space
T\subseteq C^{+}
takes the form

\displaystyle\langle a\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptb,X\otimes_{H}t\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle aS(b_{(1)})d_{u}(b_{(2)}),X\otimes_{H}t\rangle=(aS(b_{(1)}))(X_{(1)})b_{(2)}(X_{(2)}t)

\displaystyle=a(X_{(1)})(S(b_{(1)}))(X_{(2)})b_{(2)}(X_{(3)}t)=a(X_{(1)})b_{(1)}(S(X_{(2)}))b_{(2)}(X_{(3)}t)

\displaystyle=a(X_{(1)})b(S(X_{(2)})X_{(3)}t)=a(X)b(t)

where
a\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptb\in A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptB^{+},X\in U
and
t\in T
. Thus, the pairing
A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptB^{+}\times U\otimes_{H}T\rightarrow k
is induced by the pairing
B^{+}\otimes T\rightarrow k
in the sense of Proposition
4.14
. Using Lemma
4.16
we can write
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{\perp}=A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptI^{(1)}
with
I^{(1)}:=T^{\perp}
, and the induced first-order differential calculus takes the form
\Omega^{1}=A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptB^{+}/I^{(1)}
.

The following gives a criterion for when it is possible to recover
T
from
I^{(1)}
.

Lemma 6.8
.

In the situation above, assume that
B
separates the elements of
C
, and that the quantum tangent space
T
is finite dimensional. Then
T=(I^{(1)})^{\perp}
, where the orthogonal complement is taken with respect to the extended pairing
B^{+}\times C^{+}\rightarrow k
. In particular

B^{+}/I^{(1)}\times T\rightarrow k,\quad\langle b+I^{(1)},t\rangle=b(t)

is a non-degenerate pairing between finite dimensional vector space.

Proof.

Let
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}
be a basis of
T
. Since
B
separates the points of
C
, we can find
b^{1},\dots,b^{N}\in B^{+}
such that
b^{i}(t_{j})=\delta_{ij}
for all
1\leq i,j\leq N
. Note that
b^{1}+I^{(1)},\dots,b^{N}+I^{(1)}
is a dual basis of
t_{1},\dots,t_{N}
with respect to the pairing
B^{+}/I^{(1)}\times T\rightarrow k
. Suppose there is some element
s\in(I^{(1)})^{\perp}\setminus T
. Then the set
t_{1},\dots,t_{N},s
is linear independent, and thus

\tilde{s}:=s-\sum_{i=1}^{N}b^{i}(s)t_{i}

is non-zero. However, we still have
\tilde{s}\in(I^{(1)})^{\perp}
, and also

b^{j}(\tilde{s})=b^{j}(s)-b^{j}(s)=0

for all
1\leq j\leq N
. In other words, the element
\tilde{s}
is annihilated by all
b\in B^{+}
, which contradicts the assumption that
B
separates the points of
C
.
∎

If we are in the case where
H
is a sub bialgebra of
U
and
T\in{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{0}
(see the discussion around Proposition
4.9
), then the codifferential prolongation of
\Omega^{1}
indeed agrees with its maximal prolongation.

Theorem 6.9
.

Assume that
H
is a sub bialgebra of
U
, that the antipode of
A
is invertible, and that
B
separates the elements of
C
. Let
T\subseteq C^{+}
be a finite dimensional quantum tangent space such that
T\in{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{0}
, let
\mathscr{W}_{1}:=U\otimes_{H}T
be the corresponding first-order codifferential calculus, and let
\Omega^{1}=\Omega_{u}^{1}/\mathscr{W}_{1}^{\perp}
be the corresponding first-order differential calculus. Then we have
\Omega_{\mathrm{max}}^{\bullet}=\Omega_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{\bullet}
.

Proof.

Recall that the codifferential prolongation of
\Omega^{1}
is given by

\Omega_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{\bullet}=B\oplus\Omega^{1}\oplus\bigoplus_{n\geq 2}(\Omega^{1})^{\otimes_{B}n}/N_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{(n)}

where
N_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{(n)}
is the orthogonal complement of
\mathscr{W}_{n}^{\mathrm{max}}
with respect to the pairing

(\Omega^{1})^{\otimes_{B}n}\times(\mathscr{W}_{1})^{\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptn}\rightarrow k.

Thus, we have to check that

N_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{(n)}=N_{\mathrm{max}}^{(n)}=\sum_{i+j+2=n}(\Omega^{1})^{\otimes_{B}i}\otimes_{B}N_{\mathrm{max}}^{(2)}\otimes_{B}(\Omega^{1})^{\otimes_{B}j}

for all
n\geq 2
. Since
T\in{}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{0}
, we also have
V:=B^{+}/I^{(1)}\in{}^{\pi_{B}}\mathcal{M}_{0}
, and we can apply the monoidal equivalences between
({}_{U}^{C}\mathcal{M}^{0},\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt,C)
and
({}_{H}\mathcal{M}^{0},\otimes,k)
, as well as
({}_{B}^{A}\mathcal{M}_{0},\otimes_{B},B)
and
({}^{\pi_{B}}\mathcal{M}_{0},\otimes,k)
(see Proposition
4.4
and Proposition
4.9
). In particular, since
\Omega^{1}\cong A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptV
we have identifications
(\Omega^{1})^{\otimes_{B}n}\cong(A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptV)^{\otimes_{B}n}\cong A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptV^{\otimes n}
and
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptn}\cong U\otimes_{H}T^{\otimes n}
. Under these identifications, the induced pairing
\langle-,-\rangle^{\flat}\colon V^{\otimes n}\times T^{\otimes n}\rightarrow k
(in the sense of Proposition
4.14
) can be written as

\langle v^{1}\otimes\dots\otimes v^{n},t_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes t_{n}\rangle^{\flat}=\langle v^{1},t_{1}\rangle\dots\langle v^{n},t_{n}\rangle.

Indeed, using the strong monoidal structure of
\Phi
(Proposition
4.4
) and the counit of the adjunction
\Phi\dashv\Psi
(Theorem
4.1
), we obtain an isomorphism

\displaystyle\Phi((A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptV)^{\otimes_{B}n})\rightarrow V^{\otimes n},

\displaystyle(a^{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptv^{1})\otimes_{B}\dots\otimes_{B}(a^{n}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptv^{n})+B^{+}(A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptV)\mapsto\varepsilon(a^{1})v^{1}\otimes\dots\otimes\varepsilon(a^{n})v^{n}

and if we write
v^{i}=b^{i}+I^{(1)}
for suitable
b^{i}\in B^{+}
, then the preimage of
v^{1}\otimes\dots\otimes v^{n}
is given by
(b_{(1)}^{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0pt((b_{(2)}^{1})^{+}+I^{(1)}))\otimes_{B}\dots\otimes_{B}(b_{(1)}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0pt((b_{(2)}^{n})^{+}+I^{(1)}))
. It follows now

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\langle(b_{(1)}^{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0pt((b_{(2)}^{1})^{+}+I^{(1)}))\otimes_{B}\dots\otimes_{B}(b_{(1)}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0pt((b_{(2)}^{n})^{+}+I^{(1)})),

\displaystyle\hskip 256.0748pt(1\otimes_{H}t_{1})\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt\dots\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0pt(1\otimes_{H}t_{n})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle b_{(1)}^{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0pt((b_{(2)}^{1})^{+}+I^{(1)}),1\otimes_{H}t_{1}\rangle\dots\langle b_{(1)}^{n}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0pt((b_{(2)}^{n})^{+}+I^{(1)}),1\otimes_{H}t_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=b_{(1)}^{1}(1)\langle(b_{(2)}^{1})^{+}+I^{(1)},t_{1}\rangle\dots b_{(1)}^{n}(1)\langle(b_{(2)}^{n})^{+}+I^{(1)},t_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle v^{1},t_{1}\rangle\dots\langle v^{n},t_{n}\rangle.

In particular, by Lemma
6.8
, the pairing between
V^{\otimes n}
and
T^{\otimes n}
is non-degenerate. We proceed by showing that
N_{\mathrm{max}}^{(2)}=N_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{(2)}
. Let
w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}\in(N_{\mathrm{max}}^{(2)})^{\perp}
, then for every
ad_{u}(b)\in N^{(1)}

\displaystyle\langle ad_{u}(b),[\delta(w_{1})\otimes\delta(w_{2})]\rangle

\displaystyle=a(\delta(w_{1}))b(\delta(w_{2}))=\langle d(a),w_{1}\rangle\langle d(b),w_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle d(a)\otimes_{B}d(b),w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=0.

Thus, we have
[\delta(w_{1})\otimes\delta(w_{2})]\in(N^{(1)})^{\perp}=\mathscr{W}_{1}^{\perp\perp}
, where orthogonal complements are taken with respect to the pairing
\Omega_{u}^{1}\times\mathscr{W}_{1}^{u}\rightarrow k
. By identifying
\Omega_{u}^{1}
with
A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptB^{+}
we get, using Lemma
6.8
and Lemma
4.15

\mathscr{W}_{1}^{\perp\perp}=(A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptI^{(1)})^{\perp}=U\otimes_{H}(I^{(1)})^{\perp}=U\otimes_{H}T.

This implies that
[\delta(w_{1})\otimes\delta(w_{2})]\in\mathscr{W}_{1}
and thus
w_{1}\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{C}\hskip 1.0ptw_{2}\in\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}
. Since
N_{\mathrm{max}}^{(2)}\subseteq N_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{(2)}
we also have

(\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}})^{\perp\perp}=(N_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{(2)})^{\perp}\subseteq(N_{\mathrm{max}}^{(2)})^{\perp}\subseteq\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}

and since
\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}\subseteq(\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}})^{\perp\perp}
all the above inclusions are equalities, which intern implies
N_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{(2)}=N_{\mathrm{max}}^{(2)}
using the fact that the induced pairing
V\otimes V\times T\otimes T\rightarrow k
is a non-degenerate pairing between finite dimensional vector spaces and Lemma
4.15
. For the higher degrees, we first observe that we can identify
\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}
with
U\otimes_{H}\check{R}
for some subspace
\check{R}\subseteq T\otimes T
and
N_{\mathrm{max}}^{(2)}=A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptR
with
R=\check{R}^{\perp}
again using Lemma
4.16
. We then have for arbitrary
n\geq 2

\mathscr{W}_{n}^{\mathrm{max}}\cong U\otimes_{H}\bigcap_{i+j+2=n}T^{\otimes i}\otimes\check{R}\otimes T^{\otimes j}.

Thus, we can identify
N_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{(n)}=(\mathscr{W}_{n}^{\mathrm{max}})^{\perp}
with

A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0pt\left(\bigcap_{i+j+2=n}T^{\otimes i}\otimes\check{R}\otimes T^{\otimes j}\right)^{\perp}=A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0pt\left(\sum_{i+j+2=n}V^{\otimes i}\otimes R\otimes V^{\otimes j}\right).

The right-hand side is precisely the image of
N_{\mathrm{max}}^{(n)}
under the isomorphism
(\Omega^{1})^{\otimes_{B}n}\cong A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0ptV^{\otimes n}
, and we conclude that also
N_{\mathrm{max}}^{(n)}=N_{\mathrm{codiff}}^{(n)}
.
∎

The proof of the above theorem gives an explicit description of the maximal prolongation of
\Omega^{1}
, which conversely allows us to recover the maximal prolongation of
\mathscr{W}_{1}
if we happen to know
\Omega_{\mathrm{max}}^{\bullet}
.

Corollary 6.10
.

In the situation of Theorem
6.9
we have

\Omega_{\mathrm{max}}^{\bullet}\cong A\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{B}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathfrak{A}(V,\check{R}^{\perp}).

Here,
\check{R}
is the subobject of
T\otimes T
such that
\mathscr{W}_{2}^{\mathrm{max}}\cong U\otimes_{H}\check{R}
, and
\mathfrak{A}(V,\check{R}^{\perp})
is the quadratic dual algebra of
\mathfrak{C}(T,\check{R})
.

6.4.
Cartan pairs associated to first-order codifferential calculi

Besides first-order differential calculi there is also the notion of a Cartan pair, which serves as a replacement for vector fields in noncommutative geometry. The latter concept is due to Borowiec
[
7
,
9
]
. In this section we show that a first-order codifferential calculus gives rise to a Cartan pair.

Definition 6.11
.

A right
Cartan pair
over a
k
-algebra
A
is a pair
(\mathscr{X}^{1},\mathscr{L})
consisting of an
A
-bimodule
\mathscr{X}^{1}
and a linear map
\mathscr{L}\colon\mathscr{X}^{1}\rightarrow\mathrm{End}_{k}(A),X\mapsto\mathscr{L}_{X}
such that
\mathscr{L}
is injective and

(25)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{aX}(b)=a\mathscr{L}_{X}(b)

(26)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{X}(ab)=\mathscr{L}_{X}(a)b+\mathscr{L}_{Xa}(b)

for all
X\in\mathscr{X}^{1}
and
a,b\in A
.

Similarly, one defines left Cartan pairs, replacing (
25
) by

(27)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{Xa}(b)=\mathscr{L}_{X}(b)a

(28)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{X}(ab)=a\mathscr{L}_{X}(b)+\mathscr{L}_{bX}(a).

In
[
7
]
the map
\mathscr{L}
is referred to as an action. However, motivated by differential geometry, we find it more fitting to call the latter an anchor morphism as in anchor morphism of a Lie algebroid, or more generally, Lie–Rinehart algebra
[
25
]
.

Let
C
be a coalgebra and let
A:=C^{\ast}
be its dual algebra. Then a first-order codifferential calculus on
C
gives rise to a Cartan pair as follows:

Proposition 6.12
.

Let
\mathscr{W}_{1}
be a first-order codifferential calculus over
C
with differential
\delta
. Let

\mathscr{X}^{1}:=\mathrm{Hom}^{C}(C,\mathscr{W}_{1})

denote the space of right
C
-colinear maps from
C
to
\mathscr{W}_{1}
. Define left- and right
A
-action on
\mathscr{X}^{1}
via

(29)

(a\triangleright X)(c)=a(c_{(1)})X(c_{(2)})

(30)

(X\triangleleft a)(c)=a(X(c)_{(-1)})X(c)_{(0)}

for
a\in A,X\in\mathscr{W}_{1}
and
c\in C
. Additionally, we define an anchor morphism
\mathscr{L}\colon\mathscr{X}^{1}\rightarrow\mathrm{End}(A)

(31)

\mathscr{L}_{X}(a)(c)=a(\delta(X(c)))

with
a\in A,X\in\mathscr{X}^{1}
and
c\in C
. The pair
(\mathscr{X}^{1},\mathscr{L})
is a Cartan pair.

Proof.

We first show that (
29
) and (
30
) are respectively left- and right actions. Indeed, let
X\in\mathscr{X}^{1},a,b\in A
and
c\in C
, then

(1\triangleright X)(c)=\varepsilon(c_{(1)})X(c_{(2)})=X(c)

\displaystyle(a\triangleright(b\triangleright X))(c)=a(c_{(1)})b(c_{(2)})X(c_{(3)})=(ab)(c_{(1)})X(c_{(2)})=(ab\triangleright X)(c)

and also

(X\triangleleft 1)(c)=\varepsilon(X(c)_{(-1)})X(c)_{(0)}=X(c)

\displaystyle((X\triangleleft a)\triangleleft b)(c)

\displaystyle=b((X\triangleleft a)(c)_{(-1)})(X\triangleleft a)(c)_{(0)}

\displaystyle=a(X(c)_{(-1)})b(X(c)_{(-1)(0)})X(c)_{(0)(0)}

\displaystyle=(ab)(X(c)_{(-1)})X(c)_{(0)}

\displaystyle=(X\triangleleft ab)(c).

Moreover,

\mathscr{L}_{aX}(b)(c)=b(\delta((aX)(c)))=a(c_{(1)})b(\delta(X(c_{(2)})))=(a\mathscr{L}_{X}(b))(c)

and since
\delta
is a coderivation and
X
right
C
-colinear

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{X}(ab)(c)

\displaystyle=(ab)(\delta(X(c)))=a(\delta(X(c))_{(1)})b(\delta(X(c))_{(2)})

\displaystyle=a(\delta(X(c)_{(0)}))b(X(c)_{(1)})+a(X(c)_{(-1)})b(\delta(X(c)_{(0)}))

\displaystyle=a(\delta(X(c_{(1)})))b(c_{(2)})+b(\delta(a(X(c)_{(-1)})X(c)_{(0)}))

\displaystyle=(\mathscr{L}_{X}(a)b)(c)+b(\delta((X\triangleleft a)(c)))

\displaystyle=(\mathscr{L}_{X}(a)b)(c)+\mathscr{L}_{Xa}(b)(c).

To prove injectivity of
\mathscr{L}
, let
X\in\mathscr{X}^{1}
such that
\mathscr{L}_{X}(a)=0
for all
a\in A
. We have to show that
X(c)=0
for all
c\in C
. By the injectivity property of a first-order codifferential calculus, it is enough to show that
\delta(X(c)_{(0)})\otimes X(c)_{(1)}=0
(note that since
\delta
is a coderivation, injectivity of the map
w\mapsto w_{(-1)}\otimes\delta(w_{(0)})
is equivalent to injectivity of
w\mapsto\delta(w_{(0)})\otimes w_{(1)}
). Indeed, for all
a,b\in A
, we have by right
C
-colinearity of
X

a(\delta(X(c)_{(0)}))b(X(c)_{(1)})=a(\delta(X(c_{(1)})))b(c_{(2)})=(\mathscr{L}_{X}(a)b)(c)=0.

∎

7.
Codifferential calculi on quantized flag manifolds

In this section, we will study codifferential calculi on certain quotient module coalgebras of quantized universal enveloping algebras. As an application of the results in the previous section, we show that the antiholomorphic Heckenberger–Kolb calculi on the quantized projective spaces have classical dimension.

7.1.
Quantized flag manifolds

We start by fixing Lie theoretic notation. For more explanation of the below, we refer to introductory texts in Lie theory
[
18
,
26
]
. Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a complex semisimple Lie algebra of rank
r
and fix a Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
. We denote by
\Delta\subseteq\mathfrak{h}^{\ast}
the corresponding set of roots of
\mathfrak{g}
and let
E:=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\Delta
. We rescale the inner product of
E
induced by the Killing form in such a way that
(\alpha,\alpha)=2
for the shortest root. Choose a set of simple roots
\Pi:=\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r}\}
, and write
(a_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
for the Cartan matrix as well as
d_{i}:=\frac{(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})}{2}
. Furthermore, we consider the lattice of integral weights

P:=\{\lambda\in E\mid\forall 1\leq i\leq r\colon(\lambda,\alpha_{i}^{\vee})\in\mathbb{Z}\}

where
\alpha_{i}^{\vee}:=2\alpha_{i}/(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})
denotes the coroot of
\alpha_{i}
.

For
q\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{-1,0,1\}
, we can define the
quantized universal enveloping algebra

U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
as the
\mathbb{C}
-algebra with generators
E_{i},F_{i},K_{i}^{\pm 1},1\leq i\leq r
and relations

(32)

K_{i}K_{i}^{\pm 1}=K_{i}K_{i}^{\pm 1}=1,\quad K_{i}K_{j}=K_{j}K_{i}

(33)

K_{i}E_{j}K_{i}^{-1}=q_{i}^{a_{ij}}E_{j},\quad K_{i}F_{j}K_{i}^{-1}=q_{i}^{-a_{ij}}F_{j}

(34)

[E_{i},F_{j}]=\delta_{ij}\frac{1}{q_{i}-q_{i}^{-1}}(K_{i}-K_{i}^{-1})

(35)

\sum_{\ell=0}^{1-a_{ij}}(-1)^{\ell}\left[\begin{matrix}1-a_{ij}\\
\ell\end{matrix}\right]_{q_{i}}E_{i}^{1-a_{ij}-\ell}E_{j}E_{i}^{\ell}

(36)

\sum_{\ell=0}^{1-a_{ij}}(-1)^{\ell}\left[\begin{matrix}1-a_{ij}\\
\ell\end{matrix}\right]_{q_{i}}F_{i}^{1-a_{ij}-\ell}F_{j}F_{i}^{\ell}

Here we made use of
q
-integers. For an arbitrary complex number
q
such that
q\notin\{0,1,-1\}
and
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, one defines

[n]_{q}:=\frac{q^{n}-q^{-n}}{q-q^{-1}}

[n]_{q}!:=\prod_{i=1}^{n}[i]_{q}

and

\left[\begin{matrix}m\\
n\end{matrix}\right]_{q}:=\frac{[m]_{q}!}{[n]_{q}![m-n]_{q}!}.

The quantized universal enveloping algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is endowed with the structure of Hopf algebra
[
28
, Proposition 6.5]
by letting

(37)

\Delta(E_{i})=E_{i}\otimes K_{i}+1\otimes E_{i},\quad\Delta(F_{i})=F_{i}\otimes 1+K_{i}^{-1}\otimes F_{i},\quad\Delta(K_{i}^{\pm 1})=K_{i}^{\pm 1}\otimes K_{i}^{\pm 1}

(38)

\varepsilon(E_{i})=\varepsilon(F_{i})=0,\quad\varepsilon(K_{i}^{\pm})=1

(39)

S(E_{i})=-E_{i}K_{i}^{-1},\quad S(F_{i})=-K_{i}F_{i},\quad S(K_{i}^{\pm 1})=K_{i}^{\mp 1}.

We remark that by
[
28
, Proposition 6.6]
, the antipode of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is invertible.

For a fixed subset of simple roots
S\subseteq\Pi
, we consider the Levi subalgebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})
, which is the subalgebra of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
generated by the set
\{E_{i},F_{i},K_{j}^{\pm 1}\mid\alpha_{i}\in S,1\leq j\leq r\}
. The following enables us to use the tools developed in section
5
.

Proposition 7.1
.

The quantized universal enveloping algebra
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is faithfully flat as a left- and right
U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})
-module.

Proof.

From the definition of the coproduct and antipode on
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
it follows that
U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})
is a Hopf subalgebra. It also follows that
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is generated by group-like and skew primitive elements, thus by
[
37
, Corollary 5.1.14]
it follows that
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is pointed. In particular, the coradical
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})_{0}
is cocommutative and the assertion follows from the main result in
[
30
]
.
∎

For simplicity, we will from now on assume that
q
is not a root of unity. Let
\mathcal{C}
denote the full subcategory of the category of finite dimensional
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
-modules
M
that admit a decomposition

M=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in P}M[\lambda]

where
M[\lambda]:=\{m\in M\mid\forall 1\leq i\leq r\colon K_{i}m=q^{(\alpha_{i},\lambda)}m\}
denotes the weight space of the weight
\lambda
. Note that
\mathcal{C}
is a tensor subcategory of
{}_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})}\mathcal{M}
. Indeed, it is immediate that
\mathcal{C}
is closed under direct sums, and for tensor products, this follows directly from the way how the coproducts of the
K_{i}
’s are defined. The category
\mathcal{C}
is also closed under duals with respect to
S
by verifying that

M^{\ast}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in P}M^{\ast}[-\lambda].

Note that one gets the same decomposition if one considers the dual of
M
with respect to
S^{-1}
. In other words,
\mathcal{C}
is also closed under taking dual with respect to
S^{-1}
.

Definition 7.2
.

We define the
q
-deformed coordinate ring
\mathcal{O}_{q}(G)
as

\mathcal{O}_{q}(G):=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})_{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}.

In addition, for a subset
S\subseteq\Pi
we define the
quantized flag manifold

\mathcal{O}_{q}(G/L_{S}):={}^{U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})}\mathcal{O}_{q}(G)=\{b\in\mathcal{O}_{q}(G)\mid\forall X\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})\mid b_{(1)}\langle b_{(2)},X\rangle=\varepsilon(X)b\}.

Since the antipode of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
is invertible and
\mathcal{C}
is closed under taking duals with respect to
S
and
S^{-1}
, the antipode of
\mathcal{O}_{q}(G)
is bijective as well (see the discussion in Appendix
B
). Moreover,
\mathcal{O}_{q}(G/L_{S})
is a left coideal subalgebra which
O_{q}(G)
is faithfully flat over. The last assertion follows from Theorem
4.11
using that every
U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})
-module that arises as the restriction of a module in
\mathcal{C}
completely reducible. In this situation, we consider the quotient coalgebra

U_{q}^{c}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{l}_{S}):=U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})}\mathbb{C}_{\mathrm{triv}}.

By Lemma
4.10
we have
\mathcal{O}_{q}(G/L_{S})\cong U_{q}^{c}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{l}_{S})_{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
. Here, the latter even agrees with the full finitary dual
U_{q}^{c}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{l}_{S})^{\circ}
. Indeed, the quotient
U_{q}^{c}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{l}_{S})
is spanned by the images under the projection
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\twoheadrightarrow U_{q}^{c}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{l}_{S})
of monomials in
E_{i}
’s and
F_{i}
’s for
1\leq i\leq r
. If
v
is the image of any such monomial in
U_{q}^{c}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{l}_{S})
, then each
K_{i}
acts on
v
via some scalar of the form
q^{(\alpha_{i},\lambda)}
, where
\lambda\in P
. Thus, every finite dimensional
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
-quotient of
U_{q}^{c}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{l}_{S})
is already in
\mathcal{C}
.

Remark 7.3
.

Here we use
U_{q}^{c}(-)
in analogy with
\mathcal{O}_{q}(-)
on the quantized coordinate ring-side. The superscript
c
is used to avoid confusions with the quantized universal enveloping algebra, and to emphasize the coalgebra structure.

7.2.
Poincaré–Birkhoff–Witt basis for quotient coalgebras

We want to give an explicit basis for the quotient coalgebra
U_{q}^{c}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{l}_{S})
of a quantized flag manifold. First let us recall the PBW theorem for
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
. For details, we refer to
[
28
, Sec. 6.2]
. Let
W
be the Weyl group of
\mathfrak{g}
and denote by
w_{0}
its longest element. Recall that
W
is generated by involutions
s_{1},\dots,s_{r}
corresponding to the choice of simple roots
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r}
. Any choice of a reduced decomposition (this is, the number
n
is the smallest non-negative integer such that
w_{0}
admits a decomposition as below).

w_{0}=s_{i_{n}}\dots s_{i_{1}}

gives rise to elements
E_{\beta_{1}},\dots,E_{\beta_{n}},F_{\beta_{1}},\dots,F_{\beta_{n}}\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
called
root vectors
. In fact, the assignment
i\mapsto\beta_{i}
is a bijection between the set
\{1,\dots,n\}
and the positive roots of
\mathfrak{g}
. By taking certain monomials in the root vectors, we obtain a basis of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
. We adapt the formulation of
[
28
, Theorem 24, Chap. 6]
.

Theorem 7.4
.

The following elements form a vector space basis of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
:

F_{\beta_{1}}^{l_{1}}\dots F_{\beta_{n}}^{l_{n}}K_{1}^{m_{1}}\dots K_{r}^{m_{r}}E_{\beta_{1}}^{t_{1}}\dots E_{\beta_{n}}^{t_{n}}

where
l_{j},t_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
for
1\leq j\leq n
and
m_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}
for
1\leq j\leq r
.

In the sequel, we assume for the sake of simplicity that
S
is of the form
S\setminus\{\alpha_{i}\}
, where
\alpha_{i}
is a simple root which appears in any positive root with coefficient at most one in the corresponding decomposition into simple roots. In other words, we only consider irreducible (quantized) flag manifolds from now on. Following
[
20
]
, we write

\Delta_{S}^{+}:=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{Z}}S\cap\Delta^{+},\quad\overline{\Delta_{S}^{+}}:=\Delta^{+}\setminus\Delta_{S}^{+}.

In addition, we write
\overline{\Delta_{S}^{+}}=\{\beta_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\beta_{M}^{\prime}\}
, where
M=|\overline{\Delta_{S}^{+}}|
.

Proposition 7.5
.

The images of the elements

E_{\beta_{1}^{\prime}}^{l_{1}}\dots E_{\beta_{M}^{\prime}}^{l_{M}}F_{\beta_{1}^{\prime}}^{m_{1}}\dots F_{\beta_{M}^{\prime}}^{m_{M}},\quad l_{j},m_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

under the projection
U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\twoheadrightarrow U_{q}^{c}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{l}_{S})
form a basis of
U_{q}^{c}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{l}_{S})
.

Proof.

See
[
20
, Proposition 4]
.
∎

In the case of
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{r+1}
, there is a more explicit description for the root vectors. Recall that the standard
A_{r}
-root system
[
26
, p. 64]
is given by

\Delta:=\{\alpha_{ij}:=\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{r+1}\mid 1\leq i\neq j\leq r+1\}.

Here, the
\varepsilon_{i}
’s denote the standard basis vectors of
\mathbb{R}^{r+1}
. As a subset of simple roots, we choose
\Pi=\{\alpha_{i}:=\alpha_{i,i+1}\mid 1\leq i\leq r\}
. Corresponding to this choice of simple roots, the positive roots are given by the set
\Delta^{+}=\{\alpha_{ij}\mid 1\leq i<j\leq r+1\}
. Recall also, that the Weyl group of
\mathfrak{sl}_{r+1}
is given by the symmetric group on
\{1,\dots,r+1\}
, with its standard generators given by the transpositions

s_{i}:=(i,i+1),\quad 1\leq i\leq r

(written in cycle notation).

In the following, we write
[X,Y]_{c}:=XY-cYX
, where
c\in\mathbb{C}
for the twisted commutator of two elements
X,Y
of an arbitrary
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
A
.

Proposition 7.6
.

For
1\leq i<j\leq r+1
write

(40)

E_{ji}:=[\dots[[E_{j-1},E_{j-2}]_{q^{-1}},E_{j-3}]_{q^{-1}},\dots,E_{i}]_{q^{-1}}.

Then the
E_{ji}
’s are precisely the positive root vectors of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{r+1})
corresponding to the reduced decomposition of the longest element of the Weyl group

w_{0}=(s_{r}\dots s_{1})(s_{r}\dots s_{2})\dots(s_{r}s_{r-1})s_{r}.

Proof.

See
[
33
, Proposition A.3]
.
∎

7.3.
The Podleś cocalculus

For our first example of a codifferential calculus on a quantized flag manifold, we take
\mathfrak{g}:=\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
, and
S=\emptyset
. Thus, the Levi subalgebra is
U_{q}(\mathfrak{h}):=U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})=\langle K^{\pm 1}\rangle
. We write
U_{q}^{c}(S^{2}):=U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}\mathbb{C}_{\mathrm{triv}}
. Note that the root vectors for
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
are simply the generators
E
and
F
. Thus, we can give an explicit basis for
U_{q}^{c}(S^{2})
.

Corollary 7.7
.

The elements

[E^{m}F^{n}],\quad m,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

form a basis of
U_{q}^{c}(S^{2})
.

Proof.

This is a special case of Proposition
7.5
.
∎

Consider the following elements
e:=[E]
and
f:=[F]
, and let
T:=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{e,f\}
.

Lemma 7.8
.

The subspace
T
is a quantum tangent space, and moreover
T\in{}_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}\mathcal{M}^{0}
.

Proof.

Since
\varepsilon(e)=\varepsilon(f)=0
, we have
T\subseteq U_{q}^{c}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{l}_{S})^{+}
. In addition,
U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})T\subseteq T
, since

Ke=[KE]=q^{2}[EK]=q^{2}e

and similarly
Kf=q^{-2}f
. Lastly, it follows at once that
T\in{}_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}\mathcal{M}^{0}
and that
\Delta(T)\subseteq(T\oplus k[1])\otimes C
, since

\displaystyle\Delta(e)=[E]\otimes[K]+[1]\otimes[E]=e\otimes[1]+[1]\otimes e

and similarly
\Delta(f)=f\otimes[1]+[1]\otimes f
.
∎

From the above, and Proposition
5.5
it follows that
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{q}(S^{2}):=U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})}T
together with the codifferential
X\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})}t\mapsto Xt
is an equivariant first-order codifferential calculus. Our next goal is to compute its maximal prolongation.

Proposition 7.9
.

Recall the map

\check{\delta}\colon T\otimes T\rightarrow U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}U_{q}^{c}(S^{2})^{+},\quad[x]\otimes t\mapsto x_{(1)}\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}S(x_{(2)})t.

Then, in the notation of
5.9
, we have
\check{R}=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{e\otimes f-q^{2}f\otimes e\}
.

Proof.

Computing the map
\check{\delta}
on the basis elements of
T\otimes T
gives

\displaystyle\check{\delta}(e\otimes e)

\displaystyle=q^{-2}E\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}e-q^{-2}\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}[E^{2}]

\displaystyle\check{\delta}(e\otimes f)

\displaystyle=q^{2}E\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}f-q^{2}\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}[EF]

\displaystyle\check{\delta}(f\otimes e)

\displaystyle=F\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}e-1\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}[EF]

\displaystyle\check{\delta}(f\otimes f)

\displaystyle=F\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}f-1\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}[F^{2}].

A sufficient condition for an element
w\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}U_{q}^{c}(S^{2})^{+}
to lie in
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{q}(S^{2})
is that
(\varepsilon\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}\mathrm{id})(w)\in T
. The values of the basis elements of
T\otimes T
under the composition
(\varepsilon\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}\mathrm{id})\circ\check{\delta}
are

-q^{-2}[E^{2}],\quad-q^{2}[EF],\quad-[EF],\quad-[F^{2}].

Using Corollary
7.7
, we see that the only elements of
T\otimes T
, that can get mapped to
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{q}(S^{2})
are scalar multiples of
e\otimes f-q^{2}f\otimes e
, and indeed

\check{\delta}(e\otimes f-q^{2}f\otimes e)=q^{2}E\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}f-q^{2}F\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}e\in\mathscr{W}_{1}^{q}(S^{2}).

∎

The last step in computing the maximal prolongation of
\mathscr{W}_{1}^{q}(S^{2})
is to determine the quadratic coalgebra
\mathfrak{C}(T,\check{R})
. If we consider the dual vector space
V
of
T
equipped with dual basis
\{e^{\ast},f^{\ast}\}
, the orthogonal complement of
R
in
V\otimes V
with respect to the pairing

V\otimes V\times T\otimes T\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\quad(v\otimes w,s\otimes t)\mapsto v(s)w(t)

is
\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{e^{\ast}\otimes e^{\ast},f^{\ast}\otimes f^{\ast},e^{\ast}\otimes f^{\ast}+q^{-2}f^{\ast}\otimes e^{\ast}\}
. The dual quadratic algebra of
\mathfrak{C}(T,\check{R})
is therefore

\Lambda_{q^{-2}}^{\bullet}(V):=T(V)/\langle e^{\ast}\otimes e^{\ast},f^{\ast}\otimes f^{\ast},e^{\ast}\otimes f^{\ast}+q^{-2}f^{\ast}\otimes e^{\ast}\rangle.

Which has basis
1,e^{\ast},f^{\ast},e^{\ast}\wedge f^{\ast}
, which can be seen by applying Bergman’s diamond lemma
[
4
]
. With the help of Lemma
A.3
, we conclude that
[1],e,f,e\otimes f-q^{2}f\otimes e
forms a basis of
\mathfrak{C}(T,\check{R})
.
If we denote by
\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}
the one-dimensional
U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})
-module, where
K
acts via the scalar
q^{\lambda}
, and by
W(\lambda)
the induced module
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}\mathbb{C}_{\lambda}
, where we denote by
w_{\lambda}
, the vector
1\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}1
, and use the obvious identifications

U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}\mathbb{C}e\cong W(2),\quad U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}\mathbb{C}f\cong W(-2),\quad U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}\mathbb{C}1\cong W(0)

as well as the identification

W(0)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}\mathbb{C}(e\otimes f-q^{2}f\otimes e),\quad w_{0}\mapsto q^{-2}e\otimes f-f\otimes e

then the codifferential calculus explicitly looks as follows:

(41)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W(-2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\delta_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{F}}

where
\delta_{F}
is the map
\delta_{F}\colon W(\lambda)\rightarrow W(\lambda+2),w_{\lambda}\mapsto Fw_{\lambda+2}
, and
\delta_{E}\colon W(\lambda)\rightarrow W(\lambda-2)
is defined analogously. Note that (
41
) is exactly the complex given in the introduction of
[
22
]
.

7.4.
Antiholomorphic cocalculi on quantized projective spaces

We proceed by giving family of higher-rank examples. Let
r\geq 1
and
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{r+1}
. We choose the subset of simple roots
S=\{\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{r}\}
, such that the corresponding Levi subalgebra is
U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S}):=\langle K_{i}^{\pm 1},E_{j},F_{j}\colon 1\leq i\leq r,2\leq j\leq r\rangle
. Let us denote
U_{q}^{c}(\mathbb{C}P^{r}):=U_{q}^{c}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{l}_{S})
and
e_{i}:=[E_{i}E_{i-1}\dots E_{1}]\in U_{q}^{c}(\mathbb{C}P^{r})
for
1\leq i\leq r
. We consider the subspace of
U_{q}^{c}(\mathbb{C}P^{r})

T^{(0,1)}:=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\}.

In what follows, we will view modules over
U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})
as modules over
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{r})
, using the embedding
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{r})\rightarrow U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})
determined by

(42)

E_{i}\mapsto E_{i+1},\quad F_{i}\mapsto F_{i+1},\quad K_{i}\mapsto K_{i+1},\quad 1\leq i\leq r-1.

When dealing with weights, we will refer to weights of the corresponding representation of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{r})
. On that note, recall that the fundamental weights
\varpi_{1},\dots,\varpi_{r-1}
of
\mathfrak{sl}_{r}
are determined by the relations

(\alpha_{i}^{\vee},\varpi_{j})=\delta_{ij}.

Lemma 7.10
.

For
1\leq i\leq r
and
2\leq j\leq r
, we have

(1)

K_{j}e_{i}=\begin{cases}qe_{i},\quad j=i\\
q^{-1}e_{i},\quad j=i+1\\
e_{i},\quad\text{else}.\end{cases}

(2)

E_{j}e_{i}=\begin{cases}e_{i+1},\quad j=i+1\\
0,\quad\text{else}\end{cases}

(3)

F_{j}e_{i}=\begin{cases}e_{i-1},\quad j=i\\
0,\quad\text{else}\end{cases}

where
e_{0}:=e_{r+1}:=0
.

Proof.

(1)
: This follows immediately from the commutation relations

K_{i}E_{i}=q^{2}E_{i}K_{i},K_{i\pm 1}E_{i}=q^{-1}E_{i}K_{i\pm 1}

and
K_{j}E_{i}=E_{i}K_{j}
if
|i-j|\geq 2
. The latter follow since the Cartan matrix for
\mathfrak{sl}_{r+1}
is given by
a_{i}i=2,a_{i,i\pm 1}=-1
and
a_{ij}=0
for
|i-j|\geq 2
, and the fact that
(\alpha,\alpha)=2
for all roots of
\mathfrak{sl}_{r+1}
.

(2)
: Recall that for
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{r+1})
, the Serre relations among the
E_{i}^{\prime}s
are explicitly given by

E_{i\pm 1}^{2}E_{i}-[2]_{q}E_{i\pm 1}E_{i}E_{i\pm 1}+E_{i}E_{i\pm 1}=0

and

E_{i}E_{j}=E_{j}E_{i}

if
|i-j|\geq 2
. Thus, it is clear that
E_{i+1}e_{i}=e_{i+1}
for
1\leq i\leq r-1
and
E_{j}e_{i}=0
for
j>i+1
. For
j=i
, we get

\displaystyle E_{i}e_{i}

\displaystyle=[E_{i}^{2}E_{i-1}\dots E_{1}]=[[2]_{q}E_{i}E_{i-1}E_{i}\dots E_{1}]-[E_{i-1}E_{i}^{2}E_{i-2}\dots E_{1}]=0.

If
j=i-1
, then

\displaystyle E_{i-1}e_{i}

\displaystyle=[E_{i-1}E_{i}\dots E_{1}]=\frac{1}{[2]_{q}}\left([E_{i-1}^{2}E_{i}E_{i-2}\dots E_{1}]+[E_{i}E_{i-1}E_{i-2}\dots E_{1}]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{[2]_{q}}E_{i}E_{i-1}e_{i-1}

\displaystyle=0

and lastly, if
j<i-1
, then

E_{j}e_{i}=E_{i}\dots E_{j+2}E_{j}e_{j+1}=0

by the case
j=i-1
.

(3)
: Since
E_{i}
and
F_{j}
commute if
i\neq j
, it is clear that
F_{j}e_{i}=0
if
j>i
. For
j=i
, we compute using
(1)

\displaystyle F_{i}e_{i}

\displaystyle=[F_{i}E_{i}\dots E_{1}]=[E_{i}F_{i}E_{i-1}\dots E_{1}]-[[E_{i},F_{i}]E_{i-1}\dots E_{i}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{q-q^{-1}}(K_{i}-K_{i}^{-1})[E_{i-1}\dots E_{1}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{q-q^{-1}}\left(q^{-1}e_{i-1}+qe_{i-1}\right)=e_{i-1}.

Lastly, for
j<i
, we get

F_{j}e_{i}=E_{i}\dots E_{j+1}F_{j}e_{j}=E_{i}\dots E_{j+1}e_{j-1}=0

by
(2)
.
∎

In fact, the elements
e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\in U_{q}^{c}(\mathbb{C}P^{r})^{+}
form a linear independent set.

Lemma 7.11
.

For every
2\leq j\leq r+1
, the image of
E_{ji}
(see
7.6
) in
U_{q}^{c}(\mathbb{C}P^{r})
under the canonical projection is
e_{j-1}
. In particular the set
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\}
is linearly independent.

Proof.

Solely for the sake of this proof, we let
e_{0}:=[1]
. Fix
2\leq j\leq r+1
. We claim that

(43)

E_{j,j-i}e_{j-i-1}=e_{j-1}

for all
1\leq i<j
. Indeed, if
i=1
and
j\geq 3
, then

E_{j,j-1}e_{j-2}=E_{j-1}e_{j-2}=e_{j-1}

by Lemma
7.10
, and if
i=1
and
j=2
, then we see directly

E_{21}e_{0}=E_{1}[1]=e_{1}.

If
i\geq 1
and
i+1<j
, then by induction on
i

\displaystyle E_{j,j-i-1}e_{j-i-2}

\displaystyle=[E_{j,j-i},E_{j-i-1}]_{q^{-1}}e_{j-i-2}

\displaystyle=E_{j,j-1}E_{j-i-1}e_{j-i-2}-q^{-1}E_{j-i-1}E_{j,j-i}e_{j-i-2}

\displaystyle=E_{j,j-i}e_{j-i-1}=e_{j-1}.

Here, the last step follows by induction, the equality
E_{j-i-1}e_{j-i-2}=e_{j-i-1}
follows from the case
i=1
, since
E_{j-i-1}=E_{j-i,j-i-1}
, and
E_{j,j-i}e_{j-i-2}=0
, since
E_{j,j-i}
is a sum of monomials in
E_{j-i},\dots,E_{j-1}
by (
40
), which annihilate
e_{{j-i-2}}
. In the case
j-i-2=0
, the latter assertion follows directly from the relations of
U_{q}^{c}(\mathbb{C}P^{r})
and for
j-i-2>0
this is a consequence of Lemma
7.10
.

We conclude
[E_{j,1}]=E_{j,1}[1]=e_{j-1}
for all
2\leq j\leq r+1
. The last assertion follows from Proposition
7.5
.
∎

From Lemma
7.10
and
7.11
it follows that
T^{(0,1)}
is an irreducible
U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})
-submodule of
U_{q}^{c}(\mathbb{C}P^{r})
. If we restrict the action to
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{r})
along the map (
42
), then
T^{(0,1)}\cong V_{\varpi_{r-1}}
, the irreducible
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{r})
-module of highest weight
\varpi_{r-1}
.

Proposition 7.12
.

The subspace
T^{(0,1)}
is a quantum tangent space such that
T^{(0,1)}\in{}_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})}\mathcal{M}^{0}
.

Proof.

We have already seen that
U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})T^{(0,1)}\subseteq T^{(0,1)}
(Lemma
7.10
). To see the coproduct property, we compute the coproducts

\Delta(E_{i}\dots E_{1})

for
1\leq i\leq r
. Denote by
U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})
the subalgebra generated by
K_{j}^{\pm 1}
for
1\leq j\leq r
. Then in the quotient
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{r+1})\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{h})}\mathbb{C}_{\mathrm{triv}}
, the coproducts evaluate to

\sum_{j=0}^{i}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathrm{Sh}(j,i-j)}\lambda(j,\sigma)[E_{\sigma(1)}\dots E_{\sigma(j)}]\otimes[E_{\sigma(j+1)}\dots E_{\sigma(i)}]

here
\mathrm{Sh}(j,i-j)
denotes the set of
(j,i-j)
-shuffels, that is, permutations
\sigma\in S_{i}
such that
\sigma(1)<\dots<\sigma(j)
and
\sigma(j+1)<\dots<\sigma(i)
. If we pass further to the quotient
U_{q}^{c}(\mathbb{C}P^{r})
then the only summands that survive are
[E_{i}\dots E_{1}]\otimes[1]
and
[1]\otimes[E_{i}\dots E_{1}]
, and one can check that the corresponding coefficients
\lambda(j,\sigma)
are both equal to one. Thus,

\Delta(e_{i})=e_{i}\otimes[1]+[1]\otimes e_{i}

for all
1\leq i\leq r
and the assertion follows immediately.
∎

Proposition 7.13
.

In the notation of Proposition
5.9
the subset
\check{R}\subseteq T^{(0,1)}\otimes T^{(0,1)}
is given by

\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{e_{i}\otimes e_{j}-qe_{j}\otimes e_{i}\mid 1\leq i<j\leq r\}.

Proof.

The crucial observation is that
\check{\delta}
is
U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})
-linear, and therefore in particular
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{r})
-linear, if we restrict the action along the embedding
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{r})\rightarrow U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})
described above. Note that
T^{(0,1)}
is an irreducible
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{r})
-module of weight
\varpi_{r-1}
. Thus, the tensor product with itself decomposes as in
[
35
, p. 300]
(note that the decomposition of tensor products of finite dimensional type one representation of
U_{q}(g)
into irreducible components is the same as for the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, as remarked in
[
28
, Sec. 7.2]
):

T^{(0,1)}\otimes T^{(0,1)}\cong V_{2\varpi_{r}}\oplus V_{\varpi_{r-2}}.

Here
V_{\lambda}
denotes the irreducible
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{r})
-module of highest weight
\lambda
. The irreducible components are spanned by their (up to scalar multiples) unique lowest weight vectors. More precisely, we have

V_{2\varpi_{r-1}}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})(e_{1}\otimes e_{1}),\quad V_{\varpi_{r-2}}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})(e_{1}\otimes e_{2}-qe_{2}\otimes e_{1}).

Indeed, it is clear that
e_{1}\otimes e_{1}
is a lowest weight vector, and for
e_{1}\otimes e_{2}-qe_{2}\otimes e_{1}
it is immediate that it is annihilated by
F_{j}
for
j\geq 3
and for
F_{2}
, we first compute

\displaystyle F_{2}e_{2}

\displaystyle=[F_{2}E_{2}E_{1}]=\underbrace{[E_{2}F_{2}E_{1}]}_{=0}-[[E_{2},F_{2}]E_{1}]=\frac{1}{q-q^{-1}}\left([(K_{2}^{-1}-K_{2})E_{1}]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q-q^{-1}}\left(q[E_{1}]-q^{-1}[E_{1}]\right)

\displaystyle=e_{1}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle F_{2}\cdot(e_{1}\otimes e_{2}-qe_{2}\otimes e_{1})

\displaystyle=K_{2}^{-1}e_{1}\otimes e_{1}-qe_{1}\otimes e_{1}=qe_{1}\otimes e_{1}-qe_{1}\otimes e_{1}=0.

Next, we compute
\check{\delta}(e_{1}\otimes e_{2}-qe_{2}\otimes e_{1})
. Using that

(\mathrm{id}\otimes S)(\Delta(E_{1}))=E_{1}\otimes K_{1}^{-1}-1\otimes E_{1}K_{1}^{-1}

we find that

\displaystyle\check{\delta}(e_{1}\otimes e_{2})

\displaystyle=E_{1}\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})}K_{1}^{-1}e_{2}-1\otimes E_{1}K_{1}^{-1}e_{2}

\displaystyle=q^{-1}E_{1}\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})}e_{2}-q^{-1}\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})}[E_{1}E_{2}E_{1}]

and using that

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}\otimes S)(\Delta(E_{2}E_{1}))=E_{2}E_{1}\otimes K_{1}^{-1}K_{2}^{-1}-E_{2}\otimes E_{1}K_{1}^{-1}K_{2}^{-1}+E_{1}\otimes K_{1}^{-1}E_{2}K_{2}^{-1}

\displaystyle+1\otimes E_{1}K_{1}^{-1}E_{2}K_{2}^{-1}

as well as

[E_{2}E_{1}^{2}]=[[2]_{q}E_{1}E_{2}E_{1}-E_{1}^{2}E_{2}]=[2]_{q}[E_{1}E_{2}E_{1}]

we see that

\displaystyle\check{\delta}(e_{2}\otimes e_{1})

\displaystyle=\eta-E_{2}\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})}[E_{1}K_{1}^{-1}K_{2}^{-1}E_{1}]+1\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})}[E_{1}K_{1}^{-1}E_{2}K_{2}^{-1}E_{1}]

\displaystyle=\eta-q^{-1}\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})}[E_{2}E_{1}^{2}]+1\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})}[E_{1}E_{2}E_{1}]

\displaystyle=\eta+(1-q^{-1}[2]_{q})\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})}[E_{1}E_{2}E_{1}]

\displaystyle=\eta-q^{-2}\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})}[E_{1}E_{2}E_{1}].

Where
\eta\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{r+1})\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})}T^{(0,1)}
. Combining these two computations immediately gives

\check{\delta}(e_{1}\otimes e_{2}-qe_{1}\otimes e_{2})\in U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{r+1})\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})}T^{(0,1)}.

We claim that on the other hand
\check{\delta}(e_{1}\otimes e_{1})\notin U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{r+1})\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})}T^{(0,1)}
. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle(\varepsilon\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})}\mathrm{id})(\check{\delta}(e_{1}\otimes e_{1}))

\displaystyle=S(E_{1})e_{1}=-[E_{1}K_{1}^{-1}E_{1}]=-q^{-2}[E_{1}^{2}]\notin T^{(0,1)}

here the conclusion was made using the description of the basis of
U_{q}^{c}(\mathbb{C}P^{r})
from Proposition
7.5
and Proposition
7.6
.

By
U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})
-linearity of
\check{\delta}
, and since
T^{(0,1)}\otimes T^{(0,1)}
only has two non-isomorphic irreducible components we must have
\check{R}=U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})(e_{1}\otimes e_{2}-qe_{2}\otimes e_{1})
. Because

E_{i+1}\cdot(e_{i}\otimes e_{j}-qe_{j}\otimes e_{i})=e_{i+1}\otimes e_{j}-qe_{j}\otimes e_{i+1}

for
1\leq i<j-1
and

E_{j+1}\cdot(e_{i}\otimes e_{j}-qe_{j}\otimes e_{i})=e_{i}\otimes e_{j+1}-qe_{j+1}\otimes e_{i}

for
1\leq i\leq j\leq r-1
, we find that

\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\{e_{i}\otimes e_{j}-qe_{j}\otimes e_{i}\mid 1\leq i<j\leq r\}\subseteq\check{R}.

Finally, since
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V_{\varpi_{r-2}}=\binom{r}{2}
, and the set on the left-hand side is linearly independent, the assertion follows.
∎

Let us consider the first-order covariant differential calculus on
\mathcal{O}_{q}(\mathbb{C}P^{r})
induced by

\mathscr{W}_{(0,1)}^{q}(\mathbb{C}P^{r}):=U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{r+1})\otimes_{U_{q}(\mathfrak{l}_{S})}T^{(0,1)}.

We may write this induced first-order differential calculus as

\Omega_{q}^{(0,1)}(\mathbb{C}P^{r}):=\mathcal{O}_{q}(SL_{r+1})\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{O_{q}(\mathbb{C}P^{r})}}\hskip 1.0ptV^{(0,1)}

with
V^{(0,1)}=\mathcal{O}_{q}(\mathbb{C}P^{r})^{+}/I^{(0,1)}
, and
I^{(0,1)}
the orthogonal complement of
T^{(0,1)}
with respect to the non-degenerate pairing

\mathcal{O}_{q}(\mathbb{C}P^{r})^{+}\times U_{q}^{c}(\mathbb{C}P^{r})^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}.

By
[
20
, Section 7 Theorem 2]
, this first-order differential calculus is indeed isomorphic to the antiholomorphic part of the first-order Heckenberger–Kolb calculus on
\mathcal{O}_{q}(\mathbb{C}P^{r})
. As a consequence of Proposition
7.13
we obtain a new proof that the maximal prolongation of
\Omega_{q}^{(0,1)}(\mathbb{C}P^{r})
has classical dimension.

Corollary 7.14
.

The maximal prolongation of
\Omega_{q}^{(0,1)}(\mathbb{C}P^{r})
is isomorphic to

\mathcal{O}_{q}(SL_{r+1})\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{\mathcal{O}_{q}(\mathbb{C}P^{r})}}\hskip 1.0ptV^{(0,\bullet)}

where
V^{(0,\bullet)}:=T(V^{(0,1)})/\langle e^{i}\otimes e^{j}+q^{-1}e^{j}\otimes e^{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq j\leq r\rangle
, and
e^{1},\dots,e^{r}
is the dual basis of
e_{1},\dots,e_{r}
with respect to the non-degenerate pairing
V^{(0,1)}\times T^{(0,1)}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
. If we denote by
\wedge
the product of
V^{(0,\bullet)}
then the degree
n
part for each
n\geq 0
has basis

\{e^{i_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge e^{i_{n}}\mid 1\leq i_{1}<\dots<i_{n}\leq r\}.

In particular,

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Omega_{q}^{(0,n)}(\mathbb{C}P^{r}):=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V^{(0,n)}=\binom{r}{n}.

Thus, the dimension of
\Omega_{q}^{(0,\bullet)}(\mathbb{C}P^{r})
is classical.

Proof.

By Corollary
6.10
, the maximal prolongation of
\Omega_{q}^{(0,1)}(\mathbb{C}P^{r})
can be written as

\mathcal{O}_{q}(SL_{r+1})\hskip 1.0pt\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}_{\pi_{\mathcal{O}_{q}(\mathbb{C}P^{r})}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathfrak{A}(V^{(0,1)},\check{R}^{\perp}).

The orthogonal complement of
\check{R}
given in Proposition
7.13
is computed to be

\{e^{i}\otimes e^{j}+q^{-1}e^{j}\otimes e^{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq j\leq r\}.

Thus,
\mathfrak{A}(V^{(0,1)},\check{R}^{\perp})=V^{(0,\bullet)}
. Since the relations of
V^{(0,\bullet)}
are given by anti-commutation up to the scalar
q^{-1}
, the statement about the basis of
V^{(0,\bullet)}
follows from a standard application of Bergman’s diamond lemma
[
4
]
.
∎

Appendix A
Quadratic algebras and coalgebras

We start by defining quadratic algebras and coalgebras. On that note recall that the tensor algebra
T(V)
of a vector space
V
admits a coalgebra structure given by deconcatenation of tensors. Observe that this is a special case of the cotensor coalgebra
2.5
where we take
C=k
. We denote this coalgebra by
T^{c}(V)
.

Definition A.1
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional vector space, and
R\subseteq V\otimes V
a subspace.

(1)

The
quadratic algebra
determined by
V
and
R
is

\mathfrak{A}(V,R):=T(V)/\langle R\rangle.

(2)

The
quadratic coalgebra
determined by
V
and
R
is the graded subcoalgebra
\mathfrak{C}(V,R)
of
T^{c}(V)
with homogeneous components given by

\mathfrak{C}^{(0)}(V,R)=k1,\quad\mathfrak{C}^{(1)}(V,R)=V,\quad\mathfrak{C}^{(n)}(V,R)=\bigcap_{i+j+2=n}V^{\otimes i}\otimes R\otimes V^{\otimes j},\ (n\geq 2).

Note that also quadratic algebras are graded. Explicitly, the homogeneous components of a quadratic algebra
\mathfrak{A}(C,R)
are given by

\mathfrak{A}^{(0)}(C,R)=k1,\quad\mathfrak{A}^{(1)}(C,R)=V,\quad\mathfrak{A}^{(n)}=V^{\otimes n}/\left(\sum_{i+j+2}V^{\otimes i}\otimes R\otimes V^{\otimes j}\right).

Quadratic algebras and coalgebras are dual to each other, as made precise by the following.

Definition A.2
.

The quadratic dual algebra of a quadratic coalgebra
\mathfrak{C}(V,R)
is the quadratic algebra
\mathfrak{A}(V^{\ast},R^{\perp})
, where
V^{\ast}
is the dual space of
V
and
R^{\perp}
is the orthogonal complement of
R
with respect to the pairing

(V^{\ast})^{\otimes 2}\times V^{\otimes 2}\rightarrow k,\quad(v^{\ast}\otimes w^{\ast},v,w)=v^{\ast}(v)w^{\ast}(w).

The dual quadratic algebra is chosen in such a way, that one obtains an induced non-degenerate algebra-coalgebra pairing

\langle-,-\rangle\colon\mathfrak{A}(V^{\ast},R^{\perp})\times\mathfrak{C}(V,R)\rightarrow k.

Here, algebra-coalgebra pairing means that

\langle ab,c\rangle=\langle a,c_{(1)}\rangle\langle b,c_{(2)}\rangle,\quad\langle 1,c\rangle=\varepsilon(c)

for all
a,b\in\mathfrak{A}(V^{\ast},R^{\perp})
and
c\in\mathfrak{C}(V,R)
.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}(V,R)
be a quadratic coalgebra and let
\mathfrak{A}(V^{\ast},R^{\perp})
be its quadratic dual algebra. Then for each
n\geq 0
, the pairing

\mathfrak{A}^{(n)}(V^{\ast},R^{\perp})\times\mathfrak{C}^{(n)}(V,R)\rightarrow k,\quad\langle[v_{1}^{\ast}\otimes\dots\otimes v_{n}^{\ast}],v_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes v_{n}\rangle=v_{1}^{\ast}(v_{1})\dots v_{n}^{\ast}(v_{n})

is well-defined and non-degenerate. In particular, the extension of those pairings to a pairing between
\mathfrak{A}(V^{\ast},R^{\perp})
and
\mathfrak{C}(V,R)
is a non-degenerate algebra-coalgebra pairing, and moreover
\dim_{k}\mathfrak{A}^{(n)}(V^{\ast},R^{\perp})=\dim_{k}\mathfrak{C}^{(n)}(V,R)
for all
n\geq 0
.

Proof.

Non-degeneracy is clear for
n=0,1,2
. For
n\geq 3
, this directly follows by observing that the orthogonal complement of
\mathfrak{C}^{(n)}(V,R)
with respect to the pairing
(V^{\ast})^{\otimes n}\times V^{\otimes n}\rightarrow k
is precisely

\tilde{R}:=\sum_{i+j+2=n}(V^{\ast})^{\otimes i}\otimes R^{\perp}\otimes(V^{\ast})^{\otimes j}.

The inclusion
\tilde{R}\subseteq\mathfrak{C}^{(n)}(V,R)^{\perp}
is immediate, and the reverse inclusion follows by showing that
\tilde{R}^{\perp}\subseteq\mathfrak{C}^{(n)}(V,R)
. Indeed, since
V
is finite dimensional, and the pairing between
(V^{\ast})^{\otimes n}
and
V^{\otimes n}
is non-degenerate, the last inclusion implies
\mathfrak{C}^{(n)}(V,R)^{\perp}\subseteq\tilde{R}^{\perp\perp}=\tilde{R}
.
∎

Appendix B
Finitary duals

For convenience, we collect some background material on finitary duals of modules over
k
-algebras. Fix an arbitrary
k
-algebra
U
. Recall that if
W
is a (left)
U
-module, then its linear dual space
W^{\ast}
is canonically a right
U
-module with action given by

(f\triangleleft u)(w):=f(u\triangleright w).

We are interested in a universal submodule of
W^{\ast}
, given by functionals with a certain finiteness condition.

Proposition B.1
.

Let
W
be a
U
-module. For a linear map
f:W\rightarrow k
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

There exists a
U
-submodule
K
of
W
with
\dim_{k}(W/K)<\infty
and
f|_{K}=0
.

(ii)

The right
U
-submodule
fU
of
W^{\ast}
generated by
f
is finite dimensional.

Proof.

(i)\implies(ii)
: Let
K
be a submodule of
W
with
f|_{K}=0
such that
\dim_{k}(W/K)<\infty
. It follows immediately from the definition of the right
U
-module structure on
W^{\ast}
that also every
g\in fU
vanishes on
K
. Thus, the submodule
fU
can be identified with a subspace of
(W/K)^{\ast}
, which is finite dimensional by assumption.

(ii)\implies(i)
: Suppose
fU
is finite dimensional. Let

K:=\{w\in W\mid\forall g\in fU:g(w)=0\}

and note that this is a
U
submodule of
W
. By choosing a basis
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
of
fU
, it follows that

K=\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\ker(f_{i}).

This description of
K
gives us an injective map

W/K=W/\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\ker(f_{i})\rightarrow\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}W/\ker(f_{i})

showing that
W/K
is finite dimensional.
∎

Definition B.2
.

We call the set
W^{\circ}
consisting of all
f\in W^{\ast}
that satisfy either condition
(i)
or condition
(ii)
of
B.1
the
finitary dual
of
W
.

From condition
(ii)
of
B.1
it follows that
W^{\circ}
is a right
U
-submodule of
W^{\ast}
.

If we consider
W=U
with
U
-module structure given by left multiplication, then there is a third characterization of
U^{\circ}
.

Definition B.3
.

Let
M
be a
U
-module,
m\in M
and
\varphi\in M^{\ast}
. We define

c_{\varphi,m}^{M}\colon U\rightarrow k,\quad u\mapsto\varphi(u\triangleright m).

The functional
c_{f,m}^{M}
is called a
matrix coefficient
of
M
.

Proposition B.4
.

For a functional
f\in U^{\ast}
the following are equivalent:

(i)

f\in U^{\circ}
.

(ii)

The exists a finite dimensional
U
-module
M
, an element
m\in M
and
\varphi\in M^{\ast}
with
f=c_{\varphi,m}^{M}
.

Proof.

(i)\implies(ii)
: Let
f\in U^{\circ}
. Choose a
U
-submodule
K
of
U
with
\dim_{k}(U/K)<\infty
. Then
f
is equal to the matrix coefficient
c_{\overline{f},1+K}^{U/K}
, where
\overline{f}(u+K)=f(u)
.

(ii)\implies(i)
: If
f=c_{\varphi,m}^{M}
is a matrix coefficient of some finite dimensional
U
-module
M
, then for any
u\in U
, we have
fu=c_{\varphi u,m}^{M}
. Thus,
fU
is finite dimensional.
∎

As it is well-known (see for instance
[
37
, Section 2.5]
), the finitary dual of an algebra is a coalgebra.

Proposition B.5
.

Let
c_{\varphi,m}^{M}
be a matrix coefficient of a finite dimensional
U
-module
M
and let
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}
be a basis of
M
with dual basis
\{e^{1},\dots,e^{n}\}
. Then for all
u,v\in U

c_{\varphi,m}^{M}(uv)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{\varphi,e_{i}}^{M}(u)c_{e^{i},m}^{M}(v).

In particular, the dual map of the multiplication
\mu\colon U\otimes U\rightarrow U
, restricts to a map

\Delta^{\circ}\colon U^{\circ}\rightarrow U^{\circ}\otimes U^{\circ}

which endows
U^{\circ}
with the structure of a coalgebra, where the counit is given by
f\mapsto f(1)
.

If
U
is a Hopf algebra, then we can work out the addition and multiplication of two elements of
U^{\ast}
in terms of matrix coefficients. It turns out that

c_{\varphi,m}^{M}\cdot c_{\psi,n}^{N}=c_{\varphi\otimes\psi,m\otimes n}^{M\otimes N},\quad c_{\varphi,m}^{M}+c_{\psi,n}^{N}=c_{\varphi+\psi,m+n}^{M\oplus N}.

Consequently, the finitary dual of
U
is a bialgebra. Moreover, the dual map of the antipode of
U
restricts to
U^{\circ}
, and turns the latter into a Hopf algebra.

It is occasionally useful to only consider matrix coefficients of modules that belong to a tensor subcategory
\mathcal{C}\subseteq{}_{U}\mathcal{M}
(see Section
4.3
).

Definition B.6
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a tensor subcategory of
{}_{U}\mathcal{M}
. We let
U_{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
be the subset of
U^{\circ}
consisting of all matrix coefficients
c_{\varphi,m}^{M}
, with
M\in\mathcal{C}
.

The subspace
U_{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
is in fact a Hopf subalgebra of
U^{\circ}
. If the antipode of
U
has an inverse
S^{-1}
, then the dual of a left
U
-module
M
is also a left
U
-module with respect to

(x\triangleright f)(m)=f(S^{-1}(x)\triangleright m).

From this observation, it follows that the antipode of
U^{\circ}
is invertible if the antipode of
U
is. Moreover, if we consider
U_{\mathcal{C}}^{\circ}
for a tensor category
\mathcal{C}
, then it admits an invertible antipode if
\mathcal{C}
is closed under taking duals with respect to the action given by
S^{-1}
.

Appendix C
Adjoint equivalences

Here we collect some results about adjunctions and categorical equivalences. If
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{E}
are categories,
F,F^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D},G,G^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
functors, and
\varphi\colon F\rightarrow F^{\prime},\psi\colon G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
natural transformations, we denote by
G\varphi\colon GF\rightarrow GF^{\prime}
the natural transformation with components
G(\varphi_{c})\colon GFc\rightarrow GF^{\prime}c
, and by
\psi F\colon GF\rightarrow G^{\prime}F
the natural transformation with components
\varphi_{Fc}\colon GFc\rightarrow G^{\prime}Fc
.

Definition C.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
be categories.

(1)

An
adjunction
between
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
consists of functors
F\colon\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D},U\rightarrow\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
and natural transformations
\eta\colon\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow UF,\varepsilon\colon FU\rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}}
called
unit
and
counit
respectively such That

\varepsilon F\circ F\eta=\mathrm{id}_{F},\quad U\varepsilon\circ\eta U=\mathrm{id}_{U}.

In this case we say that
F
is
left adjoint
to
U
, respectively
U
is
right adjoint
to
F
and write
F\dashv U
.

(2)

We call an adjunction as above an
adjoint equivalence
if the unit
\eta\colon\mathrm{id}\rightarrow UF
and counit
\varepsilon\colon FU\rightarrow\mathrm{id}
are isomorphisms.

Theorem C.2
.

Let
F,U
be a pair of adjoint functors, with unit
\eta\colon\mathrm{id}\rightarrow UF
and counit
\varepsilon\colon FU\rightarrow\mathrm{id}
. If either
U
or
F
is an equivalence, then
\eta
and
\varepsilon
are isomorphisms. In other words the adjunction is an adjoint equivalence.

Proof.

Assume first that
U
is an equivalence. Then by
[
29
, IV.4 Theorem 1]
,
U
is part of an adjoint equivalence
(F^{\prime},U,\eta^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})
. Furthermore, by
[
29
, IV.1 Corollary 1]
there is a natural isomorphism
\phi\colon F\rightarrow F^{\prime}
such that
\eta^{\prime}=U\phi\circ\eta
. Thus, the natural transformation
\eta
is an isomorphism. By the triangle identity we have
\varepsilon F\circ\eta=\mathrm{id}
. Thus, since
\eta
is an isomorphism and
F
is an equivalence of categories, also
\varepsilon
is an isomorphism.

If
F
is an equivalence, then we can reduce to the first case by viewing
U
and
F
as functors between
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{op}}
.
∎

Appendix D
Quotients of Hopf algebras induced by quantum homogeneous spaces

In the following we let
A
be a Hopf algebra,
B\subseteq A
a left coideal subalgebra and
\pi_{B}\colon A\rightarrow A/B^{+}A
the canonical projections. If one wants the quotient
\pi_{B}(A)
to be a Hopf algebra, then there are different assumptions on
B
one can make. While in the literature it is often assumed that
AB^{+}=B^{+}A
(for instance in
[
33
, Section 4.1]
), in Section
4.1
we only assume
AB^{+}\subseteq B^{+}A
, which is a priory weaker. In the context of a quantum homogeneous space, they are however equivalent, as the following lemma shows.

Lemma D.1
.

Suppose that the antipode of
A
is bijective. Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

AB^{+}\subseteq B^{+}A
.

(2)

\pi_{B}(A)
is a quotient bialgebra.

(3)

\pi_{B}(A)
is a quotient Hopf algebra.

If
A
is faithfully flat as a right
B
-module, then the above are equivalent to

(4)

AB^{+}=B^{+}A
.

Proof.

The assertion that
\pi_{B}(A)
is a quotient bialgebra is equivalent to the assertion that
B^{+}A
is a two-sided ideal, which is intern equivalent to
AB^{+}\subseteq B^{+}A
. This shows the equivalence of
(1)
and
(2)
. If
\pi_{B}(A)
is a Hopf algebra quotient, then it is in particular a bialgebra quotient. If
AB^{+}\subseteq B^{+}A
, then by
[
31
, Lemma 1.4]

S(B^{+}A)=AB^{+}\subseteq AB^{+}A=B^{+}A.

Thus, the antipode of
A
descends to
\pi_{B}(A)
, and assertions
(1)
,
(2)
and
(3)
are all equivalent. Clearly, assertion
(4)
implies
(1)
. Now suppose that
A
is faithfully flat as a right
B
-module. To show the last implication, first note that for all
a\in A
and
b\in B

\displaystyle(S^{-1}(a_{(2)})ba_{(1)})_{(1)}\otimes\pi_{B}((S^{-1}(a_{(2)}ba_{(1)}))_{(2)})

\displaystyle\phantom{===}=S^{-1}(a_{(4)})b_{(1)}a_{(1)}\otimes\pi_{B}(S^{-1}(a_{(3)})b_{(2)}a_{(2)})

\displaystyle\phantom{===}=S^{-1}(a_{(4)})b_{(1)}a_{(1)}\otimes\pi_{B}(S^{-1}(a_{(3)}))\pi_{B}(b_{(2)})\pi_{B}(a_{(2)})

\displaystyle\phantom{===}=S^{-1}(a_{(4)})ba_{(1)}\otimes\pi_{B}(S^{-1}(a_{(3)})a_{(2)})

\displaystyle\phantom{===}=S^{-1}(a_{(2)})ba_{(1)}\otimes\pi_{B}(1).

This implies that
S^{-1}(a_{(2)})ba_{(1)}\in B
, using Corollary
4.2
. In particular, if
b\in B^{+}
, then

ba=a_{(3)}S^{-1}(a_{(2)})ba_{(1)}\in AB^{+}.

∎
